Numerical Modelling Implementation Plan — Cold Storage Digital Twin

The scope should now be strictly numerical modelling. Remove ML, PINNs, CNN, LSTM, hybrid modelling, and all AI components from the implementation plan.

The objective is to develop a 3-D transient numerical model of a cold-storage room in which air injection, leakage, heat transfer, moisture transport, pressure variation, and airflow are modelled. The dashboard should allow the user to specify different conditions at different locations and observe the complete thermodynamic/psychrometric state of every computational point as a function of time.

1. Overall Objective

Develop a transient 3-D numerical model capable of predicting:

Temperature \(T\)
Absolute pressure \(P\)
Gauge pressure \(P_g\)
Air velocity components \(u,v,w\)
Velocity magnitude \(V\)
Moisture/humidity ratio \(\omega\)
Relative humidity \(RH\)
Vapour partial pressure \(p_v\)
Dry-air partial pressure \(p_{da}\)
Saturation pressure \(p_{ws}\)
Dew-point temperature \(T_{dp}\)
Wet-bulb temperature \(T_{wb}\)
Specific humidity \(q\)
Moist-air density \(\rho\)
Dry-air density \(\rho_{da}\)
Specific volume \(v\)
Moist-air enthalpy \(h\)
Sensible heat
Latent heat
Total energy
Condensation rate
Condensed-water mass
Heat-transfer rate
Moisture-transfer rate
Mass-flow rate
Volumetric-flow rate

at every computational cell/node and every simulation time step.

The model should additionally support:

Injection or leakage of air at arbitrary locations with independently specified temperature, humidity, pressure, flow rate/volume, velocity, heat input and moisture content.

The dashboard should then display how these disturbances propagate through the cold-storage domain with time.

2. Conceptual Numerical Model

The cold storage is represented as a 3-D computational domain:

$$ \Omega = [0,L_x]\times[0,L_y]\times[0,L_z] $$

It is divided into computational cells.

Each cell contains a complete state vector:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf{Q}_{i,j,k}^{n} = [T,P,u,v,w,\omega]_{i,j,k}^{n} } $$

where:

\(i,j,k\) = spatial location
\(n\) = time step
\(T\) = temperature
\(P\) = pressure
\(u,v,w\) = velocity components
\(\omega\) = humidity ratio

All other psychrometric parameters are calculated from this state.

Therefore, conceptually:

$$ \boxed{ (T,P,\mathbf{u},\omega) \rightarrow \text{Complete psychrometric state} } $$

for every cell.

3. Proposed Numerical Workflow
                 COLD STORAGE INPUT
                        │
                        ▼
              ┌───────────────────┐
              │ Geometry & Mesh   │
              └─────────┬─────────┘
                        │
                        ▼
              ┌───────────────────┐
              │ Initial Conditions│
              │ T, P, RH, ω, etc. │
              └─────────┬─────────┘
                        │
          ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
          ▼             ▼             ▼
     Air Injection   Leakage       Wall Heat
       Sources       Sources        Transfer
          │             │             │
          └─────────────┼─────────────┘
                        ▼
              ┌───────────────────┐
              │ Pressure / Airflow│
              │     Solver        │
              └─────────┬─────────┘
                        │
                        ▼
              ┌───────────────────┐
              │ Moisture Transport│
              │     Solver        │
              └─────────┬─────────┘
                        │
                        ▼
              ┌───────────────────┐
              │ Temperature /     │
              │ Energy Solver     │
              └─────────┬─────────┘
                        │
                        ▼
              ┌───────────────────┐
              │ Condensation /    │
              │ Evaporation       │
              └─────────┬─────────┘
                        │
                        ▼
              ┌───────────────────┐
              │ Psychrometric     │
              │ Property Engine   │
              └─────────┬─────────┘
                        │
                        ▼
              ┌───────────────────┐
              │ Update Every Cell │
              │ at t + Δt         │
              └─────────┬─────────┘
                        │
                        ▼
              ┌───────────────────┐
              │ Conservation &    │
              │ Stability Checks  │
              └─────────┬─────────┘
                        │
                        ▼
                 ┌─────────────┐
                 │  Dashboard  │
                 └─────────────┘
4. Module 1 — Geometry and Computational Mesh
4.1 Cold-storage geometry

Inputs:

Length \(L_x\)
Width \(L_y\)
Height \(L_z\)
Wall thickness
Floor thickness
Ceiling thickness
Door dimensions
Inlet dimensions
Outlet dimensions
Equipment locations
Product/storage locations

Initially, use a Cartesian structured mesh.

For example:

$$ N_x\times N_y\times N_z $$

with:

$$ \Delta x=\frac{L_x}{N_x} $$ $$ \Delta y=\frac{L_y}{N_y} $$ $$ \Delta z=\frac{L_z}{N_z} $$

Later, irregular geometry can be introduced.

5. Module 2 — Initial Conditions

The entire cold-storage domain must be initialized.

For example:

$$ T(x,y,z,0)=T_0 $$ $$ P(x,y,z,0)=P_0 $$ $$ RH(x,y,z,0)=RH_0 $$

or:

$$ \omega(x,y,z,0)=\omega_0 $$ $$ \mathbf{u}(x,y,z,0)=\mathbf{u}_0 $$

The model should support both:

Uniform initial condition
T = -18 °C
RH = 80 %
P = 101325 Pa
Velocity = 0 m/s

and spatially varying conditions:

Zone 1 → -18 °C
Zone 2 → -16 °C
Zone 3 → -20 °C
6. Module 3 — Psychrometric Engine

This is one of the most important modules.

Instead of calculating psychrometric properties independently throughout the solver, create one central thermodynamic/psychrometric engine.

6.1 Fundamental variables

The model internally stores:

$$ T,\quad P,\quad \omega $$

and derives the remaining properties.

6.2 Vapour pressure
$$ p_v=\frac{\omega P}{0.621945+\omega} $$
6.3 Dry-air partial pressure
$$ p_{da}=P-p_v $$
6.4 Relative humidity
$$ RH=\frac{p_v}{p_{ws}(T)} $$

or:

$$ RH(\%)= 100\frac{p_v}{p_{ws}(T)} $$
6.5 Saturation humidity ratio
$$ \omega_s= 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws}(T)} {P-p_{ws}(T)} $$

The saturation-pressure model should account for whether the surface/air temperature is above or below freezing.

For a research-grade implementation, the saturation-property formulation should eventually be based on validated psychrometric/property correlations rather than relying permanently on a simple Magnus-type approximation.

7. Complete Psychrometric Parameter Set

For each cell:

Parameter	Symbol
Temperature	\(T\)
Absolute pressure	\(P\)
Gauge pressure	\(P_g\)
Vapour pressure	\(p_v\)
Dry-air pressure	\(p_{da}\)
Saturation pressure	\(p_{ws}\)
Relative humidity	\(RH\)
Humidity ratio	\(\omega\)
Saturation humidity ratio	\(\omega_s\)
Specific humidity	\(q\)
Dew point	\(T_{dp}\)
Wet bulb	\(T_{wb}\)
Enthalpy	\(h\)
Specific volume	\(v\)
Moist-air density	\(\rho\)
Dry-air density	\(\rho_{da}\)
Vapour density	\(\rho_v\)
Sensible energy	\(E_s\)
Latent energy	\(E_l\)
Total energy	\(E_{total}\)
Velocity	\(V\)
Mass flow	\(\dot m\)
Volumetric flow	\(\dot V\)
Condensation rate	\(\dot m_{cond}\)

Thus, clicking any point in the 3-D model should return this entire table.

8. Specific Humidity

Specific humidity:

$$ q=\frac{m_v}{m_{ma}} $$

and in terms of humidity ratio:

$$ \boxed{ q=\frac{\omega}{1+\omega} } $$
9. Dew-Point Temperature

Dew point is obtained from:

$$ p_{ws}(T_{dp})=p_v $$

Therefore, \(T_{dp}\) is determined numerically from the saturation-pressure relationship.

10. Wet-Bulb Temperature

Wet-bulb temperature should be calculated from the thermodynamic psychrometric relation.

This should be implemented as a numerical root-solving operation rather than simply treating wet-bulb temperature as an independent state variable.

11. Moist-Air Enthalpy

A practical engineering formulation is:

$$ \boxed{ h=1.006T+\omega(2501+1.86T) } $$

where:

\(T\) is in °C
\(h\) is approximately in kJ/kg dry air.

This gives the energy content associated with both dry air and water vapour.

12. Specific Volume
$$ \boxed{ v= \frac{R_{da}T_K(1+1.6078\omega)} {P} } $$

where:

$$ T_K=T+273.15 $$
13. Air Density

Dry-air density:

$$ \rho_{da} = \frac{P} {R_{da}T_K(1+1.6078\omega)} $$

Moist-air density:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{ma}=\rho_{da}(1+\omega) } $$

Density must be updated during the simulation because temperature, pressure and humidity change.

14. Module 4 — Heat Transfer Model

The temperature field will be governed by a transient energy equation.

For the basic model:

$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} = \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) +S_Q +S_{latent} } $$

where:

\(k\) = thermal conductivity
\(c_p\) = specific heat
\(S_Q\) = sensible heat source
\(S_{latent}\) = latent heat source/sink.

Once airflow is introduced:

$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p \left( \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} +\mathbf u\cdot\nabla T \right) = \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) +S_Q+S_{latent} } $$
15. Sensible Heat

Sensible heat associated with a mass \(m\):

$$ Q_s=mc_p\Delta T $$

For a continuous heat source:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_s=\dot m c_p(T_{in}-T_{ref}) } $$
16. Latent Heat

For condensation:

$$ \boxed{ Q_l=m_{cond}L_v } $$

For a rate:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_l=\dot m_{cond}L_v } $$

The dashboard should distinguish clearly between:

Sensible heat
Latent heat
Total heat/energy

rather than displaying a single ambiguous "heat" value.

17. Total Energy

For a control volume:

$$ \boxed{ E_{total}=E_s+E_l } $$

The exact implementation should define the reference state consistently.

The dashboard can therefore display:

Sensible Energy       : xxx kJ
Latent Energy         : xxx kJ
Total Energy          : xxx kJ
Heat Transfer Rate    : xxx kW
Latent Heat Rate      : xxx kW
18. Module 5 — Moisture Transport

Moisture should not simply be recalculated from RH independently.

The transported quantity should preferably be the humidity ratio \(\omega\).

The governing equation is:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) = \nabla\cdot (\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) +S_\omega } $$

This accounts for:

moisture accumulation
moisture convection
moisture diffusion
injected moisture
leaked moisture
condensation
evaporation.
19. Module 6 — Condensation

At each cell:

$$ p_v > p_{ws}(T) $$

or equivalently:

$$ RH>100\% $$

indicates supersaturation.

The model then calculates condensation.

A simple equilibrium approach:

$$ \omega_{new} = \min(\omega,\omega_s) $$

Condensed moisture:

$$ \Delta\omega_{cond} = \omega-\omega_{new} $$

Then:

$$ \dot m_{cond} = \rho_{da} \frac{\Delta\omega_{cond}}{\Delta t} V_{cell} $$

and latent heat:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{latent} = \dot m_{cond}L_v } $$

This heat is coupled back into the temperature equation.

20. Module 7 — Airflow

The model must eventually solve the air velocity field:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u=(u,v,w) } $$

with:

$$ V= \sqrt{u^2+v^2+w^2} $$

The airflow determines how temperature, moisture and energy are transported.

21. Continuity Equation

For variable-density air:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u) = S_m } $$

where \(S_m\) represents mass injection/removal.

22. Momentum Equation

The airflow model can use:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho\mathbf u)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u\otimes\mathbf u) = -\nabla P + \nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau} + \rho\mathbf g + \mathbf S_u } $$

This provides:

\(u\)
\(v\)
\(w\)
\(P\)

throughout the storage.

23. Pressure Field

Pressure must be treated as a field:

$$ P=P(x,y,z,t) $$

rather than as one constant number.

The dashboard should therefore be able to display:

Pressure contour
$$ P(x,y,z) $$
Pressure difference
$$ \Delta P=P-P_{ref} $$
Pressure evolution
$$ P(x,y,z,t) $$

This is particularly important for modelling leakage and pressure-driven injection.

24. Module 8 — Air Injection

This is a major feature of the proposed model.

The dashboard should allow the user to create multiple injection points.

For example:

Injection Point 1
x = 1.0 m
y = 2.5 m
z = 4.0 m

Temperature = -5 °C
RH = 70 %
Pressure = 102 kPa
Velocity = 3 m/s
Area = 0.05 m²
Heat = 2 kW

and independently:

Injection Point 2
x = 8.0 m
y = 1.5 m
z = 2.0 m

Temperature = -25 °C
RH = 90 %
Pressure = 101 kPa
Flow rate = 0.8 m³/s
25. Injection Input Parameters

Each injection source should contain:

$$ \boxed{ I_i= (x,y,z, A, P, T, RH, \omega, \dot V, \dot m, V, \mathbf u, \dot Q, \dot Q_s, \dot Q_l, t_{start}, t_{end}) } $$

where:

location
area
pressure
temperature
humidity
humidity ratio
volumetric flow
mass flow
velocity
heat rate
sensible heat
latent heat
start time
end time

can be independently specified.

26. Injection by Velocity

If velocity and opening area are known:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V=A V } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m=\rho\dot V } $$
27. Injection by Volumetric Flow Rate

If:

$$ \dot V $$

is specified:

$$ \boxed{ V=\frac{\dot V}{A} } $$

Then:

$$ \dot m=\rho\dot V $$
28. Pressure-Driven Injection

If pressure is specified:

$$ \Delta P=P_{in}-P_{room} $$

For an orifice:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V= C_dA \sqrt{\frac{2\Delta P}{\rho}} } $$

This allows the user to specify:

Pressure = 105 kPa
Room pressure = 101 kPa
Opening area = 0.05 m²
Cd = 0.65

and the model calculates the resulting airflow.

29. Injection Humidity

If the user specifies:

RH
$$ p_{v,in}=RH_{in}p_{ws}(T_{in}) $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{in} = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,in}} {P_{in}-p_{v,in}} } $$

Alternatively the user can directly specify:

RH
humidity ratio
dew point
vapour pressure.

The interface should allow these as alternative input modes rather than requiring all simultaneously.

30. Injection Heat

If an air stream enters with temperature different from the room:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_s= \dot m c_p(T_{in}-T_{ref}) } $$

If the user directly specifies heat:

$$ \boxed{ T_{in} = T_{ref} + \frac{\dot Q_s} {\dot m c_p} } $$

The system must distinguish between:

Heat rate
$$ \dot Q \quad [W] $$

and:

Total heat
$$ Q\quad[J] $$
31. Injection Latent Heat

If the injected air has a different humidity ratio:

$$ \Delta\omega = \omega_{in}-\omega_{room} $$

The moisture-associated energy can be represented through enthalpy or latent heat:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_l = \dot m_{da} (\omega_{in}-\omega_{room})L_v } $$

This is important when humid outside air enters a cold room.

32. Multiple Injection Points

The numerical source term becomes:

$$ \boxed{ S_m=\sum_{i=1}^{N}S_{m,i} } $$ $$ \boxed{ S_\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{N}S_{\omega,i} } $$ $$ \boxed{ S_Q=\sum_{i=1}^{N}S_{Q,i} } $$

Thus, the model can simulate:

Inlet 1 → cold dry air
Inlet 2 → warm humid air
Inlet 3 → high-pressure air
Inlet 4 → low-temperature air
Inlet 5 → internal heat source

simultaneously.

33. Spatial Distribution of Injection

A point source should not necessarily be applied to only one numerical cell.

A source can be distributed over nearby cells using a normalized Gaussian:

$$ G(\mathbf x) = \frac{1} {(2\pi\sigma^2)^{3/2}} e^{-|\mathbf x-\mathbf x_s|^2/(2\sigma^2)} $$

with:

$$ \int_\Omega G(\mathbf x)dV=1 $$

Then:

$$ S_Q(\mathbf x) = \dot QG(\mathbf x) $$

and similarly for mass and moisture.

For physical air inlets, however, boundary-face injection is preferable where possible.

34. Module 9 — Leakage

Leakage should be treated as another mass, momentum, energy and moisture boundary/source mechanism.

For a leakage opening:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_{leak} = C_dA_{leak} \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P|} {\rho} } } $$

where:

$$ \Delta P=P_{outside}-P_{inside} $$

The sign determines the direction.

35. Leakage Direction

If:

$$ P_{outside}>P_{inside} $$

then:

$$ \text{outside air}\rightarrow\text{cold storage} $$

If:

$$ P_{inside}>P_{outside} $$

then:

$$ \text{cold storage}\rightarrow\text{outside} $$

This is critical because infiltration can introduce:

warm air
moisture
sensible heat
latent heat.
36. Leakage Moisture

For incoming outside air:

$$ \dot m_{v,leak} = \dot m_{da,leak}\omega_{outside} $$

This moisture enters the cold room and may subsequently condense.

Therefore:

Leakage
   ↓
Moisture increases
   ↓
RH increases
   ↓
Dew point relationship changes
   ↓
Condensation may occur
   ↓
Latent heat released
   ↓
Temperature field changes
37. Door Opening / Leakage Event

The dashboard should allow transient events:

Door:
Opening location = x,y,z
Area = 4 m²
Start time = 3600 s
End time = 3660 s
Outside T = 30 °C
Outside RH = 65 %
Outside P = 101 kPa

During that interval:

$$ A_{door}(t)=A_{door} $$

and outside-air infiltration is calculated.

Before and after:

$$ A_{door}(t)=0 $$

This allows simulation of realistic door-opening events.

38. Module 10 — Wall Heat Transfer

The walls should be modelled as thermal boundaries.

Convective boundary:

$$ \boxed{ -k\frac{\partial T}{\partial n} = h(T_\infty-T_s) } $$

For multilayer insulation:

$$ R_{wall} = \frac1{h_i} + \sum_i\frac{L_i}{k_i} + \frac1{h_o} $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ U=\frac1{R_{wall}} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q=UA(T_{outside}-T_{inside}) } $$

This heat load becomes a boundary source for the numerical model.

39. Internal Heat Sources

The model should support:

refrigeration equipment
fans
lighting
motors
workers
stored products
other equipment.

If a source releases:

$$ \dot Q $$

over a volume:

$$ V_s $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ S_Q=\frac{\dot Q}{V_s} } $$
40. Numerical Discretization

For the advanced numerical model, use the Finite Volume Method (FVM).

This is preferable to relying solely on the existing FDM because FVM provides a natural framework for:

mass conservation
heat conservation
moisture conservation
airflow
pressure boundaries
injection
leakage
arbitrary source terms.

The general transport equation is:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho\phi)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u\phi) = \nabla\cdot(\Gamma\nabla\phi) + S_\phi } $$

where \(\phi\) can represent:

temperature
humidity
momentum/scalar quantities.
41. Numerical Solution Sequence

At each time step:

$$ t_n\rightarrow t_{n+1} $$

the solver should execute:

Step 1 — Determine boundary/source conditions
Injection
Leakage
Walls
Doors
Equipment
Outside conditions
Step 2 — Solve pressure
$$ P^n\rightarrow P^{n+1} $$
Step 3 — Solve velocity
$$ \mathbf u^n\rightarrow\mathbf u^{n+1} $$
Step 4 — Solve moisture
$$ \omega^n\rightarrow\omega^{n+1} $$
Step 5 — Solve temperature
$$ T^n\rightarrow T^{n+1} $$
Step 6 — Condensation calculation

Calculate:

$$ RH,\omega_s,T_{dp},\dot m_{cond},\dot Q_{latent} $$
Step 7 — Update thermodynamic properties

Calculate:

$$ \rho,\rho_{da},v,h,p_v,p_{da},T_{wb},\ldots $$
Step 8 — Conservation checks

Check:

$$ \text{mass balance} $$ $$ \text{moisture balance} $$ $$ \text{energy balance} $$
Step 9 — Save state

Store:

$$ Q(x,y,z,t) $$
Step 10 — Dashboard update

Display the resulting fields.

42. Time-Dependent Data Structure

This is essential for your requirement of every point with lapse of time.

The simulation should conceptually generate:

$$ \boxed{ Q(x_i,y_j,z_k,t_n) } $$

For example:

Time = 0 s
    Cell (10,20,5)
        T = -18.0 °C
        P = 101.3 kPa
        RH = 80 %
        ...

Time = 10 s
    Cell (10,20,5)
        T = -17.8 °C
        P = 101.2 kPa
        RH = 82 %
        ...

Time = 20 s
    Cell (10,20,5)
        T = -17.5 °C
        P = 101.1 kPa
        RH = 85 %
        ...
43. Dashboard — 3-D Field Visualization

The dashboard should provide selectable fields:

┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│ FIELD                               │
│                                     │
│ ○ Temperature                       │
│ ○ Pressure                          │
│ ○ Relative Humidity                 │
│ ○ Humidity Ratio                    │
│ ○ Vapour Pressure                   │
│ ○ Dew Point                         │
│ ○ Wet Bulb                          │
│ ○ Enthalpy                          │
│ ○ Density                           │
│ ○ Specific Volume                   │
│ ○ Velocity Magnitude                │
│ ○ Velocity X                         │
│ ○ Velocity Y                         │
│ ○ Velocity Z                         │
│ ○ Sensible Heat                     │
│ ○ Latent Heat                       │
│ ○ Total Energy                      │
│ ○ Condensation Rate                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────┘

The user selects a field and the 3-D domain changes accordingly.

44. Time Slider

The dashboard should contain:

Time: 00:35:20

|────────────●────────────────────|

0 s                              3600 s

Moving the slider changes:

$$ Q(x,y,z,t) $$

displayed on the 3-D model.

This makes it possible to see the propagation of:

warm air
cold air
humidity
pressure
heat
condensation.
45. Point Selection

The user should be able to click anywhere in the 3-D domain.

For example:

Selected Point

x = 4.25 m
y = 7.10 m
z = 2.40 m

The dashboard then displays:

Parameter	Value
Temperature	-16.82 °C
Pressure	101.24 kPa
RH	87.3 %
Humidity ratio	0.00092 kg/kg
Vapour pressure	0.082 kPa
Dew point	-18.6 °C
Wet bulb	-17.4 °C
Enthalpy	-13.2 kJ/kg
Density	1.40 kg/m³
Specific volume	0.714 m³/kg
Velocity	0.82 m/s
Sensible energy	...
Latent energy	...
Total energy	...
Condensation rate	...

This is one of the most important dashboard features.

46. Point History

After selecting a point, the dashboard should plot:

Temperature vs time
$$ T(t) $$
Pressure vs time
$$ P(t) $$
RH vs time
$$ RH(t) $$
Humidity ratio vs time
$$ \omega(t) $$
Enthalpy vs time
$$ h(t) $$
Dew point vs time
$$ T_{dp}(t) $$
Velocity vs time
$$ V(t) $$
Latent heat vs time
$$ Q_l(t) $$

etc.

Thus, the user can answer:

"What happened at this particular location during the first 2 hours?"

47. Spatial Cross-Section

The dashboard should also allow:

XY plane at z = 2.5 m

XZ plane at y = 4.0 m

YZ plane at x = 6.0 m

The selected psychrometric quantity can then be displayed as a 2-D contour.

For example:

$$ T(x,y,z=2.5,t) $$

or:

$$ RH(x,y,z=2.5,t) $$
48. Velocity Visualization

Velocity should be displayed using vectors/arrows:

$$ \mathbf u=(u,v,w) $$

and magnitude:

$$ V=|\mathbf u| $$

This will allow the user to see:

      → → →
    ↗       ↘
  →     ↓     →
    ↘       ↗
      → → →

representing the air circulation inside the cold storage.

49. Injection Visualization

Injection points should be displayed directly in the 3-D geometry.

For example:

             Injection 1
                 ↓
       ┌───────────────────┐
       │       ↓↓↓         │
       │      ↓↓↓          │
       │                   │
       │             →→→   │
       │          Injection│
       │             2     │
       └───────────────────┘

Each injection point should have a marker.

Clicking it should show:

Injection #2

T       = -5 °C
P       = 104 kPa
RH      = 75 %
Flow    = 0.45 m³/s
Velocity= 3.2 m/s
Heat    = 1.8 kW
50. Leakage Visualization

Leakage locations should also be displayed.

For example:

Door / Leakage #1

Area       = 4 m²
ΔP         = +125 Pa
Flow       = 1.32 m³/s
Direction  = INTO ROOM
T outside  = 30 °C
RH outside = 65 %

The dashboard can show the leakage airflow vector.

51. Psychrometric Chart

A dedicated psychrometric chart should show the thermodynamic trajectory of selected points.

For a selected location:

$$ (T(t),\omega(t)) $$

can be plotted over time.

For example:

Humidity ratio
     ↑
     │        ● t=3600
     │      ●
     │    ●
     │  ●
     │●
     └────────────────→ Temperature

This allows visualization of processes such as:

cooling
heating
humidification
dehumidification
condensation
mixing.
52. Room-Level Quantities

In addition to local values, the dashboard should calculate spatial statistics.

Volume-average temperature
$$ \boxed{ \bar T= \frac{1}{V} \int_VT\,dV } $$
Average RH
$$ \bar{RH} = \frac1V\int_VRH\,dV $$
Average pressure
$$ \bar P= \frac1V\int_VP\,dV $$
Total condensed water
$$ M_{cond,total} = \int_0^t\dot m_{cond}\,dt $$
Total heat load
$$ Q_{total} = \int_0^t\dot Q\,dt $$
53. Cold-Storage Performance Indicators

The dashboard should calculate:

Cooling time
$$ t^*= \min\{t:\bar T(t)\leq T_{target}\} $$
Maximum temperature
$$ T_{max}(t)=\max_{\Omega}T $$
Minimum temperature
$$ T_{min}(t)=\min_{\Omega}T $$
Maximum RH
$$ RH_{max}(t) $$
Maximum pressure difference
$$ \Delta P_{max} $$
Total condensation
$$ M_{cond} $$
Total heat entering
$$ Q_{in} $$
Total leakage mass
$$ M_{leak} $$
Total injected mass
$$ M_{inj} $$
54. Conservation Checks

Every simulation should continuously check:

Mass balance
$$ \boxed{ \frac{dM}{dt} = \dot M_{in} - \dot M_{out} + \dot M_{source} } $$
Moisture balance
$$ \boxed{ \frac{dM_v}{dt} = \dot M_{v,in} - \dot M_{v,out} + \dot M_{v,source} - \dot M_{cond} + \dot M_{evap} } $$
Energy balance
$$ \boxed{ \frac{dE}{dt} = \dot Q_{in} - \dot Q_{out} + \dot Q_{wall} + \dot Q_{equipment} + \dot Q_{latent} } $$

The dashboard should report conservation errors.

55. Numerical Stability

For diffusion:

$$ \alpha=\frac{k}{\rho c_p} $$

For a 3-D explicit scheme with unequal grid spacing:

$$ \boxed{ \alpha\Delta t \left( \frac1{\Delta x^2} + \frac1{\Delta y^2} + \frac1{\Delta z^2} \right) \leq \frac12 } $$

For moisture:

$$ \boxed{ D_{eff}\Delta t \left( \frac1{\Delta x^2} + \frac1{\Delta y^2} + \frac1{\Delta z^2} \right) \leq \frac12 } $$

For advection, monitor the CFL condition:

$$ C_x=\frac{|u|\Delta t}{\Delta x} $$ $$ C_y=\frac{|v|\Delta t}{\Delta y} $$ $$ C_z=\frac{|w|\Delta t}{\Delta z} $$

The timestep should satisfy the selected numerical scheme's stability requirement.

58. Important Input Design Decision

Do not require the user to specify every possible parameter simultaneously.

For example, for an inlet, the user could select:

Flow specification
○ Velocity
○ Volumetric flow
○ Mass flow
○ Pressure difference

and:

Moisture specification
○ RH
○ Humidity ratio
○ Dew point
○ Vapour pressure

and:

Thermal specification
○ Temperature
○ Sensible heat rate
○ Total heat rate

The system calculates the remaining dependent properties.

This avoids physically inconsistent over-specification.

59. Example Complete Simulation

Suppose:

Cold room
$$ T_0=-18^\circ C $$ $$ RH_0=85\% $$ $$ P_0=101325 Pa $$
Injection

At:

$$ (x,y,z)=(2,4,3)m $$

air enters at:

$$ T_{in}=-5^\circ C $$ $$ RH_{in}=70\% $$ $$ P_{in}=103kPa $$

with:

$$ \dot V=0.5m^3/s $$
Leakage

Door:

$$ A=3m^2 $$

Outside:

$$ T_{out}=30^\circ C $$ $$ RH_{out}=60\% $$ $$ P_{out}=101.5kPa $$

for:

$$ t=600-660s $$

The solver calculates:

Pressure field
       ↓
Velocity field
       ↓
Air transport
       ↓
Temperature transport
       ↓
Moisture transport
       ↓
RH / dew point / wet bulb
       ↓
Condensation
       ↓
Latent heat
       ↓
Updated temperature

and repeats this for every timestep.

Upgrade:

$$ \frac{\partial\omega}{\partial t} $$

to:

$$ \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) = \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) +S_\omega $$

Deliverable: airflow + temperature + moisture coupling.

with:

as external/source mechanisms.

The central research objective

The numerical model should ultimately answer this question:

Given the geometry, initial conditions, wall properties, air-injection conditions, leakage conditions, heat loads, and boundary conditions of a cold-storage facility, how do temperature, pressure, airflow, moisture, humidity, energy and condensation evolve at every spatial location as a function of time?

That should be the core scope of the project. ML/AI should be completely outside this implementation plan.

The most important immediate development step is therefore not the dashboard. It is to establish the underlying computational state and physics correctly:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Psychrometrics} \rightarrow \text{Heat + Moisture} \rightarrow \text{Pressure + Airflow} \rightarrow \text{Injection + Leakage} \rightarrow \text{Full transient 3-D model} \rightarrow \text{Dashboard} } $$

This sequence will prevent the dashboard from becoming merely a visualization layer over an incomplete physical model.











Module 1 — Geometry and Computational Mesh: Complete Mathematical Formulation

For the numerical cold-storage model, Module 1 establishes the physical domain and converts it into a computational mesh on which temperature, pressure, velocity, moisture and all derived psychrometric quantities will be calculated.

The objective is to define:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Physical Cold Storage} \longrightarrow \text{Mathematical Domain} \longrightarrow \text{Computational Cells} } $$

The following formulation is suitable for a 3-D structured Cartesian Finite Volume Model.

1. Physical Domain

Consider a rectangular cold-storage room having:

Length \(L_x\)
Width \(L_y\)
Height \(L_z\)

The computational domain is

$$ \boxed{ \Omega = \left\{ (x,y,z): 0\leq x\leq L_x,\; 0\leq y\leq L_y,\; 0\leq z\leq L_z \right\} } $$

The total room volume is:

$$ \boxed{ V_{room}=L_xL_yL_z } $$
Example

If:

$$ L_x=20\,m,\qquad L_y=10\,m,\qquad L_z=5\,m $$

then:

$$ V_{room}=20\times10\times5=1000\,m^3 $$
2. Coordinate System

Define a Cartesian coordinate system:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf{x}=(x,y,z) } $$

where:

\(x\): longitudinal direction
\(y\): lateral direction
\(z\): vertical direction

The origin can be placed at one lower corner:

$$ \boxed{ (0,0,0) } $$

and the opposite upper corner is:

$$ \boxed{ (L_x,L_y,L_z) } $$
3. Six Physical Boundaries

The rectangular room has six boundary surfaces:

Left wall
$$ \boxed{x=0} $$
Right wall
$$ \boxed{x=L_x} $$
Front wall
$$ \boxed{y=0} $$
Back wall
$$ \boxed{y=L_y} $$
Floor
$$ \boxed{z=0} $$
Ceiling
$$ \boxed{z=L_z} $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \partial\Omega= \Gamma_{x=0} \cup \Gamma_{x=L_x} \cup \Gamma_{y=0} \cup \Gamma_{y=L_y} \cup \Gamma_{z=0} \cup \Gamma_{z=L_z} } $$
4. Number of Computational Cells

Divide the room into:

$$ N_x $$

cells in the \(x\)-direction,

$$ N_y $$

cells in the \(y\)-direction, and

$$ N_z $$

cells in the \(z\)-direction.

Total number of cells:

$$ \boxed{ N_{cells}=N_xN_yN_z } $$

For example:

$$ N_x=40,\quad N_y=20,\quad N_z=10 $$

gives:

$$ N_{cells}=40\times20\times10 =8000 $$

Each cell will eventually contain:

$$ T,\;P,\;u,\;v,\;w,\;\omega $$

and derived quantities.

5. Uniform Cartesian Mesh

For a uniform mesh:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta x=\frac{L_x}{N_x} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \Delta y=\frac{L_y}{N_y} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \Delta z=\frac{L_z}{N_z} } $$

If:

$$ \Delta x=\Delta y=\Delta z $$

the mesh is called a uniform cubic mesh.

6. Non-Uniform Mesh

For higher resolution near:

air inlets
outlets
doors
walls
refrigeration units
leakage locations

a non-uniform mesh can be used.

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta x_i=x_{i+1/2}-x_{i-1/2} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \Delta y_j=y_{j+1/2}-y_{j-1/2} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \Delta z_k=z_{k+1/2}-z_{k-1/2} } $$

where the half-index positions represent cell faces.

7. Cell Indexing

Each cell is identified using:

$$ \boxed{ (i,j,k) } $$

where:

$$ i=1,2,\ldots,N_x $$ $$ j=1,2,\ldots,N_y $$ $$ k=1,2,\ldots,N_z $$

Thus:

$$ \boxed{ C_{i,j,k} } $$

represents one computational control volume.

8. Cell-Face Coordinates

For a uniform grid, the \(x\)-faces are:

$$ \boxed{ x_{i-\frac12}=(i-1)\Delta x } $$ $$ \boxed{ x_{i+\frac12}=i\Delta x } $$

Similarly:

$$ \boxed{ y_{j-\frac12}=(j-1)\Delta y } $$ $$ \boxed{ y_{j+\frac12}=j\Delta y } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ z_{k-\frac12}=(k-1)\Delta z } $$ $$ \boxed{ z_{k+\frac12}=k\Delta z } $$
9. Cell-Center Coordinates

The center of cell \((i,j,k)\) is:

$$ \boxed{ x_i= \frac{x_{i-\frac12}+x_{i+\frac12}}{2} } $$ $$ \boxed{ y_j= \frac{y_{j-\frac12}+y_{j+\frac12}}{2} } $$ $$ \boxed{ z_k= \frac{z_{k-\frac12}+z_{k+\frac12}}{2} } $$

For a uniform mesh:

$$ \boxed{ x_i=\left(i-\frac12\right)\Delta x } $$ $$ \boxed{ y_j=\left(j-\frac12\right)\Delta y } $$ $$ \boxed{ z_k=\left(k-\frac12\right)\Delta z } $$
10. Position Vector of Cell Center

The location of cell \((i,j,k)\) is:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf{x}_{i,j,k} = (x_i,y_j,z_k) } $$

This becomes important when specifying an injection or leakage point.

11. Control-Volume Dimensions

For cell \((i,j,k)\):

$$ \Delta x_i=x_{i+1/2}-x_{i-1/2} $$ $$ \Delta y_j=y_{j+1/2}-y_{j-1/2} $$ $$ \Delta z_k=z_{k+1/2}-z_{k-1/2} $$
12. Cell Volume

The control-volume volume is:

$$ \boxed{ V_{i,j,k} = \Delta x_i\Delta y_j\Delta z_k } $$

For a uniform grid:

$$ \boxed{ V_{cell}=\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ V_{room} = \sum_{i=1}^{N_x} \sum_{j=1}^{N_y} \sum_{k=1}^{N_z} V_{i,j,k} } $$

For a complete rectangular domain:

$$ \boxed{ \sum V_{i,j,k}=L_xL_yL_z } $$

This is an important mesh verification check.

13. Six Cell Faces

Every internal control volume has six faces:

$$ \boxed{ f\in \{E,W,N,S,T,B\} } $$

where:

\(E\): east
\(W\): west
\(N\): north
\(S\): south
\(T\): top
\(B\): bottom
14. Face Areas

East and west faces:

$$ \boxed{ A_E=A_W=\Delta y\Delta z } $$

North and south faces:

$$ \boxed{ A_N=A_S=\Delta x\Delta z } $$

Top and bottom faces:

$$ \boxed{ A_T=A_B=\Delta x\Delta y } $$

For a non-uniform grid:

$$ A_E=A_W=\Delta y_j\Delta z_k $$ $$ A_N=A_S=\Delta x_i\Delta z_k $$ $$ A_T=A_B=\Delta x_i\Delta y_j $$
15. Face-Normal Vectors

The outward normal vectors are:

East
$$ \boxed{ \mathbf n_E=(1,0,0) } $$
West
$$ \boxed{ \mathbf n_W=(-1,0,0) } $$
North
$$ \boxed{ \mathbf n_N=(0,1,0) } $$
South
$$ \boxed{ \mathbf n_S=(0,-1,0) } $$
Top
$$ \boxed{ \mathbf n_T=(0,0,1) } $$
Bottom
$$ \boxed{ \mathbf n_B=(0,0,-1) } $$

The corresponding vector face areas are:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf A_f=A_f\mathbf n_f } $$
16. Distance Between Neighboring Cell Centers

For a uniform Cartesian mesh:

$$ \boxed{ d_E=d_W=\Delta x } $$ $$ \boxed{ d_N=d_S=\Delta y } $$ $$ \boxed{ d_T=d_B=\Delta z } $$

For nonuniform cells:

$$ \boxed{ d_{PE}=x_E-x_P } $$

and similarly for the other directions.

17. Neighbouring Cells

For cell \(P=(i,j,k)\):

$$ E=(i+1,j,k) $$ $$ W=(i-1,j,k) $$ $$ N=(i,j+1,k) $$ $$ S=(i,j-1,k) $$ $$ T=(i,j,k+1) $$ $$ B=(i,j,k-1) $$

This six-neighbour topology is fundamental to the numerical solution.

18. Cell-Center Distance for a General Pair

For two cell centers:

$$ \mathbf{x}_P=(x_P,y_P,z_P) $$

and:

$$ \mathbf{x}_N=(x_N,y_N,z_N) $$

the Euclidean distance is:

$$ \boxed{ d_{PN} = \sqrt{ (x_N-x_P)^2+ (y_N-y_P)^2+ (z_N-z_P)^2 } } $$

For adjacent Cartesian cells this simplifies to \(\Delta x\), \(\Delta y\), or \(\Delta z\).

19. Total Mesh Volume Check

After generating the mesh:

$$ \boxed{ V_{mesh} = \sum_{i,j,k}V_{i,j,k} } $$

The geometric error should be:

$$ \boxed{ \epsilon_V = \frac{|V_{mesh}-V_{room}|} {V_{room}} \times100 } $$

For a correctly generated mesh:

$$ \boxed{ \epsilon_V\approx0 } $$

up to floating-point precision.

20. Surface Area of the Cold Room

The six wall areas are:

$$ A_{x=0}=L_yL_z $$ $$ A_{x=L_x}=L_yL_z $$ $$ A_{y=0}=L_xL_z $$ $$ A_{y=L_y}=L_xL_z $$ $$ A_{z=0}=L_xL_y $$ $$ A_{z=L_z}=L_xL_y $$

Therefore the total surface area is:

$$ \boxed{ A_{room} = 2(L_xL_y+L_xL_z+L_yL_z) } $$

This area will later be required for wall heat-transfer calculations.

21. Boundary Identification

A cell face belongs to the external boundary if it satisfies:

West boundary
$$ i=1 $$
East boundary
$$ i=N_x $$
South boundary
$$ j=1 $$
North boundary
$$ j=N_y $$
Bottom boundary
$$ k=1 $$
Top boundary
$$ k=N_z $$

This lets the solver automatically distinguish internal faces from physical boundaries.

22. Boundary-Cell Volume

Boundary cells have the same control-volume formulation:

$$ V_{i,j,k} = \Delta x_i\Delta y_j\Delta z_k $$

but one or more faces lie on the physical boundary.

For example, cell \((1,j,k)\) has its west face on:

$$ x=0 $$
23. Ghost Cells

For boundary-condition implementation, ghost cells can optionally be introduced.

For example, west boundary:

Ghost cell     Boundary     Interior
     |              |            |
     ●--------------|------------●
    i-1            x=0           i

The ghost-cell location is:

$$ \boxed{ x_G=x_P-\Delta x } $$

for a uniform grid.

Ghost cells are useful for implementing:

Dirichlet boundary conditions
Neumann boundary conditions
wall heat transfer
velocity boundaries
injection boundaries.
24. Mesh Resolution

The spatial resolution is:

$$ \boxed{ R_x=\frac{N_x}{L_x} } $$ $$ \boxed{ R_y=\frac{N_y}{L_y} } $$ $$ \boxed{ R_z=\frac{N_z}{L_z} } $$

in cells/m.

Alternatively, the characteristic mesh size can be defined as:

$$ \boxed{ h= \max(\Delta x,\Delta y,\Delta z) } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ h= \min(\Delta x,\Delta y,\Delta z) } $$

depending on the convergence-study definition.

25. Aspect Ratio

The cell aspect ratios are:

$$ \boxed{ AR_{xy}=\frac{\Delta x}{\Delta y} } $$ $$ \boxed{ AR_{xz}=\frac{\Delta x}{\Delta z} } $$ $$ \boxed{ AR_{yz}=\frac{\Delta y}{\Delta z} } $$

For cubic cells:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta x=\Delta y=\Delta z } $$

and therefore:

$$ AR_{xy}=AR_{xz}=AR_{yz}=1 $$

For the first implementation, keeping the mesh close to isotropic is advantageous.

26. Cell Size and Number of Cells

If a desired cell size \(h\) is specified:

$$ \boxed{ N_x= \left\lceil \frac{L_x}{h} \right\rceil } $$ $$ \boxed{ N_y= \left\lceil \frac{L_y}{h} \right\rceil } $$ $$ \boxed{ N_z= \left\lceil \frac{L_z}{h} \right\rceil } $$

The actual cell dimensions then become:

$$ \Delta x=\frac{L_x}{N_x} $$ $$ \Delta y=\frac{L_y}{N_y} $$ $$ \Delta z=\frac{L_z}{N_z} $$
27. Mesh Independence / Grid-Convergence Parameter

For a sequence of meshes:

$$ h_1>h_2>h_3 $$

where \(h_3\) is the finest mesh.

A representative characteristic length can be:

$$ \boxed{ h= \left( \Delta x\Delta y\Delta z \right)^{1/3} } $$

For a uniform cubic mesh:

$$ \boxed{ h=\Delta x=\Delta y=\Delta z } $$
28. Refinement Ratio

For two successive meshes:

$$ \boxed{ r_h=\frac{h_{coarse}}{h_{fine}} } $$

For example:

$$ h_{coarse}=0.5m $$ $$ h_{fine}=0.25m $$

gives:

$$ r_h=2 $$
29. Spatial Coordinates of an Arbitrary Physical Point

For a user-selected point:

$$ \mathbf{x}_q=(x_q,y_q,z_q) $$

the code must determine which cell contains the point.

For a uniform mesh:

$$ \boxed{ i= \left\lfloor \frac{x_q}{\Delta x} \right\rfloor+1 } $$ $$ \boxed{ j= \left\lfloor \frac{y_q}{\Delta y} \right\rfloor+1 } $$ $$ \boxed{ k= \left\lfloor \frac{z_q}{\Delta z} \right\rfloor+1 } $$

with appropriate clipping at the domain boundaries.

This will later support the dashboard's "click anywhere and get all parameters" functionality.

30. Normalized Coordinates Inside a Cell

For interpolation, define:

$$ \xi= \frac{x_q-x_i}{x_{i+1}-x_i} $$ $$ \eta= \frac{y_q-y_j}{y_{j+1}-y_j} $$ $$ \zeta= \frac{z_q-z_k}{z_{k+1}-z_k} $$

where:

$$ 0\leq\xi,\eta,\zeta\leq1 $$
31. Trilinear Interpolation

If the selected point does not exactly coincide with a cell center, a field \(\phi\) can be interpolated.

For the eight surrounding points:

$$ \boxed{ \phi(x,y,z) = \sum_{a=0}^{1} \sum_{b=0}^{1} \sum_{c=0}^{1} N_{abc}\phi_{abc} } $$

where:

$$ N_{000} =(1-\xi)(1-\eta)(1-\zeta) $$ $$ N_{100} =\xi(1-\eta)(1-\zeta) $$ $$ N_{010} =(1-\xi)\eta(1-\zeta) $$ $$ N_{001} =(1-\xi)(1-\eta)\zeta $$ $$ N_{110} =\xi\eta(1-\zeta) $$ $$ N_{101} =\xi(1-\eta)\zeta $$ $$ N_{011} =(1-\xi)\eta\zeta $$ $$ N_{111} =\xi\eta\zeta $$

This is particularly useful for dashboard point queries.

32. Spatial Field Representation

Every primary numerical variable is represented as:

$$ \boxed{ \phi=\phi(x,y,z,t) } $$

For example:

$$ T=T(x,y,z,t) $$ $$ P=P(x,y,z,t) $$ $$ \omega=\omega(x,y,z,t) $$ $$ u=u(x,y,z,t) $$ $$ v=v(x,y,z,t) $$ $$ w=w(x,y,z,t) $$

The complete numerical state is:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf Q(x,y,z,t) = [T,P,u,v,w,\omega] } $$
33. Time-Dependent Computational Grid

The mesh itself generally remains fixed:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf{x}_{i,j,k} \neq \mathbf{x}_{i,j,k}(t) } $$

while the physical variables evolve:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf Q_{i,j,k}^{n} \rightarrow \mathbf Q_{i,j,k}^{n+1} } $$

Thus:

$$ \mathbf Q_{i,j,k}^{n} = [T_{i,j,k}^{n}, P_{i,j,k}^{n}, u_{i,j,k}^{n}, v_{i,j,k}^{n}, w_{i,j,k}^{n}, \omega_{i,j,k}^{n}] $$
34. Computational Cell State

For every cell, the model will eventually maintain:

$$ \boxed{ \mathcal S_{i,j,k}^{n} = \{ x,y,z, V, T,P, u,v,w, \omega, RH, p_v, p_{da}, p_{ws}, T_{dp}, T_{wb}, h, v_{sp}, \rho, E_s, E_l, E_{total}, \dot m_{cond} \} } $$

The geometry module itself only needs to establish:

$$ \boxed{ x,y,z,V,A_f,\mathbf n_f,\Delta x,\Delta y,\Delta z } $$

The remaining quantities are supplied by subsequent physics modules.

35. Injection-Point Geometry

Since your final model must support air injection at arbitrary points, Module 1 should also provide a geometric representation of injection locations.

An injection point is:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf{x}_{inj} = (x_{inj},y_{inj},z_{inj}) } $$

The distance between the injection point and a cell center is:

$$ \boxed{ r_{ijk} = \sqrt{ (x_i-x_{inj})^2+ (y_j-y_{inj})^2+ (z_k-z_{inj})^2 } } $$

This distance will later be used to distribute a point/volumetric source.

36. Mapping an Injection Point to a Cell

The injection point can be mapped to:

$$ \boxed{ (i_{inj},j_{inj},k_{inj}) } $$

using:

$$ i_{inj} = \left\lfloor \frac{x_{inj}}{\Delta x} \right\rfloor+1 $$ $$ j_{inj} = \left\lfloor \frac{y_{inj}}{\Delta y} \right\rfloor+1 $$ $$ k_{inj} = \left\lfloor \frac{z_{inj}}{\Delta z} \right\rfloor+1 $$

This is only a geometric mapping. The actual injection source term belongs to the injection module.

37. Injection Opening Area

If an inlet is rectangular:

$$ \boxed{ A_{inj}=W_{inj}H_{inj} } $$

If circular:

$$ \boxed{ A_{inj} = \frac{\pi D_{inj}^{2}}{4} } $$

If arbitrary:

$$ \boxed{ A_{inj} = \int_{\Gamma_{inj}}dA } $$

This area will later be required for:

$$ \dot V=A_{inj}V_{inj} $$
38. Leakage Opening Geometry

Similarly, a leakage opening has:

$$ \boxed{ A_{leak} } $$

For a rectangular door:

$$ \boxed{ A_{door}=W_{door}H_{door} } $$

For a circular opening:

$$ \boxed{ A_{leak}=\frac{\pi D^2}{4} } $$

For an irregular opening:

$$ \boxed{ A_{leak} = \int_{\Gamma_{leak}}dA } $$
39. Distance From a Cell to an Injection/Leakage Location

For any cell:

$$ \boxed{ r_{ijk} = |\mathbf{x}_{ijk}-\mathbf{x}_{source}| } $$

or explicitly:

$$ r_{ijk} = \sqrt{ (x_i-x_s)^2+ (y_j-y_s)^2+ (z_k-z_s)^2 } $$

This gives the geometry required for later source-distribution functions.

40. Source Distribution Radius

If a source affects cells within a radius \(R_s\):

$$ \boxed{ \mathcal C_s = \{ (i,j,k): r_{ijk}\leq R_s \} } $$

The number of affected cells is:

$$ \boxed{ N_s=|\mathcal C_s| } $$

This allows an injection to be distributed over a finite region rather than a single cell.

41. Gaussian Source Geometry

If a Gaussian spatial source is used later:

$$ \boxed{ G(r) = \frac{1} {(2\pi\sigma^2)^{3/2}} \exp \left( -\frac{r^2}{2\sigma^2} \right) } $$

where:

$$ r= \sqrt{ (x-x_s)^2+ (y-y_s)^2+ (z-z_s)^2 } $$

The normalization condition is:

$$ \boxed{ \int_{\Omega}G(\mathbf{x})\,dV\approx1 } $$

over a sufficiently large domain/source region.

42. Obstacles Inside the Cold Storage

If products, racks, machinery, walls or other objects occupy part of the domain, define a solid region:

$$ \boxed{ \Omega_s } $$

and fluid region:

$$ \boxed{ \Omega_f=\Omega-\Omega_s } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \Omega=\Omega_f\cup\Omega_s } $$

with:

$$ \Omega_f\cap\Omega_s=\emptyset $$
43. Fluid-Cell Indicator Function

Define:

$$ \boxed{ I_f(\mathbf{x})= \begin{cases} 1,&\mathbf{x}\in\Omega_f\\ 0,&\mathbf{x}\in\Omega_s \end{cases} } $$

Similarly, solid indicator:

$$ \boxed{ I_s(\mathbf{x})= \begin{cases} 1,&\mathbf{x}\in\Omega_s\\ 0,&\mathbf{x}\in\Omega_f \end{cases} } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ I_f+I_s=1 } $$

This will later allow the solver to distinguish air cells from solid/obstacle cells.

44. Porosity / Occupied Volume

For a partially occupied computational cell:

$$ \boxed{ \varepsilon_{i,j,k} = \frac{V_{fluid}} {V_{cell}} } $$

where:

$$ 0\leq\varepsilon\leq1 $$

For a pure fluid cell:

$$ \varepsilon=1 $$

For a completely solid cell:

$$ \varepsilon=0 $$

This is optional for the first version but useful if racks/products occupy substantial volume.

45. Fluid Volume

For a cell containing an obstruction:

$$ \boxed{ V_{fluid} = \varepsilon V_{cell} } $$

and solid volume:

$$ \boxed{ V_{solid} = (1-\varepsilon)V_{cell} } $$

with:

$$ \boxed{ V_{fluid}+V_{solid}=V_{cell} } $$
46. Mesh Quality Metric

For a structured Cartesian mesh, the primary geometric quality parameter is the aspect ratio.

A simple maximum aspect ratio is:

$$ \boxed{ AR_{max} = \max \left( \frac{\Delta x}{\Delta y}, \frac{\Delta y}{\Delta x}, \frac{\Delta x}{\Delta z}, \frac{\Delta z}{\Delta x}, \frac{\Delta y}{\Delta z}, \frac{\Delta z}{\Delta y} \right) } $$

For cubic cells:

$$ \boxed{ AR_{max}=1 } $$
47. Mesh-Resolution Requirement Near Important Regions

The mesh should be refined around:

$$ \boxed{ \text{inlets} + \text{leakage} + \text{doors} + \text{walls} + \text{cooling units} + \text{major heat sources} } $$

If an inlet diameter is \(D_{inj}\), a useful geometric criterion is to ensure several cells span the inlet:

$$ \boxed{ N_{D} = \frac{D_{inj}}{h} } $$

where \(N_D\) is the number of cells across the characteristic inlet dimension.

This is a mesh-design parameter, not a universal physical requirement. Its appropriate value must be established through grid-convergence testing.

48. Mesh Refinement Ratio Between Regions

If a fine region has:

$$ h_f $$

and surrounding coarse region:

$$ h_c $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ r_{mesh} = \frac{h_c}{h_f} } $$

For example:

$$ r_{mesh}=2 $$

means the fine mesh is twice as spatially resolved.

49. Mesh Data Required by the Solver

Module 1 should ultimately output a mesh object containing:

$$ \boxed{ \mathcal M= \{ N_x,N_y,N_z, x_f,y_f,z_f, x_c,y_c,z_c, \Delta x,\Delta y,\Delta z, V, A_f, \mathbf n_f, d_f, BC_{faces} \} } $$

where:

\(x_f,y_f,z_f\) = face coordinates
\(x_c,y_c,z_c\) = cell-center coordinates
\(V\) = cell volume
\(A_f\) = face areas
\(\mathbf n_f\) = face normals
\(d_f\) = center-to-center distances
\(BC_{faces}\) = boundary identification.
50. Most Important Formulas of Module 1

For implementation, these are the core equations you should actually code first:

Domain
$$ \boxed{ \Omega=[0,L_x]\times[0,L_y]\times[0,L_z] } $$
Number of cells
$$ \boxed{ N=N_xN_yN_z } $$
Cell dimensions
$$ \boxed{ \Delta x=\frac{L_x}{N_x}, \quad \Delta y=\frac{L_y}{N_y}, \quad \Delta z=\frac{L_z}{N_z} } $$
Cell-center coordinates
$$ \boxed{ x_i=\left(i-\frac12\right)\Delta x } $$ $$ \boxed{ y_j=\left(j-\frac12\right)\Delta y } $$ $$ \boxed{ z_k=\left(k-\frac12\right)\Delta z } $$
Cell volume
$$ \boxed{ V_{ijk}=\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z } $$
Face areas
$$ \boxed{ A_E=A_W=\Delta y\Delta z } $$ $$ \boxed{ A_N=A_S=\Delta x\Delta z } $$ $$ \boxed{ A_T=A_B=\Delta x\Delta y } $$
Total volume
$$ \boxed{ V_{room}=L_xL_yL_z = \sum_{ijk}V_{ijk} } $$
Total wall area
$$ \boxed{ A_{room} = 2(L_xL_y+L_xL_z+L_yL_z) } $$
Cell location
$$ \boxed{ \mathbf{x}_{ijk}=(x_i,y_j,z_k) } $$
Distance between points
$$ \boxed{ r= \sqrt{ (x-x_s)^2+ (y-y_s)^2+ (z-z_s)^2 } } $$
Injection-cell mapping
$$ \boxed{ i= \left\lfloor\frac{x_s}{\Delta x}\right\rfloor+1 } $$ $$ \boxed{ j= \left\lfloor\frac{y_s}{\Delta y}\right\rfloor+1 } $$ $$ \boxed{ k= \left\lfloor\frac{z_s}{\Delta z}\right\rfloor+1 } $$
Cell aspect ratio
$$ \boxed{ AR=\frac{h_{max}}{h_{min}} } $$
Characteristic mesh size
$$ \boxed{ h=(\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z)^{1/3} } $$
51. What Module 1 Should Produce

At the end of Module 1, the numerical model should know the complete geometry:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Room dimensions} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \text{Cell locations} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \text{Cell volumes} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \text{Face areas} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \text{Face normals} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \text{Neighbour connectivity} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \text{Boundary faces} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \text{Injection/leakage locations} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \text{Obstacle/solid regions} } $$

and, most importantly, a spatial coordinate system on which the subsequent equations operate:

$$ \boxed{ \underbrace{ \text{Geometry + Mesh} }_{Module\;1} \rightarrow \underbrace{ T,P,\mathbf u,\omega }_{Modules\;2+} } $$
Recommended implementation hierarchy

For your project, I would implement Module 1 in this order:

1. Room geometry
       ↓
2. Cartesian mesh
       ↓
3. Cell centers
       ↓
4. Cell volumes
       ↓
5. Face areas
       ↓
6. Face normals
       ↓
7. Neighbour connectivity
       ↓
8. Boundary-face identification
       ↓
9. Arbitrary point → cell mapping
       ↓
10. Injection/leakage location mapping
       ↓
11. Obstacles / occupied regions
       ↓
12. Mesh-quality & volume checks
       ↓
13. Export mesh to numerical solver

This gives a clean geometric foundation for the next modules. Module 2 should then use this mesh to define the initial \(T,P,RH/\omega,\mathbf u\) fields and the complete psychrometric state.













Module 2 — Initial Conditions: Complete Mathematical Formulation

Module 2 defines the initial thermodynamic and flow state of every computational cell at \(t=0\).

Module 1 gave us the spatial domain:

$$ \Omega=[0,L_x]\times[0,L_y]\times[0,L_z] $$

and the computational cells \((i,j,k)\).

Module 2 now assigns the initial values of:

$$ \boxed{ T,\;P,\;\mathbf{u},\;\omega } $$

to every cell. From these primary variables, the complete psychrometric state is calculated.

The fundamental objective is:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf Q_{ijk}^{0} = \left[ T_{ijk}^{0}, P_{ijk}^{0}, u_{ijk}^{0}, v_{ijk}^{0}, w_{ijk}^{0}, \omega_{ijk}^{0} \right] } $$

where superscript \(0\) denotes \(t=0\).

1. Initial-State Vector

For each computational cell:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf Q_{i,j,k}^{0} = \begin{bmatrix} T_{i,j,k}^{0}\\ P_{i,j,k}^{0}\\ u_{i,j,k}^{0}\\ v_{i,j,k}^{0}\\ w_{i,j,k}^{0}\\ \omega_{i,j,k}^{0} \end{bmatrix} } $$

These are the primary initial variables.

The following are derived:

$$ \boxed{ RH,\;p_v,\;p_{da},\;p_{ws},\;T_{dp},\;T_{wb}, h,\;v,\;\rho,\;q,\;E_s,\;E_l,\;E_{total} } $$
2. Initial Temperature Field

Temperature is represented as:

$$ \boxed{ T=T(x,y,z,t) } $$

At the beginning:

$$ \boxed{ T(x,y,z,0)=T_0(x,y,z) } $$

For a uniform cold room:

$$ \boxed{ T_0(x,y,z)=T_{room,0} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ T_{ijk}^{0}=T_{room,0} } $$

for every fluid cell.

Example

If the initial room temperature is:

$$ T_{room,0}=-18^\circ C $$

then:

$$ T_{ijk}^{0}=-18^\circ C $$

throughout the room.

3. Spatially Varying Initial Temperature

The model should also support nonuniform temperature.

For example, a linear vertical temperature gradient:

$$ \boxed{ T_0(z) = T_{bottom} + \frac{T_{top}-T_{bottom}}{L_z}z } $$

At \(z=0\):

$$ T_0(0)=T_{bottom} $$

At \(z=L_z\):

$$ T_0(L_z)=T_{top} $$
4. General 3-D Temperature Distribution

A general prescribed temperature field can be written:

$$ \boxed{ T_0(x,y,z)=f_T(x,y,z) } $$

The computational initialization is then:

$$ \boxed{ T_{ijk}^{0} = f_T(x_i,y_j,z_k) } $$

This allows temperature measurements from sensors or experimental data to be mapped onto the numerical grid.

5. Temperature Zones

Suppose the cold storage contains several initial zones:

$$ \Omega=\Omega_1\cup\Omega_2\cup\cdots\cup\Omega_N $$

with:

$$ T_0= \begin{cases} T_1,&\mathbf{x}\in\Omega_1\\ T_2,&\mathbf{x}\in\Omega_2\\ \vdots\\ T_N,&\mathbf{x}\in\Omega_N \end{cases} $$

For example:

Zone 1 → -18 °C
Zone 2 → -20 °C
Zone 3 → -16 °C

This is useful if the room is initially thermally nonuniform.

6. Initial Pressure Field

Pressure is represented as:

$$ \boxed{ P=P(x,y,z,t) } $$

At \(t=0\):

$$ \boxed{ P(x,y,z,0)=P_0(x,y,z) } $$

For a uniform initial pressure:

$$ \boxed{ P_{ijk}^{0}=P_0 } $$

For example:

$$ P_0=101325\;Pa $$
7. Absolute and Gauge Pressure

This distinction is critical.

The thermodynamic equations require absolute pressure:

$$ \boxed{ P_{abs}>0 } $$

Gauge pressure is:

$$ \boxed{ P_g=P_{abs}-P_{atm} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ P_{abs}=P_g+P_{atm} } $$

For example, if:

$$ P_g=500\,Pa $$

and:

$$ P_{atm}=101325\,Pa $$

then:

$$ P_{abs}=101825\,Pa $$

The numerical solver should store:

$$ \boxed{P=P_{abs}} $$

while the dashboard can display both absolute and gauge pressure.

8. Atmospheric Pressure

If atmospheric pressure is not explicitly supplied, it may be calculated approximately from elevation.

For an atmosphere model:

$$ \boxed{ P_{atm}=P_{atm}(z_{elev}) } $$

For a standard atmosphere in the troposphere, one common formulation is:

$$ \boxed{ P_{atm} = P_{ref} \left( 1-\frac{Lh}{T_{ref}} \right)^{\frac{gM}{RL}} } $$

where:

\(h\) = elevation
\(L\) = lapse rate
\(T_{ref}\) = reference temperature
\(M\) = molar mass of air
\(R\) = universal gas constant.

For a relatively small cold-storage room, atmospheric pressure can generally be treated as spatially uniform initially.

9. Initial Velocity Field

The velocity field is:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u=(u,v,w) } $$

At \(t=0\):

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u(x,y,z,0) = \mathbf u_0(x,y,z) } $$

For a stationary room:

$$ \boxed{ u_0=v_0=w_0=0 } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ |\mathbf u_0|=0 } $$
10. Initial Velocity Magnitude

Velocity magnitude is:

$$ \boxed{ V_0 = \sqrt{ u_0^2+v_0^2+w_0^2 } } $$

For each cell:

$$ \boxed{ V_{ijk}^{0} = \sqrt{ (u_{ijk}^{0})^2+ (v_{ijk}^{0})^2+ (w_{ijk}^{0})^2 } } $$
11. Initial Airflow in a Specified Direction

If the initial velocity magnitude is \(V_0\) and the direction is given by the unit vector:

$$ \hat{\mathbf d} = (d_x,d_y,d_z) $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ d_x^2+d_y^2+d_z^2=1 } $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u_0=V_0\hat{\mathbf d} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ u_0=V_0d_x } $$ $$ \boxed{ v_0=V_0d_y } $$ $$ \boxed{ w_0=V_0d_z } $$
12. Direction From Two Points

If airflow is specified from:

$$ \mathbf x_1=(x_1,y_1,z_1) $$

towards:

$$ \mathbf x_2=(x_2,y_2,z_2) $$

then:

$$ \mathbf d= \mathbf x_2-\mathbf x_1 $$

or:

$$ \mathbf d= (x_2-x_1,\;y_2-y_1,\;z_2-z_1) $$

Normalize:

$$ \boxed{ \hat{\mathbf d} = \frac{\mathbf d}{|\mathbf d|} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u_0=V_0\hat{\mathbf d} } $$
13. Initial Humidity Specification

The user should be able to specify initial moisture using one of:

Relative humidity
Humidity ratio
Specific humidity
Vapour pressure
Dew-point temperature

The numerical model should convert the selected input into its primary moisture variable:

$$ \boxed{\omega_0} $$

For the proposed model, humidity ratio \(\omega\) should be the transported moisture variable.

14. Saturation Vapour Pressure

At the initial temperature:

$$ \boxed{ p_{ws,0}=p_{ws}(T_0) } $$

This is the saturation vapour pressure at the initial temperature.

For a cold-storage model, the saturation-pressure correlation should account for temperatures below freezing, where saturation over ice can become important.

15. Initial Relative Humidity

If initial RH is specified:

$$ \boxed{ RH_0=\phi_0 } $$

where:

$$ 0\leq\phi_0\leq1 $$

or:

$$ 0\leq RH_0\leq100\% $$

The initial vapour pressure is:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,0} = \phi_0p_{ws}(T_0) } $$

If RH is supplied as percentage:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,0} = \frac{RH_0}{100} p_{ws}(T_0) } $$
16. Initial Dry-Air Partial Pressure

Once \(P_0\) and \(p_{v,0}\) are known:

$$ \boxed{ p_{da,0} = P_0-p_{v,0} } $$
17. Initial Humidity Ratio

The humidity ratio is:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_0 = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,0}} {P_0-p_{v,0}} } $$

Substituting:

$$ p_{v,0} = \phi_0p_{ws}(T_0) $$

gives:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_0 = 0.621945 \frac{ \phi_0p_{ws}(T_0) }{ P_0-\phi_0p_{ws}(T_0) } } $$

This is one of the most important initialization equations.

18. If Humidity Ratio Is Directly Specified

If the user directly provides:

$$ \omega_0 $$

then calculate vapour pressure:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,0} = \frac{\omega_0P_0} {0.621945+\omega_0} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ RH_0= \frac{p_{v,0}} {p_{ws}(T_0)} } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ RH_0(\%) = 100 \frac{p_{v,0}} {p_{ws}(T_0)} } $$
19. If Specific Humidity Is Specified

Specific humidity is:

$$ q= \frac{m_v} {m_{da}+m_v} $$

Relationship with humidity ratio:

$$ \boxed{ q= \frac{\omega}{1+\omega} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \omega= \frac{q}{1-q} } $$

Thus if \(q_0\) is supplied:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_0= \frac{q_0}{1-q_0} } $$
20. If Vapour Pressure Is Specified

If:

$$ p_{v,0} $$

is given directly:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_0= 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,0}} {P_0-p_{v,0}} } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ RH_0= \frac{p_{v,0}} {p_{ws}(T_0)} } $$
21. If Dew Point Is Specified

If the initial dew point is:

$$ T_{dp,0} $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,0} = p_{ws}(T_{dp,0}) } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_0= 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,0}} {P_0-p_{v,0}} } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ RH_0= \frac{p_{v,0}} {p_{ws}(T_0)} } $$

This gives a complete moisture state from dew point.

22. Initial Dew-Point Temperature

If \(p_{v,0}\) is known:

$$ \boxed{ p_{ws}(T_{dp,0})=p_{v,0} } $$

Therefore \(T_{dp,0}\) is obtained by solving:

$$ \boxed{ f(T_{dp,0}) = p_{ws}(T_{dp,0})-p_{v,0}=0 } $$

using a numerical root finder such as bisection or Newton-Raphson.

23. Initial Wet-Bulb Temperature

The wet-bulb temperature is obtained from the psychrometric energy/mass-transfer relationship.

Conceptually:

$$ \boxed{ f(T_{wb,0},T_0,P_0,\omega_0)=0 } $$

The implementation should solve this equation numerically.

\(T_{wb}\) is therefore a derived initial property, not an independent primary field.

24. Initial Enthalpy

For moist air:

$$ \boxed{ h_0 = 1.006T_0 + \omega_0(2501+1.86T_0) } $$

with:

\(T_0\) in °C
\(h_0\) in kJ/kg dry air.

For every cell:

$$ \boxed{ h_{ijk}^{0} = 1.006T_{ijk}^{0} + \omega_{ijk}^{0} (2501+1.86T_{ijk}^{0}) } $$
25. Initial Specific Volume

Using moist-air properties:

$$ \boxed{ v_0 = \frac{ R_{da}T_{K,0} (1+1.6078\omega_0) } {P_0} } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ T_{K,0}=T_0+273.15 } $$

Thus:

$$ \boxed{ v_{ijk}^{0} = \frac{ R_{da}T_{K,ijk}^{0} (1+1.6078\omega_{ijk}^{0}) } {P_{ijk}^{0}} } $$
26. Initial Dry-Air Density
$$ \boxed{ \rho_{da,0} = \frac{1} {v_0} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{da,0} = \frac{ P_0 } { R_{da}T_{K,0} (1+1.6078\omega_0) } } $$
27. Initial Moist-Air Density

Using:

$$ \rho_{ma} = \rho_{da}(1+\omega) $$

we obtain:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{ma,0} = \rho_{da,0}(1+\omega_0) } $$

This density is important because it is used in:

mass conservation
momentum equations
airflow
mass flow rate
injection
leakage
heat transport.
28. Initial Vapour Density

The vapour component can be approximated using:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{v,0} = \frac{p_{v,0}} {R_vT_{K,0}} } $$

where:

$$ R_v\approx461.5\;J/(kg\,K) $$
29. Initial Dry-Air Mass

For a cell:

$$ \boxed{ m_{da,ijk}^{0} = \rho_{da,ijk}^{0} V_{ijk} } $$

Total dry-air mass:

$$ \boxed{ M_{da}^{0} = \sum_{i,j,k} \rho_{da,ijk}^{0} V_{ijk} } $$

For a uniform room:

$$ \boxed{ M_{da}^{0} = \rho_{da,0}V_{room} } $$
30. Initial Water-Vapour Mass

The water-vapour mass in a cell is:

$$ \boxed{ m_{v,ijk}^{0} = \omega_{ijk}^{0} m_{da,ijk}^{0} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ m_{v,ijk}^{0} = \omega_{ijk}^{0} \rho_{da,ijk}^{0} V_{ijk} } $$

Total water vapour:

$$ \boxed{ M_v^{0} = \sum_{i,j,k} \omega_{ijk}^{0} \rho_{da,ijk}^{0} V_{ijk} } $$
31. Initial Total Moist-Air Mass

Since:

$$ m_{ma}=m_{da}+m_v $$

we have:

$$ \boxed{ m_{ma,ijk}^{0} = \rho_{da,ijk}^{0} (1+\omega_{ijk}^{0}) V_{ijk} } $$

Total mass:

$$ \boxed{ M_{ma}^{0} = \sum_{i,j,k} m_{ma,ijk}^{0} } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ M_{ma}^{0} = \sum_{i,j,k} \rho_{ma,ijk}^{0} V_{ijk} } $$
32. Initial Sensible Energy

For the numerical model, define a consistent reference temperature \(T_{ref}\).

Then sensible energy in a cell can be represented as:

$$ \boxed{ E_{s,ijk}^{0} = m_{ma,ijk}^{0} c_p (T_{ijk}^{0}-T_{ref}) } $$

If the reference is \(0^\circ C\):

$$ \boxed{ E_{s,ijk}^{0} = m_{ma,ijk}^{0}c_pT_{ijk}^{0} } $$

The exact energy reference must remain consistent throughout the simulation.

33. Initial Latent Energy

Using a reference state, a simplified latent-energy representation is:

$$ \boxed{ E_{l,ijk}^{0} = m_{v,ijk}^{0}L_v } $$

where:

$$ L_v $$

is the latent heat of vaporization/sublimation appropriate to the thermodynamic regime.

For a high-fidelity cold-storage model, the phase-dependent treatment of water/ice should be handled consistently.

34. Initial Total Energy

Then:

$$ \boxed{ E_{total,ijk}^{0} = E_{s,ijk}^{0} + E_{l,ijk}^{0} } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ E_{total}^{0} = \sum_{i,j,k} E_{total,ijk}^{0} } $$

However, for the eventual conservation equation, it is better to establish one consistent thermodynamic energy/enthalpy reference rather than independently mixing arbitrary sensible and latent reference energies.

35. Initial Kinetic Energy

If airflow initially exists:

$$ \boxed{ E_{kin,ijk}^{0} = \frac12 m_{ijk}^{0} |\mathbf u_{ijk}^{0}|^2 } $$

where:

$$ |\mathbf u|^2=u^2+v^2+w^2 $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ E_{kin,ijk}^{0} = \frac12 m_{ijk}^{0} (u_{ijk}^{0\,2} + v_{ijk}^{0\,2} + w_{ijk}^{0\,2}) } $$

For a stationary room:

$$ E_{kin}^{0}=0 $$
36. Initial Gravitational Potential Energy

If gravitational potential energy is required:

$$ \boxed{ E_{grav,ijk}^{0} = m_{ijk}^{0}gz_{ijk} } $$

The zero of gravitational potential can be chosen at:

$$ z=0 $$

so:

$$ E_{grav}=mgz $$

This is generally not necessary for the cold-storage thermal energy balance, but may be relevant in a complete energy accounting.

37. Initial State From Temperature + RH + Pressure

This is likely to be the default initialization mode in your dashboard.

The user enters:

$$ T_0,\quad RH_0,\quad P_0 $$

Then:

Step 1
$$ p_{ws,0}=p_{ws}(T_0) $$
Step 2
$$ p_{v,0} = \frac{RH_0}{100}p_{ws,0} $$
Step 3
$$ p_{da,0}=P_0-p_{v,0} $$
Step 4
$$ \boxed{ \omega_0 = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,0}} {P_0-p_{v,0}} } $$
Step 5

Calculate:

$$ T_{dp,0} $$ $$ T_{wb,0} $$
Step 6

Calculate:

$$ h_0 $$ $$ v_0 $$ $$ \rho_0 $$
Step 7

Initialize the entire domain.

Thus:

$$ \boxed{ (T_0,RH_0,P_0) \rightarrow \omega_0 \rightarrow \text{complete psychrometric state} } $$
38. Spatially Varying Initial RH

Just as temperature can vary:

$$ \boxed{ RH_0=RH_0(x,y,z) } $$

Then:

$$ p_{v,0}(x,y,z) = \frac{RH_0(x,y,z)}{100} p_{ws}[T_0(x,y,z)] $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_0(x,y,z) = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,0}(x,y,z)} {P_0(x,y,z)-p_{v,0}(x,y,z)} } $$

This is important if different sections of the cold storage initially have different humidity.

39. Spatially Varying Pressure

The model can initialize:

$$ \boxed{ P_0=P_0(x,y,z) } $$

For example, if a prescribed pressure gradient exists:

$$ \boxed{ P_0(z) = P_{ref} - \rho gz } $$

for hydrostatic pressure variation.

For a room of only a few metres, this variation is relatively small, but it becomes relevant when pressure-driven flow is later introduced.

40. Hydrostatic Pressure

For a fluid at rest:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{dP}{dz}=-\rho g } $$

If density is approximately constant:

$$ \boxed{ P(z)=P_0-\rho gz } $$

depending on the chosen vertical coordinate and reference pressure.

More generally:

$$ \boxed{ P(z)=P(z_0)-\int_{z_0}^{z}\rho(\zeta)g\,d\zeta } $$

This provides an optional physically consistent initial pressure field.

41. Initial Buoyancy State

If temperature varies vertically, density also varies:

$$ \rho_0=\rho(T_0,P_0,\omega_0) $$

and therefore buoyancy can develop after initialization.

The gravitational body force is:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf F_g=\rho_0\mathbf g } $$

with:

$$ \mathbf g=(0,0,-g) $$

for the conventional \(+z\) upward coordinate.

42. Boussinesq Initial Density Approximation

If the low-Mach buoyancy model uses the Boussinesq approximation:

$$ \boxed{ \rho = \rho_{ref} [ 1-\beta(T-T_{ref}) ] } $$

where:

$$ \beta = -\frac{1}{\rho} \left( \frac{\partial\rho}{\partial T} \right)_P $$

For an ideal gas approximation:

$$ \boxed{ \beta\approx\frac1{T_{ref,K}} } $$

This should only be used when the Boussinesq assumptions are appropriate.

43. Initial Boundary State vs Initial Interior State

It is important to distinguish:

Initial condition
$$ \boxed{ \phi(\mathbf x,0)=\phi_0(\mathbf x) } $$

from:

Boundary condition
$$ \boxed{ \phi(\mathbf x,t)=\phi_b(\mathbf x,t), \qquad \mathbf x\in\partial\Omega } $$

For example:

$$ T(x,y,z,0)=-18^\circ C $$

is an initial condition.

But:

$$ T|_{\text{wall}}=-20^\circ C $$

is a boundary condition.

Module 2 should only define the initial state. Boundary conditions belong to later modules.

44. Initial Conditions for Obstacles

If Module 1 contains solid/product regions, their initial temperature can be independently specified.

For a solid region:

$$ \boxed{ T_s(x,y,z,0)=T_{s,0}(x,y,z) } $$

For example:

$$ T_{product,0}=-10^\circ C $$

while:

$$ T_{air,0}=-18^\circ C $$

This creates an initial thermal load that can subsequently transfer heat to the air.

45. Product Initial Energy

For a product mass \(m_p\):

$$ \boxed{ E_{p,0} = m_pc_{p,p}(T_{p,0}-T_{ref}) } $$

If phase change/freezing occurs, latent energy can be included:

$$ \boxed{ E_{p,total} = E_{p,sensible} + E_{p,latent} } $$

This becomes relevant if the cold-storage model is eventually extended to cooling/freezing of stored products.

46. Initial Refrigeration Equipment State

If a cooling unit is represented as an initial source/sink:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{cool,0} } $$

can be assigned.

The corresponding volumetric energy sink is:

$$ \boxed{ S_{Q,cool,0} = -\frac{\dot Q_{cool,0}} {V_{cool}} } $$

However, this is technically a source/boundary condition, not an initial condition. It belongs to the subsequent heat-source/boundary module.

47. Initial Moisture Condensation Check

Immediately after initialization, the model must check:

$$ \boxed{ RH_0>100\% } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_0>\omega_{s,0} } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{s,0} = 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws}(T_0)} {P_0-p_{ws}(T_0)} } $$

If:

$$ \omega_0>\omega_{s,0} $$

the initial state is supersaturated and must be handled by the condensation model.

For a physically consistent initialization without immediate condensation:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_0\leq\omega_{s,0} } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ RH_0\leq100\% } $$
48. Initial Moisture Margin to Saturation

A useful diagnostic is:

$$ \boxed{ M_{\omega,0} = \omega_{s,0}-\omega_0 } $$

If:

$$ M_{\omega,0}>0 $$

the air is below saturation.

If:

$$ M_{\omega,0}=0 $$

the air is saturated.

If:

$$ M_{\omega,0}<0 $$

the air is supersaturated.

49. Initial Relative-Humidity Constraint

The initial RH should satisfy:

$$ \boxed{ 0\leq RH_0\leq100\% } $$

for a single-phase equilibrium initial condition.

Values above 100% may represent a transient supersaturated state, but then the condensation model must immediately act on that state.

50. Initial Pressure Constraint

Absolute pressure must satisfy:

$$ \boxed{ P_0>p_{v,0}>0 } $$

because:

$$ p_{da,0}=P_0-p_{v,0}>0 $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ 0<p_{v,0}<P_0 } $$
51. Initial Humidity-Ratio Constraint

The humidity ratio must satisfy:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_0\geq0 } $$

For ordinary atmospheric moist air:

$$ \omega_0\ll1 $$

typically expressed in:

$$ kg_{water}/kg_{dry\,air} $$
52. Initial Velocity Constraint

If the room is initially stationary:

$$ \boxed{ u_0=v_0=w_0=0 } $$

If fans are operating:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u_0\neq0 } $$

and the prescribed initial velocity must be physically consistent with the fan/inlet boundary conditions that will be imposed afterward.

53. Initial Reynolds Number

An optional initial diagnostic is:

$$ \boxed{ Re_0= \frac{\rho_0V_0L_c} {\mu_0} } $$

where:

\(L_c\) = characteristic length
\(\mu_0\) = dynamic viscosity.

For a cold-storage room, \(L_c\) may be chosen as the room characteristic dimension or an appropriate inlet/hydraulic length depending on what flow regime is being assessed.

54. Initial Prandtl Number
$$ \boxed{ Pr_0= \frac{\mu_0c_p} {k_0} } $$

This is a useful dimensionless diagnostic for thermal transport.

55. Initial Schmidt Number

For moisture transport:

$$ \boxed{ Sc_0= \frac{\mu_0} {\rho_0D_{eff,0}} } $$

where:

$$ D_{eff,0} $$

is the effective moisture diffusivity.

56. Initial Peclet Numbers

Thermal Peclet number:

$$ \boxed{ Pe_{T,0} = \frac{V_0L_c} {\alpha_0} } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ \alpha_0= \frac{k_0} {\rho_0c_p} } $$

Moisture Peclet number:

$$ \boxed{ Pe_{m,0} = \frac{V_0L_c} {D_{eff,0}} } $$

These indicate the relative importance of advection versus diffusion.

57. Initial State Initialization Algorithm

For the default case:

$$ \boxed{ T_0,\;RH_0,\;P_0,\;\mathbf u_0 } $$

the initialization procedure is:

Room Geometry
      ↓
Mesh from Module 1
      ↓
Assign T0(x,y,z)
      ↓
Assign P0(x,y,z)
      ↓
Assign RH0(x,y,z)
      ↓
Calculate pws(T0)
      ↓
Calculate pv
      ↓
Calculate pda
      ↓
Calculate ω0
      ↓
Calculate q0
      ↓
Calculate Tdp,0
      ↓
Calculate Twb,0
      ↓
Calculate h0
      ↓
Calculate specific volume
      ↓
Calculate density
      ↓
Calculate initial mass
      ↓
Calculate initial energy
      ↓
Check RH / saturation
      ↓
Check physical validity
      ↓
Create Q0[i,j,k]
58. Complete Initial-State Formula Set

For every computational cell \((i,j,k)\):

Primary variables
$$ \boxed{ T_{ijk}^{0} } $$ $$ \boxed{ P_{ijk}^{0} } $$ $$ \boxed{ u_{ijk}^{0},\; v_{ijk}^{0},\; w_{ijk}^{0} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \omega_{ijk}^{0} } $$
Derived psychrometric state
$$ \boxed{ T_{K,ijk}^{0}=T_{ijk}^{0}+273.15 } $$ $$ \boxed{ p_{ws,ijk}^{0} = p_{ws}(T_{ijk}^{0}) } $$ $$ \boxed{ p_{v,ijk}^{0} = \frac{\omega_{ijk}^{0}P_{ijk}^{0}} {0.621945+\omega_{ijk}^{0}} } $$ $$ \boxed{ p_{da,ijk}^{0} = P_{ijk}^{0}-p_{v,ijk}^{0} } $$ $$ \boxed{ RH_{ijk}^{0} = \frac{p_{v,ijk}^{0}} {p_{ws,ijk}^{0}} } $$ $$ \boxed{ q_{ijk}^{0} = \frac{\omega_{ijk}^{0}} {1+\omega_{ijk}^{0}} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \omega_{s,ijk}^{0} = 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws,ijk}^{0}} {P_{ijk}^{0}-p_{ws,ijk}^{0}} } $$ $$ \boxed{ p_{v,ijk}^{0} = p_{ws}(T_{dp,ijk}^{0}) } $$ $$ \boxed{ f(T_{wb,ijk}^{0})=0 } $$ $$ \boxed{ h_{ijk}^{0} = 1.006T_{ijk}^{0} + \omega_{ijk}^{0} (2501+1.86T_{ijk}^{0}) } $$ $$ \boxed{ v_{ijk}^{0} = \frac{ R_{da}T_{K,ijk}^{0} (1+1.6078\omega_{ijk}^{0}) } {P_{ijk}^{0}} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \rho_{da,ijk}^{0} = \frac1{v_{ijk}^{0}} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \rho_{ma,ijk}^{0} = \rho_{da,ijk}^{0} (1+\omega_{ijk}^{0}) } $$ $$ \boxed{ V_{ijk}^{0} = \sqrt{ (u_{ijk}^{0})^2+ (v_{ijk}^{0})^2+ (w_{ijk}^{0})^2 } } $$
59. Complete Cell State After Module 2

At the end of Module 2, every computational cell should have:

$$ \boxed{ \mathcal S_{ijk}^{0} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} x_i,y_j,z_k\\ V_{ijk}\\ T_{ijk}^{0}\\ P_{ijk}^{0}\\ u_{ijk}^{0},v_{ijk}^{0},w_{ijk}^{0}\\ \omega_{ijk}^{0}\\ RH_{ijk}^{0}\\ p_{v,ijk}^{0}\\ p_{da,ijk}^{0}\\ p_{ws,ijk}^{0}\\ \omega_{s,ijk}^{0}\\ q_{ijk}^{0}\\ T_{dp,ijk}^{0}\\ T_{wb,ijk}^{0}\\ h_{ijk}^{0}\\ v_{ijk}^{0}\\ \rho_{ijk}^{0}\\ m_{ijk}^{0}\\ E_{ijk}^{0} \end{array} \right] } $$

This is the initial digital representation of the cold-storage room.

60. Most Important Implementation Formula Chain

For your actual project, the most important initialization chain is:

$$ \boxed{ T_0,\;P_0,\;RH_0 } $$ $$ \Downarrow $$ $$ \boxed{ p_{ws,0}=p_{ws}(T_0) } $$ $$ \Downarrow $$ $$ \boxed{ p_{v,0} = \frac{RH_0}{100}p_{ws,0} } $$ $$ \Downarrow $$ $$ \boxed{ \omega_0 = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,0}} {P_0-p_{v,0}} } $$ $$ \Downarrow $$ $$ \boxed{ q_0=\frac{\omega_0}{1+\omega_0} } $$ $$ \Downarrow $$ $$ \boxed{ h_0 = 1.006T_0+ \omega_0(2501+1.86T_0) } $$ $$ \Downarrow $$ $$ \boxed{ v_0= \frac{R_{da}T_{K,0}(1+1.6078\omega_0)} {P_0} } $$ $$ \Downarrow $$ $$ \boxed{ \rho_0=\frac1{v_0} } $$ $$ \Downarrow $$ $$ \boxed{ T_{dp,0},T_{wb,0},E_{s,0},E_{l,0},E_{total,0} } $$ $$ \Downarrow $$ $$ \boxed{ \mathbf Q_{ijk}^{0} } $$

This is the state that will be passed from Module 2 → Module 3.

61. Module 2 Deliverables

After completing Module 2, the code should be capable of producing:

Input
$$ \boxed{ T_0,\;P_0,\;RH_0,\;\mathbf u_0 } $$

or equivalent moisture specifications.

Output for every cell
$$ \boxed{ T,P,\mathbf u,\omega } $$

plus:

$$ \boxed{ RH,p_v,p_{da},p_{ws},\omega_s,q,T_{dp},T_{wb},h,v,\rho } $$

and initial:

$$ \boxed{ m,\;E_s,\;E_l,\;E_{total} } $$
Validation

The initialization module must verify:

$$ P>0 $$ $$ 0\le RH\le100\% $$ $$ \omega\ge0 $$ $$ p_v<P $$ $$ V_{mesh}=V_{room} $$

and, for a non-supersaturated initial state:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_0\le\omega_s(T_0,P_0) } $$
Final relationship between Modules 1 and 2
$$ \boxed{ \underbrace{ L_x,L_y,L_z,N_x,N_y,N_z }_{\text{Module 1}} \rightarrow \underbrace{ x_i,y_j,z_k,V_{ijk},A_f }_{\text{Mesh}} } $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ \underbrace{ T_0,P_0,RH_0,\mathbf u_0 }_{\text{Module 2 inputs}} \rightarrow \underbrace{ T,P,\omega,\mathbf u }_{\text{Primary fields}} \rightarrow \underbrace{ RH,p_v,T_{dp},T_{wb},h,\rho,v,\ldots }_{\text{Complete initial psychrometric state}} } $$

So, Module 2 does not yet advance the solution in time. It creates the physically consistent \(t=0\) state. The actual evolution,

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf Q^0\rightarrow\mathbf Q^1\rightarrow\mathbf Q^2\rightarrow\cdots } $$

starts in the subsequent numerical-solver modules.













Module 3 — Psychrometric Engine

Module 3 is the thermodynamic property engine of the numerical cold-storage model.

Its job is not to solve the temperature, pressure, airflow, or moisture transport equations. Instead, it takes the primary state variables calculated by Modules 1–2 and by the subsequent numerical solver and converts them into the complete local psychrometric state.

The central relationship is:

$$ \boxed{ (T,P,\omega) \longrightarrow \left[ p_{ws},p_v,p_{da},RH,\omega_s,q,T_{dp},T_{wb}, h,v,\rho,\rho_{da},\rho_v,\ldots \right] } $$

At every computational cell and every time step:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf{x}_{ijk},t_n \rightarrow \text{complete psychrometric state} } $$

This is particularly important for your project because temperature, moisture, pressure, injection and leakage are coupled through psychrometric properties.

1. Role of Module 3 in the Overall Numerical Model

The overall architecture becomes:

MODULE 1
Geometry & Mesh
       │
       ▼
(x,y,z), Vcell, Af, normals
       │
       ▼
MODULE 2
Initial Conditions
       │
       ▼
T₀, P₀, ω₀, u₀,v₀,w₀
       │
       ▼
MODULE 3
PSYCHROMETRIC ENGINE
       │
       ├──→ RH
       ├──→ Vapour pressure
       ├──→ Dew point
       ├──→ Wet bulb
       ├──→ Enthalpy
       ├──→ Density
       ├──→ Specific volume
       ├──→ Sensible/latent properties
       └──→ Saturation properties
       │
       ▼
MODULE 4+
Numerical Transport
       │
       ├── Temperature
       ├── Pressure
       ├── Airflow
       ├── Moisture
       ├── Condensation
       ├── Injection
       └── Leakage
       │
       ▼
Updated T, P, ω
       │
       └──────────────→ Module 3 again

Therefore Module 3 operates continuously during the simulation, not just at \(t=0\).

2. Primary State Variables

The recommended primary thermodynamic variables are:

$$ \boxed{ T,\quad P,\quad\omega } $$

where:

\(T\) = dry-bulb air temperature
\(P\) = absolute pressure
\(\omega\) = humidity ratio

Velocity:

$$ \mathbf u=(u,v,w) $$

is handled by the airflow solver, but its magnitude can be passed to the psychrometric/dashboard layer.

The reason for using \(T,P,\omega\) is that they provide a convenient complete thermodynamic state for moist air.

3. Unit Convention

The internal numerical implementation should use:

$$ \boxed{ T_K=T_{^\circ C}+273.15 } $$ $$ \boxed{ P=P_{abs}\quad [Pa] } $$ $$ \boxed{ \omega = kg_{water}/kg_{dry\,air} } $$

Temperature may be displayed in °C on the dashboard, but Kelvin should be used where required by thermodynamic equations.

Pressure should be stored internally as absolute pressure.

4. Fundamental Moist-Air Composition

Moist air is treated as a mixture of:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Dry air}+\text{water vapour} } $$

Total pressure is:

$$ \boxed{ P=p_{da}+p_v } $$

where:

\(p_{da}\) = dry-air partial pressure
\(p_v\) = water-vapour partial pressure.

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ p_{da}=P-p_v } $$

This is one of the fundamental equations of the entire psychrometric engine.

5. Humidity Ratio

Humidity ratio is defined as:

$$ \boxed{ \omega= \frac{m_v}{m_{da}} } $$

where:

\(m_v\) = mass of water vapour
\(m_{da}\) = mass of dry air.

Using partial pressures:

$$ \boxed{ \omega = 0.621945 \frac{p_v} {P-p_v} } $$

The factor:

$$ 0.621945 $$

is approximately:

$$ \frac{M_v}{M_{da}} $$

where \(M_v\) and \(M_{da}\) are the molecular masses of water vapour and dry air.

6. Vapour Pressure From Humidity Ratio

Rearranging the previous equation:

$$ \omega= 0.621945 \frac{p_v}{P-p_v} $$

gives:

$$ \boxed{ p_v = \frac{\omega P} {0.621945+\omega} } $$

This equation is extremely important because the numerical moisture solver will generally transport \(\omega\), while the psychrometric engine needs \(p_v\).

7. Dry-Air Partial Pressure

Once \(p_v\) is known:

$$ \boxed{ p_{da}=P-p_v } $$

Substituting the humidity-ratio relationship:

$$ \boxed{ p_{da} = \frac{0.621945P} {0.621945+\omega} } $$
8. Saturation Vapour Pressure

The saturation vapour pressure is:

$$ \boxed{ p_{ws}=p_{ws}(T) } $$

This is the pressure of water vapour when air is saturated at temperature \(T\).

For your cold-storage application this is particularly important because the temperature can be below \(0^\circ C\).

Therefore the implementation should distinguish between:

$$ \boxed{ p_{ws,w}(T) } $$

saturation over liquid water, and:

$$ \boxed{ p_{ws,i}(T) } $$

saturation over ice.

The appropriate formulation depends on the thermodynamic regime.

For research-grade implementation, use a validated water/ice saturation-pressure formulation rather than permanently relying on a simple Magnus equation.

9. Saturation Humidity Ratio

Once \(p_{ws}\) is known:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_s = 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws}} {P-p_{ws}} } $$

This represents the maximum equilibrium humidity ratio at the given:

$$ T,P $$
10. Relative Humidity

Relative humidity is:

$$ \boxed{ \phi = \frac{p_v}{p_{ws}} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ RH(\%)= 100\frac{p_v}{p_{ws}} } $$

Substituting the humidity-ratio equation:

$$ \boxed{ RH = 100 \frac{ \omega P }{ (0.621945+\omega)p_{ws} } } $$

This equation means that RH is not an independent transported field in your model.

It is derived from:

$$ \boxed{ T,P,\omega } $$
11. Relative Humidity Interpretation

The psychrometric engine should classify the state:

Unsaturated
$$ \boxed{ RH<100\% } $$
Saturated
$$ \boxed{ RH=100\% } $$
Supersaturated
$$ \boxed{ RH>100\% } $$

For normal equilibrium moist air, the last state should trigger condensation treatment.

12. Saturation Ratio

Define:

$$ \boxed{ S= \frac{p_v}{p_{ws}} } $$

Since:

$$ RH=100S $$

we have:

$$ \boxed{ S=\frac{RH}{100} } $$

This is useful internally for detecting saturation.

13. Moisture Deficit

Define the saturation moisture deficit:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta\omega = \omega_s-\omega } $$

Interpretation:

$$ \Delta\omega>0 $$

→ air can potentially hold additional moisture.

$$ \Delta\omega=0 $$

→ saturated.

$$ \Delta\omega<0 $$

→ supersaturated.

This quantity is useful to the condensation module.

14. Moisture Excess

Alternatively:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{excess} = \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$

The condensation module can use this quantity.

15. Specific Humidity

Specific humidity is:

$$ \boxed{ q= \frac{m_v} {m_{da}+m_v} } $$

Using humidity ratio:

$$ \boxed{ q= \frac{\omega}{1+\omega} } $$

Conversely:

$$ \boxed{ \omega= \frac{q}{1-q} } $$
16. Vapour Mass Fraction

For the moist-air mixture:

$$ \boxed{ Y_v=q } $$

and dry-air mass fraction:

$$ \boxed{ Y_{da} = 1-q } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ Y_{da}+Y_v=1 } $$
17. Dew-Point Temperature

The dew point \(T_{dp}\) is defined by:

$$ \boxed{ p_{ws}(T_{dp})=p_v } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ f(T_{dp}) = p_{ws}(T_{dp})-p_v=0 } $$

The numerical engine solves this equation.

A root-finding procedure can be:

$$ T_{dp}^{(n+1)} = T_{dp}^{(n)} - \frac{ f(T_{dp}^{(n)}) }{ f'(T_{dp}^{(n)}) } $$

for Newton-Raphson, or a bracketed method such as bisection.

18. Dew-Point Interpretation

The dew point tells us:

At what temperature would the current water-vapour content reach saturation?

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ T<T_{dp} \Rightarrow \text{condensation tendency} } $$

while:

$$ \boxed{ T>T_{dp} \Rightarrow \text{unsaturated} } $$

subject to the appropriate water/ice phase treatment.

19. Wet-Bulb Temperature
