Wet-bulb temperature \(T_{wb}\) is another derived psychrometric property.

It should be obtained from the thermodynamic wet-bulb relation.

A general root formulation is:

$$ \boxed{ f(T_{wb},T,P,\omega)=0 } $$

The solver finds:

$$ \boxed{ T_{wb} } $$

numerically.

The psychrometric engine should therefore treat:

$$ T_{wb} $$

as a derived property, not a transported variable.

20. Moist-Air Enthalpy

A practical engineering approximation is:

$$ \boxed{ h= 1.006T+ \omega(2501+1.86T) } $$

where:

\(T\) = °C
\(h\) = kJ/kg dry air.

Separate terms:

$$ \boxed{ h_{da}=1.006T } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ h_v= \omega(2501+1.86T) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ h=h_{da}+h_v } $$
21. Enthalpy Difference

Between two states:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta h=h_2-h_1 } $$

This is critical for:

air injection
leakage
mixing
heat loads
refrigeration calculations.

For an air stream:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{air} = \dot m_{da} (h_{in}-h_{room}) } $$

This is one of the most useful connections between Module 3 and the injection/leakage modules.

22. Sensible Enthalpy Component

The sensible contribution is approximately:

$$ \boxed{ h_s=c_{p,da}T } $$

or using the engineering approximation:

$$ \boxed{ h_s\approx1.006T } $$
23. Latent Enthalpy Component

The water-vapour contribution is:

$$ \boxed{ h_l = \omega(2501+1.86T) } $$

A simplified latent component is:

$$ \boxed{ h_{latent} \approx \omega L_v } $$

where \(L_v\) is the appropriate latent heat.

24. Total Enthalpy
$$ \boxed{ h_{total} = h_{sensible}+h_{latent} } $$

The exact energy reference should be kept consistent across the entire numerical model.

25. Specific Volume of Moist Air

The specific volume per unit mass of dry air is:

$$ \boxed{ v = \frac{ R_{da}T_K (1+1.6078\omega) } {P} } $$

where:

$$ T_K=T+273.15 $$

and:

$$ R_{da}\approx287.05 \;J/(kg\,K) $$
26. Moist-Air Density

The corresponding density on a dry-air mass basis is:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{da} = \frac1v } $$

therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{da} = \frac{ P }{ R_{da}T_K(1+1.6078\omega) } } $$
27. Total Moist-Air Density

Since:

$$ m_{ma}=m_{da}+m_v $$

and:

$$ m_v=\omega m_{da} $$

then:

$$ m_{ma}=m_{da}(1+\omega) $$

Thus:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{ma} = \rho_{da}(1+\omega) } $$

This distinction should be kept explicit in the code.

28. Vapour Density

Water vapour can be treated approximately as an ideal gas:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_v= \frac{p_v}{R_vT_K} } $$

where:

$$ R_v\approx461.5\;J/(kg\,K) $$
29. Dry-Air Density From Partial Pressure

Alternatively:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{da} = \frac{p_{da}} {R_{da}T_K} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{da} = \frac{P-p_v} {R_{da}T_K} } $$

This provides a useful independent consistency check.

30. Mixture Density From Components

The mixture density can be written:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{ma} = \rho_{da}+\rho_v } $$

under the ideal-gas mixture treatment.

This should approximately agree with the density calculated from the moist-air specific-volume formulation.

31. Humidity Ratio From Density

Since:

$$ \omega= \frac{m_v}{m_{da}} $$

for a common control volume:

$$ \boxed{ \omega = \frac{\rho_v}{\rho_{da}} } $$

This gives another internal consistency check.

32. Partial-Pressure Ratio

From:

$$ \omega= 0.621945 \frac{p_v}{p_{da}} $$

we obtain:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{p_v}{p_{da}} = \frac{\omega}{0.621945} } $$

This is useful for validating the pressure decomposition.

33. Psychrometric State Transformation

The engine should support the following transformations.

Given \(T,P,RH\)
$$ \boxed{ (T,P,RH) \rightarrow p_{ws} \rightarrow p_v \rightarrow \omega } $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ \omega \rightarrow q,h,v,\rho,T_{dp},T_{wb} } $$
Given \(T,P,\omega\)
$$ \boxed{ (T,P,\omega) \rightarrow p_v \rightarrow RH } $$

then all other properties.

Given \(T,P,T_{dp}\)
$$ \boxed{ T_{dp} \rightarrow p_v \rightarrow \omega \rightarrow RH } $$
Given \(T,P,q\)
$$ \boxed{ q \rightarrow \omega \rightarrow p_v \rightarrow RH } $$
34. Psychrometric Engine as a State Function

For each cell:

$$ \boxed{ \mathcal{P} = f(T,P,\omega) } $$

where:

$$ \mathcal{P} = \{ p_{ws}, p_v, p_{da}, RH, \omega_s, q, T_{dp}, T_{wb}, h, v, \rho_{da}, \rho_{ma}, \rho_v \} $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \mathcal P_{ijk}^{n} = f ( T_{ijk}^{n}, P_{ijk}^{n}, \omega_{ijk}^{n} ) } $$

This equation is essentially the heart of Module 3.

35. Psychrometric Engine at Every Time Step

Suppose the numerical solver has advanced from:

$$ t^n $$

to:

$$ t^{n+1} $$

and obtained:

$$ T^{n+1},P^{n+1},\omega^{n+1} $$

Then Module 3 executes:

$$ \boxed{ (T^{n+1},P^{n+1},\omega^{n+1}) \rightarrow \mathcal P^{n+1} } $$

The updated properties are then passed to the next physical calculations.

36. Relationship With Temperature Solver

The temperature solver produces:

$$ \boxed{ T^{n+1} } $$

Module 3 uses it to calculate:

$$ p_{ws}^{n+1} $$ $$ T_{dp}^{n+1} $$ $$ T_{wb}^{n+1} $$

and other properties.

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ T \rightarrow p_{ws} \rightarrow RH,\;T_{dp},\;T_{wb} } $$

Temperature therefore strongly affects moisture behaviour.

37. Relationship With Moisture Solver

The moisture solver produces:

$$ \boxed{ \omega^{n+1} } $$

Module 3 calculates:

$$ p_v^{n+1} = \frac{\omega^{n+1}P^{n+1}} {0.621945+\omega^{n+1}} $$

then:

$$ RH^{n+1} = 100 \frac{p_v^{n+1}} {p_{ws}(T^{n+1})} $$

Thus:

$$ \boxed{ \omega \rightarrow p_v \rightarrow RH } $$
38. Relationship With Pressure Solver

The pressure solver provides:

$$ \boxed{ P(x,y,z,t) } $$

Module 3 uses this to calculate:

$$ \omega \leftrightarrow p_v $$

and:

$$ \rho $$

and:

$$ v $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ P \rightarrow \rho,\;v,\;p_v,\;RH,\;h } $$

Pressure also affects the saturation humidity ratio:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_s = 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws}} {P-p_{ws}} } $$

This means pressure changes can influence the moisture capacity of air.

39. Relationship With Airflow Solver

The airflow solver determines:

$$ \boxed{ u,v,w } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ V=\sqrt{u^2+v^2+w^2} } $$

Module 3 calculates density:

$$ \boxed{ \rho=\rho(T,P,\omega) } $$

The airflow solver then uses density for mass flux:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m=\rho\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf A } $$

Thus:

$$ \boxed{ T,P,\omega \rightarrow \rho \rightarrow \text{airflow/mass flow} } $$

and airflow subsequently transports \(T\) and \(\omega\).

This creates a two-way coupling:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Psychrometrics} \leftrightarrow \text{Airflow} } $$
40. Relationship With Injection

Suppose injection air has:

$$ T_{in},P_{in},RH_{in} $$

Module 3 calculates:

$$ p_{v,in} = \frac{RH_{in}}{100} p_{ws}(T_{in}) $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{in} = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,in}} {P_{in}-p_{v,in}} } $$

then:

$$ h_{in} = 1.006T_{in} + \omega_{in}(2501+1.86T_{in}) $$

and:

$$ \rho_{in} = \rho(T_{in},P_{in},\omega_{in}) $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ (T_{in},P_{in},RH_{in}) \rightarrow (\omega_{in},h_{in},\rho_{in},\ldots) } $$

The injection module then uses these properties to determine mass, moisture and energy source terms.

41. Injection Mass Flow

If:

$$ \dot V_{in} $$

is specified:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{in} = \rho_{in}\dot V_{in} } $$

The dry-air mass flow is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{da,in} = \frac{\dot m_{in}} {1+\omega_{in}} } $$

Water-vapour mass flow:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,in} = \omega_{in}\dot m_{da,in} } $$
42. Injection Moisture Source

For an injection volume \(V_s\):

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega} = \frac{ \dot m_{da,in}\omega_{in} } {V_s} } $$

The actual conservative transport formulation may instead use dry-air mass and moisture fluxes directly through the inlet face.

43. Injection Energy Source

Using enthalpy:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{in} = \dot m_{da,in} h_{in} } $$

For a relative/reference heat load compared with room air:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{mix} = \dot m_{da,in} (h_{in}-h_{room}) } $$

This is preferable to manually separating sensible and latent effects when the full moist-air enthalpy formulation is being used.

44. Relationship With Leakage

For outside air:

$$ T_{out},P_{out},RH_{out} $$

Module 3 calculates:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{out},h_{out},\rho_{out} } $$

The leakage module calculates:

$$ \dot m_{leak} $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,leak} = \dot m_{da,leak}\omega_{out} } $$

and the enthalpy load is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{leak} = \dot m_{da,leak} (h_{out}-h_{room}) } $$

This is why Module 3 is fundamental to realistic infiltration modelling.

45. Relationship With Condensation

This is one of the most important couplings.

Module 3 calculates:

$$ p_v $$

and:

$$ p_{ws} $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ RH= 100\frac{p_v}{p_{ws}} } $$

If:

$$ RH>100\% $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ \omega>\omega_s } $$

then the condensation module is activated.

The excess moisture is:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta\omega_{cond} = \omega-\omega_s } $$

for:

$$ \omega>\omega_s $$
46. Condensation Mass

For a cell volume \(V_c\):

$$ \boxed{ \Delta m_{cond} = \rho_{da} (\omega-\omega_s)V_c } $$

for an equilibrium condensation correction.

A dynamic model can instead define:

$$ \boxed{ S_{cond} = k_{cond}(\omega-\omega_s) } $$

when:

$$ \omega>\omega_s $$

The exact rate law belongs to the condensation module.

47. Condensation Latent Heat

Once:

$$ \dot m_{cond} $$

is known:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{latent} = \dot m_{cond}L } $$

where \(L\) is the appropriate latent heat for the phase transition being modelled.

This energy source is returned to the temperature/energy solver.

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ T,\omega,P \rightarrow \text{Psychrometrics} \rightarrow \text{Condensation} \rightarrow \text{Latent heat} \rightarrow T } $$

This forms a closed thermodynamic feedback loop.

48. Relationship With Heat Transfer

Heat-transfer calculations require:

$$ \rho,\;c_p,\;k $$

and potentially other thermophysical properties.

The psychrometric engine provides:

$$ \boxed{ \rho=\rho(T,P,\omega) } $$

Therefore thermal diffusivity becomes:

$$ \boxed{ \alpha = \frac{k} {\rho c_p} } $$

and the temperature solver uses \(\alpha\).

Hence:

$$ \boxed{ T,P,\omega \rightarrow \rho \rightarrow \alpha \rightarrow \text{temperature evolution} } $$
49. Relationship With Energy Conservation

For a control volume:

$$ E=E(T,\omega,P) $$

The psychrometric engine provides the thermodynamic properties needed to calculate energy.

For example:

$$ \boxed{ h= 1.006T+ \omega(2501+1.86T) } $$

Therefore an incoming/outgoing air stream contributes:

$$ \boxed{ \dot E_{air} = \dot m_{da}h } $$

This is used in the energy conservation equation.

50. Relationship With Density and Mass Conservation

The continuity equation requires:

$$ \boxed{ \rho } $$

The psychrometric engine calculates:

$$ \boxed{ \rho=\rho(T,P,\omega) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Temperature} + \text{Pressure} + \text{Moisture} \rightarrow \text{Density} \rightarrow \text{Mass conservation} } $$

This is why using a fixed air density throughout the simulation would be inconsistent with the fully coupled model.

51. Relationship With Dashboard

The numerical solver should store only the necessary primary variables and required diagnostic fields.

For any selected cell:

$$ \boxed{ (T,P,\omega) \rightarrow \mathcal P } $$

The dashboard can then display:

Temperature
Pressure
Gauge Pressure
Relative Humidity
Humidity Ratio
Specific Humidity
Vapour Pressure
Dry-Air Pressure
Saturation Pressure
Dew Point
Wet Bulb
Enthalpy
Specific Volume
Moist-Air Density
Dry-Air Density
Vapour Density
Velocity
Sensible Energy
Latent Energy
Total Energy
Condensation
52. Psychrometric State at Every Cell

At time \(t_n\):

$$ \boxed{ \mathcal P_{ijk}^{n} = f( T_{ijk}^{n}, P_{ijk}^{n}, \omega_{ijk}^{n} ) } $$

Therefore the complete 3-D field of relative humidity is:

$$ \boxed{ RH(x,y,z,t) } $$

and similarly:

$$ \boxed{ T_{dp}(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ T_{wb}(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ h(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \rho(x,y,z,t) } $$

etc.

This is exactly what enables your requirement:

Every psychrometric parameter at every point with lapse of time.

53. Time History at One Point

For a selected point:

$$ \mathbf{x}_p=(x_p,y_p,z_p) $$

the engine produces:

$$ \boxed{ \mathcal P_p(t) } $$

Therefore:

$$ T_p(t) $$ $$ P_p(t) $$ $$ RH_p(t) $$ $$ \omega_p(t) $$ $$ h_p(t) $$ $$ T_{dp,p}(t) $$

etc.

The dashboard can plot:

$$ \boxed{ \phi(t) } $$

for any selected psychrometric property \(\phi\).

54. Spatial Distribution at One Time

At a fixed time:

$$ t=t^* $$

the dashboard can display:

$$ \boxed{ \phi(x,y,z,t^*) } $$

for any parameter.

For example:

$$ RH(x,y,z,t^*) $$

or:

$$ T_{dp}(x,y,z,t^*) $$

or:

$$ h(x,y,z,t^*) $$
55. Complete Spatiotemporal Representation

Ultimately the model contains:

$$ \boxed{ \phi(x,y,z,t) } $$

for each:

$$ \phi \in \{ T,P,RH,\omega,p_v,T_{dp},T_{wb},h,\rho,v,\ldots \} $$

Thus the digital twin represents a 4-D field:

$$ \boxed{ 3\text{-D space}+time } $$
56. Psychrometric Mixing of Two Air Streams

This is particularly important for injection and leakage.

Suppose streams 1 and 2 mix.

Dry-air mass conservation:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{da,m} = \dot m_{da,1} + \dot m_{da,2} } $$

Water-vapour conservation:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,m} = \dot m_{da,1}\omega_1 + \dot m_{da,2}\omega_2 } $$

Therefore mixed humidity ratio:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_m = \frac{ \dot m_{da,1}\omega_1+ \dot m_{da,2}\omega_2 } { \dot m_{da,1}+\dot m_{da,2} } } $$
57. Mixing Enthalpy

Dry-air-weighted enthalpy:

$$ \boxed{ h_m = \frac{ \dot m_{da,1}h_1+ \dot m_{da,2}h_2 } { \dot m_{da,1}+\dot m_{da,2} } } $$

Then the mixed temperature is obtained by solving:

$$ \boxed{ h_m= 1.006T_m+ \omega_m(2501+1.86T_m) } $$

Thus the psychrometric engine can also provide the state of mixed air.

58. Mixing and Leakage in Your Model

This creates an important physical process:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Outside air} + \text{Cold-room air} \rightarrow \text{Mixed air} } $$

The resulting:

$$ T_m,\omega_m,P_m $$

then produce:

$$ RH_m,T_{dp,m},T_{wb,m},h_m,\rho_m $$

through Module 3.

59. Psychrometric Engine and Pressure-Driven Leakage

Suppose:

$$ P_{out}>P_{room} $$

Then leakage enters the room.

The leakage module calculates:

$$ \dot V_{leak} = C_dA_{leak} \sqrt{ \frac{2(P_{out}-P_{room})} {\rho_{out}} } $$

But \(\rho_{out}\) comes from Module 3:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{out} = \rho(T_{out},P_{out},\omega_{out}) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Psychrometrics} \rightarrow \rho_{out} \rightarrow \text{Leakage flow} } $$
60. Psychrometric Engine and Pressure-Driven Injection

Similarly:

$$ \dot V_{inj} = C_dA_{inj} \sqrt{ \frac{2(P_{inj}-P_{room})} {\rho_{inj}} } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{inj} = \rho(T_{inj},P_{inj},\omega_{inj}) } $$

Again, Module 3 directly participates in calculating the resulting flow.

61. Thermodynamic Consistency Checks

Module 3 should perform internal checks.

Pressure decomposition
$$ \boxed{ P\stackrel{?}{=}p_{da}+p_v } $$
Humidity ratio
$$ \boxed{ \omega \stackrel{?}{=} 0.621945 \frac{p_v}{p_{da}} } $$
Relative humidity
$$ \boxed{ RH \stackrel{?}{=} 100\frac{p_v}{p_{ws}} } $$
Specific humidity
$$ \boxed{ q \stackrel{?}{=} \frac{\omega}{1+\omega} } $$
Specific volume/density
$$ \boxed{ \rho_{da} \stackrel{?}{=} \frac1v } $$

These checks help prevent numerical inconsistencies.

62. Validity Checks

For every cell:

Temperature
$$ \boxed{ T_K>0 } $$
Pressure
$$ \boxed{ P>0 } $$
Vapour pressure
$$ \boxed{ 0\leq p_v<P } $$
Humidity ratio
$$ \boxed{ \omega\geq0 } $$
Specific humidity
$$ \boxed{ 0\leq q<1 } $$
Specific volume
$$ \boxed{ v>0 } $$
Density
$$ \boxed{ \rho>0 } $$
63. Phase-Region Identification

The engine should identify whether the air is:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Unsaturated} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \text{Saturated} } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Supersaturated} } $$

using:

$$ \boxed{ \omega-\omega_s } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ RH-100\% } $$

This flag is passed to the condensation module.

64. Recommended Module 3 Internal Structure

I would divide psychrometrics.py into the following functions:

psychrometrics.py

├── saturation_pressure()
├── saturation_pressure_water()
├── saturation_pressure_ice()
│
├── vapour_pressure_from_rh()
├── vapour_pressure_from_omega()
├── dry_air_pressure()
│
├── humidity_ratio_from_rh()
├── humidity_ratio_from_vapour_pressure()
├── rh_from_humidity_ratio()
├── specific_humidity()
│
├── dew_point()
├── wet_bulb()
│
├── enthalpy()
├── specific_volume()
├── dry_air_density()
├── moist_air_density()
├── vapour_density()
│
├── saturation_humidity_ratio()
├── moisture_deficit()
│
├── sensible_energy()
├── latent_energy()
├── total_energy()
│
├── complete_state()
└── validate_state()
65. Central complete_state() Concept

The most important function should conceptually behave as:

$$ \boxed{ \texttt{complete\_state}(T,P,\omega) } $$

and return:

$$ \boxed{ \{ p_{ws}, p_v, p_{da}, RH, \omega_s, q, T_{dp}, T_{wb}, h, v, \rho_{da}, \rho_{ma}, \rho_v, \ldots \} } $$

Therefore every solver can call the same property engine.

66. Complete Module 3 Formula Chain

For each cell:

$$ \boxed{ T,P,\omega } $$
Temperature conversion
$$ \boxed{ T_K=T+273.15 } $$

↓

Saturation pressure
$$ \boxed{ p_{ws}=p_{ws}(T) } $$

↓

Vapour pressure
$$ \boxed{ p_v= \frac{\omega P} {0.621945+\omega} } $$

↓

Dry-air pressure
$$ \boxed{ p_{da}=P-p_v } $$

↓

Relative humidity
$$ \boxed{ RH= 100\frac{p_v}{p_{ws}} } $$

↓

Saturation humidity ratio
$$ \boxed{ \omega_s= 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws}} {P-p_{ws}} } $$

↓

Specific humidity
$$ \boxed{ q=\frac{\omega}{1+\omega} } $$

↓

Dew point
$$ \boxed{ p_{ws}(T_{dp})=p_v } $$

↓

Wet bulb
$$ \boxed{ f(T_{wb},T,P,\omega)=0 } $$

↓

Enthalpy
$$ \boxed{ h= 1.006T+ \omega(2501+1.86T) } $$

↓

Specific volume
$$ \boxed{ v= \frac{ R_{da}T_K(1+1.6078\omega) }{P} } $$

↓

Density
$$ \boxed{ \rho_{da}=\frac1v } $$ $$ \boxed{ \rho_{ma}=\rho_{da}(1+\omega) } $$

↓

Condensation test
$$ \boxed{ \omega>\omega_s \quad\Rightarrow\quad \text{condensation} } $$

This entire chain is executed for every cell at every saved/required timestep.

67. How Module 3 Connects Everything Together

The most useful way to visualize its role is:

                     ┌──────────────┐
                     │ Temperature  │
                     │    Solver    │
                     └──────┬───────┘
                            │ T
                            ▼
                     ┌──────────────┐
                     │              │
Pressure Solver ────►│ PSYCHROMETRIC│◄──── Moisture Solver
       P             │    ENGINE    │       ω
                     │              │
                     └──────┬───────┘
                            │
       ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐
       │                    │                    │
       ▼                    ▼                    ▼
    Density             Humidity             Enthalpy
       │                    │                    │
       ▼                    ▼                    ▼
   Airflow             Condensation         Energy
    Solver                Solver             Solver
       │                    │                    │
       └────────────────────┼────────────────────┘
                            │
                            ▼
                    Injection / Leakage
                            │
                            ▼
                     Updated source terms
                            │
                            ▼
                       Next timestep

So Module 3 is not an isolated calculator.

It is the common thermodynamic layer connecting:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Temperature} \leftrightarrow \text{Pressure} \leftrightarrow \text{Moisture} \leftrightarrow \text{Density} \leftrightarrow \text{Airflow} \leftrightarrow \text{Heat} \leftrightarrow \text{Condensation} \leftrightarrow \text{Injection} \leftrightarrow \text{Leakage} } $$
68. Final Mathematical Representation

The cleanest formulation for your entire numerical model is to define the psychrometric state operator:

$$ \boxed{ \mathcal P= \mathcal{F}(T,P,\omega) } $$

where:

$$ \mathcal P= \begin{bmatrix} p_{ws}\\ p_v\\ p_{da}\\ RH\\ \omega_s\\ q\\ T_{dp}\\ T_{wb}\\ h\\ v\\ \rho_{da}\\ \rho_{ma}\\ \rho_v \end{bmatrix} $$

At every spatial location and time:

$$ \boxed{ \mathcal P(x,y,z,t) = \mathcal F [ T(x,y,z,t), P(x,y,z,t), \omega(x,y,z,t) ] } $$

This gives you the complete psychrometric field:

$$ \boxed{ \text{3-D space}+\text{time} \rightarrow \text{complete thermodynamic state} } $$
The key point for the implementation

Do not solve separate PDEs for RH, dew point, wet bulb, enthalpy, density, etc. The numerical solver should primarily evolve the physically conserved/transported quantities such as:

$$ \boxed{ T,\;P,\;\mathbf u,\;\omega } $$

while Module 3 continuously derives the rest:

$$ \boxed{ RH,\;p_v,\;p_{ws},\;T_{dp},\;T_{wb},\;h,\;v,\;\rho,\;q,\ldots } $$

This separation is what will make the numerical model internally consistent and will allow the dashboard to show all psychrometric parameters at every point and every time without treating each one as an independent physical field.

















Module 4 — Heat Transfer Model, Sensible Heat, Latent Heat & Total Energy

Module 4 calculates how thermal energy moves into, out of, and within the cold-storage domain.

For your numerical model, the heat-transfer module must account for:

Transient heat storage
Heat conduction/diffusion
Airflow/advection of thermal energy
Wall heat transfer
Internal heat generation
Air injection heat
Air leakage/infiltration heat
Sensible heat
Latent heat due to moisture/condensation
Total energy
Coupling between temperature and moisture

The fundamental temperature field is:

$$ \boxed{T=T(x,y,z,t)} $$

and the complete thermal model should ultimately solve for:

$$ \boxed{ T(x,y,z,t) } $$

while calculating the corresponding energy fields:

$$ \boxed{ E_s(x,y,z,t),\quad E_l(x,y,z,t),\quad E_{total}(x,y,z,t) } $$
1. Heat-Transfer Components

The total thermal-energy change inside the cold storage can be conceptually written as:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{dE_{total}}{dt} = \dot Q_{cond} + \dot Q_{adv} + \dot Q_{wall} + \dot Q_{inj} + \dot Q_{leak} + \dot Q_{internal} + \dot Q_{phase} } $$

where:

\(\dot Q_{cond}\) = conduction
\(\dot Q_{adv}\) = advection
\(\dot Q_{wall}\) = wall heat transfer
\(\dot Q_{inj}\) = injected-air energy
\(\dot Q_{leak}\) = leakage/infiltration energy
\(\dot Q_{internal}\) = internal heat sources
\(\dot Q_{phase}\) = phase-change/latent heat.

The exact numerical governing equation should avoid double-counting any of these terms.

2. General Energy Conservation Equation

For moist air moving through the room, a conservative energy formulation can be written as:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho h)} {\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u h) = \nabla\cdot(k_{eff}\nabla T) + S_h } $$

where:

\(\rho\) = moist-air density
\(h\) = specific enthalpy
\(\mathbf u=(u,v,w)\)
\(k_{eff}\) = effective thermal conductivity
\(S_h\) = volumetric energy source/sink.

However, because your model explicitly separates temperature, moisture and latent heat, it is often more transparent to formulate the temperature equation separately and couple it to the moisture/condensation equation.

3. Temperature-Based Energy Equation

The basic temperature equation is:

$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} = \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) + S_Q + S_{latent} } $$

This is the starting point for the heat-transfer solver.

When airflow is included:

$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p \left( \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} + \mathbf u\cdot\nabla T \right) = \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) + S_Q + S_{latent} } $$
4. Expanded Advection Term

The velocity vector is:

$$ \mathbf u=(u,v,w) $$

Therefore:

$$ \mathbf u\cdot\nabla T = u\frac{\partial T}{\partial x} + v\frac{\partial T}{\partial y} + w\frac{\partial T}{\partial z} $$

Thus:

$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p \left[ \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} + u\frac{\partial T}{\partial x} + v\frac{\partial T}{\partial y} + w\frac{\partial T}{\partial z} \right] = \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) + S_Q + S_{latent} } $$
5. Three-Dimensional Heat-Conduction Term

For constant thermal conductivity:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) = k\nabla^2T } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla^2T = \frac{\partial^2T}{\partial x^2} + \frac{\partial^2T}{\partial y^2} + \frac{\partial^2T}{\partial z^2} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} = k \left( \frac{\partial^2T}{\partial x^2} + \frac{\partial^2T}{\partial y^2} + \frac{\partial^2T}{\partial z^2} \right) + S_Q + S_{latent} } $$

before including airflow.

6. Thermal Diffusivity

Define thermal diffusivity:

$$ \boxed{ \alpha= \frac{k}{\rho c_p} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} = \alpha\nabla^2T + \frac{S_Q+S_{latent}} {\rho c_p} } $$

For the airflow case:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} + \mathbf u\cdot\nabla T = \alpha\nabla^2T + \frac{S_Q+S_{latent}} {\rho c_p} } $$
7. Effective Thermal Diffusivity

If turbulent mixing is later represented using an effective turbulent diffusivity:

$$ \boxed{ \alpha_{eff} = \alpha+\alpha_t } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ \alpha_t=\frac{\nu_t}{Pr_t} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \alpha_{eff} = \frac{k}{\rho c_p} + \frac{\nu_t}{Pr_t} } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} + \mathbf u\cdot\nabla T = \alpha_{eff}\nabla^2T + \frac{S_Q+S_{latent}} {\rho c_p} } $$

This should only be introduced when the airflow model includes an appropriate turbulence/effective-mixing treatment.

8. Finite-Volume Heat Equation

For your planned FVM implementation, start from:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho c_pT)} {\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho c_p\mathbf uT) = \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) + S_Q + S_{latent} } $$

Integrating over a control volume \(V_P\):

$$ \boxed{ \int_{V_P} \frac{\partial(\rho c_pT)}{\partial t}dV + \int_{V_P} \nabla\cdot(\rho c_p\mathbf uT)dV = \int_{V_P} \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T)dV + \int_{V_P} (S_Q+S_{latent})dV } $$

Using the divergence theorem:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{d}{dt} (\rho_Pc_pT_PV_P) + \sum_f \rho_f c_{p,f} T_f (\mathbf u_f\cdot\mathbf A_f) = \sum_f k_f(\nabla T)_f\cdot\mathbf A_f + (S_Q+S_{latent})_PV_P } $$

This is the main equation that the numerical heat solver will discretize.

9. Transient Heat Storage

The energy stored in a cell changes with temperature:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{dE_s}{dt} = m c_p\frac{dT}{dt} } $$

For a control volume:

$$ \boxed{ m=\rho V } $$

therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{dE_s}{dt} = \rho Vc_p\frac{dT}{dt} } $$
10. Sensible Heat

Sensible heat is energy associated with temperature change without phase change.

For mass \(m\):

$$ \boxed{ Q_s=mc_p\Delta T } $$

where:

$$ \Delta T=T_2-T_1 $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ Q_s=mc_p(T_2-T_1) } $$
11. Sensible Heat of a Cold-Room Cell

For cell \(i,j,k\):

$$ \boxed{ Q_{s,ijk} = m_{ijk}c_p (T_{ijk}-T_{ref}) } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ m_{ijk}=\rho_{ijk}V_{ijk} } $$

Thus:

$$ \boxed{ Q_{s,ijk} = \rho_{ijk}V_{ijk} c_p (T_{ijk}-T_{ref}) } $$
12. Sensible Energy Rate

The rate of sensible-energy change is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_s = mc_p\frac{dT}{dt} } $$

For a cell:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{s,ijk} = \rho_{ijk}V_{ijk}c_p \frac{dT_{ijk}}{dt} } $$
13. Sensible Heat Capacity

The thermal capacitance of a cell is:

$$ \boxed{ C_{cell}=\rho Vc_p } $$

with units:

$$ J/K $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ Q_s=C_{cell}\Delta T } $$

This is useful for understanding how quickly each cell responds to a heat input.

14. Sensible Heat of an Air Stream

For an air stream:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_s = \dot m c_p (T_{in}-T_{ref}) } $$

If comparing incoming air with room air:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{s,inj} = \dot m_{da}c_p (T_{in}-T_{room}) } $$

A more complete moist-air treatment should use enthalpy differences rather than manually separating all sensible and latent components.

15. Sensible Heat From Wall Transfer

For convective heat transfer:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{wall} = h_cA(T_{outside}-T_{surface}) } $$

where:

\(h_c\) = convection coefficient
\(A\) = wall area.

Positive \(\dot Q_{wall}\) means heat enters the cold room under the selected sign convention.

16. Wall Heat Flux

The heat flux is:

$$ \boxed{ q''= h_c(T_\infty-T_s) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q=q''A } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q= h_cA(T_\infty-T_s) } $$
17. Conduction Through a Wall

Fourier's law:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf q=-k\nabla T } $$

In one dimension:

$$ \boxed{ q_x=-k\frac{dT}{dx} } $$

For a plane wall:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q= \frac{kA}{L} (T_{out}-T_{in}) } $$
18. Wall Thermal Resistance

For a wall layer:

$$ \boxed{ R_{cond}= \frac{L}{kA} } $$

Convective resistance:

$$ \boxed{ R_{conv}= \frac1{hA} } $$

For inside and outside convection plus multiple wall layers:

$$ \boxed{ R_{total} = \frac1{h_iA} + \sum_n\frac{L_n}{k_nA} + \frac1{h_oA} } $$
19. Overall Heat-Transfer Coefficient

Define:

$$ \boxed{ U= \frac1{ \frac1{h_i} + \sum_n\frac{L_n}{k_n} + \frac1{h_o} } } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{wall} = UA(T_{out}-T_{room}) } $$

This can be used as a boundary heat load in the numerical domain.

20. Latent Heat

Latent heat is energy associated with a phase change.

For water vapour condensation:

$$ \boxed{ Q_l=m_{cond}L } $$

For a condensation rate:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_l = \dot m_{cond}L } $$

where \(L\) is the appropriate phase-change latent heat.

21. Condensation Energy

If water vapour condenses:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Water vapour} \rightarrow \text{Liquid water/ice} } $$

the released latent energy is:

$$ \boxed{ Q_{cond} = m_{cond}L } $$

For a cold-storage model, the appropriate latent heat must be selected consistently with whether the relevant phase transition is condensation to liquid water or deposition/freezing to ice.

22. Latent Heat Source Term

If condensation occurs inside a cell:

$$ \boxed{ S_{latent} = \dot m_{cond}L } $$

if \(S_{latent}\) is expressed as power per unit volume, then:

$$ \boxed{ S_{latent,v} = \frac{\dot m_{cond}L} {V_{cell}} } $$

Units:

$$ W/m^3 $$

This source is added to the temperature equation.

23. Moisture-Phase Coupling

If:

$$ \omega>\omega_s $$

then excess water vapour is removed by condensation.

Define:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta\omega_{cond} = \omega-\omega_s } $$

for:

$$ \omega>\omega_s $$

The corresponding condensed mass is:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta m_{cond} = \rho_{da} \Delta\omega_{cond} V_{cell} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta Q_{latent} = \Delta m_{cond}L } $$

This heat feeds back into temperature.

24. Dynamic Condensation Model

Instead of instantaneous equilibrium, define:

$$ \boxed{ S_{cond} = k_{cond} (\omega-\omega_s) } $$

for:

$$ \omega>\omega_s $$

Then the mass condensation rate is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{cond} = \rho_{da} V_{cell} k_{cond} (\omega-\omega_s) } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{latent} = \rho_{da} V_{cell} k_{cond} (\omega-\omega_s)L } $$

This provides a relaxation-type condensation model.

25. Latent Heat From Moisture Change

For a change in vapour mass:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta Q_l = \Delta m_vL } $$

Since:

$$ m_v=\omega m_{da} $$

we obtain:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta Q_l = m_{da} \Delta\omega L } $$

for a fixed dry-air mass.

26. Latent Energy Density

For a cell:

$$ \boxed{ e_l = \rho_{da}\omega L } $$

with units:

$$ J/m^3 $$

This provides a spatial latent-energy field:

$$ \boxed{ e_l=e_l(x,y,z,t) } $$
27. Total Sensible Energy

For the entire cold-storage room:

$$ \boxed{ E_s = \int_\Omega \rho c_p(T-T_{ref})\,dV } $$

Discrete form:

$$ \boxed{ E_s = \sum_{i,j,k} \rho_{ijk}c_p (T_{ijk}-T_{ref}) V_{ijk} } $$
28. Total Latent Energy

Using the vapour mass:

$$ \boxed{ E_l = \int_\Omega \rho_{da}\omega L\,dV } $$

Discrete form:

$$ \boxed{ E_l = \sum_{i,j,k} \rho_{da,ijk} \omega_{ijk} L V_{ijk} } $$

This represents the energy associated with the water-vapour content under the selected reference convention.

29. Total Energy

A simplified sensible + latent representation is:

$$ \boxed{ E_{total}=E_s+E_l } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ E_{total} = \int_\Omega [ \rho c_p(T-T_{ref}) + \rho_{da}\omega L ]dV } $$

Discrete form:

$$ \boxed{ E_{total} = \sum_{i,j,k} \left[ \rho_{ijk}c_p(T_{ijk}-T_{ref}) + \rho_{da,ijk}\omega_{ijk}L \right] V_{ijk} } $$

Important: this is an engineering decomposition for reporting. The actual conservation solver should use one internally consistent energy/enthalpy formulation so that latent heat is not counted twice.

30. Total Energy Rate
$$ \boxed{ \frac{dE_{total}}{dt} = \frac{dE_s}{dt} + \frac{dE_l}{dt} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot E_{total} = \dot E_s+\dot E_l } $$
31. Energy Due to Airflow

Airflow transports thermal energy.

The enthalpy flux through a face is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{adv,f} = \dot m_f h_f } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_f = \rho_f (\mathbf u_f\cdot\mathbf A_f) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{adv,f} = \rho_f h_f (\mathbf u_f\cdot\mathbf A_f) } $$
32. Net Advective Heat Transfer

For a control volume:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{adv,net} = -\sum_f \rho_fh_f (\mathbf u_f\cdot\mathbf A_f) } $$

The sign depends on the chosen outward-normal convention.

This is how warm injected air can transport energy into the room.

33. Air Injection Energy

For injected air:

$$ \boxed{ \dot E_{inj} = \dot m_{da,in}h_{in} } $$

Relative to room air:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{inj} = \dot m_{da,in} (h_{in}-h_{room}) } $$

where:

$$ h_{in} = h(T_{in},P_{in},\omega_{in}) $$

and:

$$ h_{room} = h(T_{room},P_{room},\omega_{room}) $$

This automatically incorporates the effect of both temperature and humidity.

34. Sensible Component of Injection

If explicitly separated:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{s,inj} = \dot m_{da,in} c_{p,ma} (T_{in}-T_{room}) } $$
35. Latent Component of Injection

Approximately:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{l,inj} = \dot m_{da,in} (\omega_{in}-\omega_{room}) L } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{inj} \approx \dot Q_{s,inj} + \dot Q_{l,inj} } $$

For the actual energy solver, use the enthalpy formulation consistently rather than simultaneously adding both the full enthalpy difference and these components.

36. Leakage Heat Load

For incoming outside air:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{leak} = \dot m_{da,leak} (h_{out}-h_{room}) } $$

This is particularly important for cold storage because outside air may be:

much warmer
much more humid
at a different pressure.
37. Leakage Sensible Heat
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{s,leak} = \dot m_{da,leak} c_p (T_{out}-T_{room}) } $$
38. Leakage Latent Heat

Approximately:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{l,leak} = \dot m_{da,leak} (\omega_{out}-\omega_{room}) L } $$

If the infiltrated moisture subsequently condenses, its phase-change heat is handled by the condensation module.

39. Internal Heat Sources

Suppose equipment generates:

$$ \dot Q_{equip} $$

inside volume:

$$ V_{equip} $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ S_{Q,equip} = \frac{\dot Q_{equip}} {V_{equip}} } $$

For multiple heat sources:

$$ \boxed{ S_Q= \sum_nS_{Q,n} } $$
40. Distributed Heat Source

For a spatially varying source:

$$ \boxed{ S_Q=S_Q(x,y,z,t) } $$

The total heat generated is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{source} = \int_\Omega S_Q\,dV } $$

Discrete:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{source} = \sum_{i,j,k} S_{Q,ijk}V_{ijk} } $$
41. Gaussian Heat Source

If heat is generated around a point:

$$ \mathbf x_s=(x_s,y_s,z_s) $$

use:

$$ \boxed{ S_Q(\mathbf x) = \dot Q_sG(\mathbf x) } $$

where:

$$ G(\mathbf x) = \frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^2)^{3/2}} e^{-r^2/(2\sigma^2)} $$

and:

$$ r= \sqrt{ (x-x_s)^2+ (y-y_s)^2+ (z-z_s)^2 } $$

with appropriate normalization over the computational domain.

42. Heat Flux Boundary Condition

For a wall:

$$ \boxed{ -k\nabla T\cdot\mathbf n = q'' } $$

where:

$$ q''>0 $$

can be defined as heat entering the room depending on the sign convention.

For convection:

$$ \boxed{ -k\nabla T\cdot\mathbf n = h(T_\infty-T_s) } $$
43. Dirichlet Thermal Boundary

If wall temperature is directly prescribed:

$$ \boxed{ T|_{\Gamma}=T_{wall} } $$

This is a fixed-temperature boundary.

44. Neumann Thermal Boundary

If heat flux is prescribed:

$$ \boxed{ -k\frac{\partial T}{\partial n}=q'' } $$
45. Convective/Robin Boundary

For realistic walls:

$$ \boxed{ -k\frac{\partial T}{\partial n} = h(T_\infty-T_s) } $$

This is generally more physically useful than prescribing an arbitrary wall temperature.

46. Radiation Heat Transfer

If radiation is included:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{rad} = \epsilon\sigma A (T_{sur,K}^4-T_{s,K}^4) } $$

where:

\(\epsilon\) = emissivity
\(\sigma\) = Stefan-Boltzmann constant
temperatures are in Kelvin.

Then the wall heat transfer can be:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{wall} = \dot Q_{conv} + \dot Q_{rad} } $$

Radiation can be added later if required.

47. Product Sensible Heat

If stored products are included:

$$ \boxed{ Q_{product} = m_pc_{p,p}(T_p-T_{ref}) } $$

and the product heat-transfer rate is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{product} = m_pc_{p,p} \frac{dT_p}{dt} } $$

If the product is modelled as a separate solid domain, this becomes another energy equation rather than merely an imposed source.

48. Product Latent Heat

If the product undergoes freezing/thawing:

$$ \boxed{ Q_{freeze} = m_pL_f } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{freeze} = \dot m_{freeze}L_f } $$

This should only be included if product phase change is part of your model scope.

49. Total Heat Load on Cold Storage

A useful room-level heat-load equation is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{total,in} = \dot Q_{wall} + \dot Q_{injection} + \dot Q_{leakage} + \dot Q_{equipment} + \dot Q_{product} + \dot Q_{other} } $$

The refrigeration system must eventually remove this energy:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{refrigeration} \geq \dot Q_{total,in} } $$

for a steady or quasi-steady operating condition, subject to the rest of the energy balance.

50. Cooling Load Due to Air Exchange

For an air exchange rate:

$$ \dot V_{air} $$

mass flow is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m = \rho\dot V_{air} } $$

Dry-air mass flow:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{da} = \frac{\rho\dot V_{air}} {1+\omega} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{air} = \dot m_{da} (h_{outside}-h_{inside}) } $$

This is a particularly important equation for door-opening/infiltration calculations.

51. Total Energy Balance of the Room

For the entire domain:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{dE_{total}}{dt} = \dot E_{in} - \dot E_{out} + \dot Q_{wall} + \dot Q_{internal} + \dot Q_{phase} } $$

where air energy flows are included in:

$$ \dot E_{in},\quad\dot E_{out} $$

and:

$$ \dot Q_{phase} = \dot Q_{cond} - \dot Q_{evap} $$

under a chosen sign convention.

52. Full Temperature Equation for Your Model

The recommended coupled temperature equation is:

$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p \left( \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} + u\frac{\partial T}{\partial x} + v\frac{\partial T}{\partial y} + w\frac{\partial T}{\partial z} \right) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x} \left( k\frac{\partial T}{\partial x} \right) + \frac{\partial}{\partial y} \left( k\frac{\partial T}{\partial y} \right) + \frac{\partial}{\partial z} \left( k\frac{\partial T}{\partial z} \right) + S_Q + S_{latent} } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ S_Q = S_{wall} + S_{equipment} + S_{injection} + S_{leakage} + S_{product} +\cdots } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ S_{latent} = S_{cond}L - S_{evap}L } $$

with the appropriate mass-rate units.

53. Discrete 3-D FDM Temperature Equation

If the existing FDM solver is retained as the reference solver, for uniform spacing:

$$ \boxed{ T_{i,j,k}^{n+1} = T_{i,j,k}^{n} + \alpha\Delta t \left[ \frac{ T_{i+1,j,k}^{n} -2T_{i,j,k}^{n} +T_{i-1,j,k}^{n} }{\Delta x^2} \right. } $$ $$ \boxed{ \left. + \frac{ T_{i,j+1,k}^{n} -2T_{i,j,k}^{n} +T_{i,j-1,k}^{n} }{\Delta y^2} + \frac{ T_{i,j,k+1}^{n} -2T_{i,j,k}^{n} +T_{i,j,k-1}^{n} }{\Delta z^2} \right] + \frac{\Delta t} {\rho c_p} (S_Q+S_{latent}) } $$

This is useful as the baseline before moving fully to the FVM formulation.

54. Advection in the Discrete Model

Once airflow is included, the temperature equation additionally contains:

$$ \boxed{ -u\frac{\partial T}{\partial x} -v\frac{\partial T}{\partial y} -w\frac{\partial T}{\partial z} } $$

For example, first-order upwind in \(x\):

$$ \left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial x}\right)_i \approx \begin{cases} \dfrac{T_i-T_{i-1}}{\Delta x}, &u>0\\[8pt] \dfrac{T_{i+1}-T_i}{\Delta x}, &u<0 \end{cases} $$

and similarly for \(y,z\).

55. Heat Transfer Between Adjacent Cells

For two adjacent cells \(P\) and \(N\):

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{P\rightarrow N} = -k_fA_f \frac{T_N-T_P} {d_{PN}} } $$

Equivalently, the flux is:

$$ \boxed{ q_f'' = -k_f \frac{T_N-T_P} {d_{PN}} } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_f=q_f''A_f } $$
56. Thermal Conductivity at a Face

For two materials/cells with conductivities \(k_P\) and \(k_N\), the harmonic mean is commonly used:

$$ \boxed{ k_f = \frac{ 2k_Pk_N } {k_P+k_N} } $$

For equal materials:

$$ k_f=k $$

This becomes important when the mesh contains different materials.

57. Heat Transfer Coefficient Between Cell and Boundary

For a boundary cell:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{boundary} = h_fA_f(T_\infty-T_P) } $$

This heat rate is converted into a volumetric source in the control volume:

$$ \boxed{ S_{boundary} = \frac{ h_fA_f(T_\infty-T_P) } {V_P} } $$
58. Total Sensible + Latent Energy Field

At every cell:

$$ \boxed{ E_{total,ijk} = E_{s,ijk}+E_{l,ijk} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ E_{total}(x,y,z,t) } $$

can be displayed on the dashboard just like temperature or RH.

59. Dashboard Heat Quantities

For each point/cell, the dashboard should be able to display:

Temperature
$$ T(x,y,z,t) $$
Sensible energy
$$ E_s(x,y,z,t) $$
Latent energy
$$ E_l(x,y,z,t) $$
Total energy
$$ E_{total}(x,y,z,t) $$
Heat-transfer rate
$$ \dot Q(x,y,z,t) $$
Condensation heat
$$ \dot Q_{cond}(x,y,z,t) $$
Wall heat flux
$$ q''_{wall}(x,y,z,t) $$
60. Time Evolution

At each timestep:

$$ t^n\rightarrow t^{n+1} $$

the numerical model performs:

$$ \boxed{ T^n \rightarrow \text{heat transport} \rightarrow T^{n+1} } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \omega^n \rightarrow \text{moisture transport} \rightarrow \text{condensation} \rightarrow S_{latent} \rightarrow T^{n+1} } $$

This creates the coupling:

$$ \boxed{ \omega \rightarrow \text{condensation} \rightarrow \text{latent heat} \rightarrow T } $$

while temperature simultaneously affects saturation:

$$ \boxed{ T \rightarrow p_{ws} \rightarrow \omega_s \rightarrow \text{condensation} } $$
61. Critical Coupling Between Modules 3 and 4

Module 3 gives:

$$ \boxed{ \rho=\rho(T,P,\omega) } $$ $$ \boxed{ h=h(T,\omega) } $$ $$ \boxed{ p_{ws}=p_{ws}(T) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \omega_s=\omega_s(T,P) } $$

Module 4 uses:

$$ \rho,\;c_p,\;k,\;h $$

to solve the heat equation.

Then Module 4 updates:

$$ \boxed{ T^{n+1} } $$

which goes back to Module 3.

Thus:

           MODULE 3
       Psychrometric Engine
              │
       ρ, h, pws, ωs
              │
              ▼
        MODULE 4
       Heat Transfer
              │
              │ T
              ▼
       MODULE 3 again
62. Coupling With Injection

Injection provides:

$$ T_{in},P_{in},RH_{in},\omega_{in}, \dot V_{in},\dot m_{in} $$

Module 3 calculates:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{in},h_{in},\omega_{in} } $$

Module 4 then calculates the thermal contribution:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{inj} = \dot m_{da,in} (h_{in}-h_{room}) } $$

This becomes a heat/enthalpy source in the room.

63. Coupling With Leakage

Leakage provides:

$$ T_{out},P_{out},RH_{out} $$

Module 3 calculates:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{out},h_{out},\rho_{out} } $$

Leakage provides:

$$ \dot m_{leak} $$

and Module 4 calculates:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{leak} = \dot m_{da,leak} (h_{out}-h_{room}) } $$

Thus humid warm outside air contributes both:

$$ \boxed{ \text{sensible load} + \text{latent/moisture load} } $$
64. Coupling With Condensation

The sequence is:

$$ T,P,\omega $$

↓

$$ p_{ws},p_v $$

↓

$$ RH,\omega_s $$

↓

If:

$$ \omega>\omega_s $$

then:

$$ \dot m_{cond}>0 $$

↓

$$ \dot Q_{latent} = \dot m_{cond}L $$

↓

Temperature equation:

$$ \boxed{ +\frac{\dot Q_{latent}}{\rho c_pV} } $$

↓

Updated:

$$ T^{n+1} $$

This is one of the most important physical feedback mechanisms in the complete model.

65. Complete Module 4 Data Flow
                  MODULE 1
             Geometry & Mesh
                    │
                    ▼
            Cell V, Af, d, n
                    │
                    ▼
                  MODULE 2
             Initial Conditions
                    │
                    ▼
               T, P, ω, u
                    │
                    ▼
                  MODULE 3
             Psychrometrics
                    │
        ┌───────────┼────────────┐
        ▼           ▼            ▼
       ρ            h          ωs/pws
        │           │            │
        └───────────┼────────────┘
                    ▼
             MODULE 4
            HEAT TRANSFER
                    │
        ┌───────────┼──────────────┐
        ▼           ▼              ▼
   Conduction   Advection      Heat Sources
        │           │              │
        │           │       ┌──────┼───────┐
        │           │       ▼      ▼       ▼
        │           │    Injection Leakage Walls
        │           │
        └───────────┼──────────────┘
                    ▼
             Latent Heat
                    ▲
                    │
             Condensation
                    ▲
                    │
              Module 3
                    │
                    ▼
                T(x,y,z,t)
66. Recommended Core Equations for Implementation

For the first complete version, I recommend making these equations the core of Module 4.

Governing temperature equation
$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p \left( \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} + \mathbf u\cdot\nabla T \right) = \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) + S_Q + S_{latent} } $$
Thermal diffusivity
$$ \boxed{ \alpha=\frac{k}{\rho c_p} } $$
Sensible energy
$$ \boxed{ E_s = \int_\Omega \rho c_p(T-T_{ref})dV } $$
Latent energy
$$ \boxed{ E_l = \int_\Omega \rho_{da}\omega L\,dV } $$
Total energy
$$ \boxed{ E_{total}=E_s+E_l } $$
Wall heat transfer
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{wall}=UA(T_{out}-T_{room}) } $$
Air-stream energy
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{air} = \dot m_{da} (h_{air}-h_{room}) } $$
Condensation latent heat
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{cond} = \dot m_{cond}L } $$
Internal heat source
$$ \boxed{ S_Q=\frac{\dot Q}{V_s} } $$
Face conduction
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_f = -k_fA_f \frac{T_N-T_P}{d_{PN}} } $$
67. One Important Modelling Decision

For your project, do not independently add

$$ \dot Q_{s,inj} $$

and:

$$ \dot Q_{l,inj} $$

and also

$$ \dot m_{da}h_{in} $$

to the same energy equation.

That would risk double-counting energy.

A cleaner final formulation is to use moist-air enthalpy for air-stream energy:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{air} = \dot m_{da} (h_{in}-h_{room}) } $$

and use explicit latent heat only for phase-change processes such as condensation/evaporation.

Similarly, if the energy equation is formulated directly in terms of total enthalpy, the condensation latent heat should not be added a second time.

68. Final Module 4 Relationship

The final numerical model should therefore have:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Module 3} \rightarrow \rho,h,p_{ws},\omega_s } $$ $$ \boxed{ \text{Module 4} \rightarrow T,E_s,E_l,E_{total} } $$

with feedback:

$$ \boxed{ T \rightarrow \text{Module 3} } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \omega \rightarrow \text{condensation} \rightarrow Q_{latent} \rightarrow \text{Module 4} } $$

while injection and leakage provide:

$$ \boxed{ (T_{in},P_{in},RH_{in},\dot V_{in},\mathbf u_{in}) } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ (T_{out},P_{out},RH_{out},A_{leak},\Delta P) } $$

respectively.

So the complete thermal model becomes:

$$ \boxed{ \begin{aligned} &T,P,\omega,\mathbf u\\ &\downarrow\\ &\text{Psychrometric properties}\\ &\downarrow\\ &\rho,h,p_{ws},\omega_s\\ &\downarrow\\ &\text{Conduction + Advection + Wall Heat + Injection + Leakage}\\ &\downarrow\\ &\text{Condensation/Latent Heat}\\ &\downarrow\\ &T(x,y,z,t)\\ &\downarrow\\ &E_s(x,y,z,t),E_l(x,y,z,t),E_{total}(x,y,z,t) \end{aligned} } $$

This gives the basis for the dashboard to show temperature, sensible heat, latent heat, total energy and heat-transfer rates at every computational location as functions of time, while remaining physically coupled to the pressure, moisture, airflow, injection and leakage modules.
















Module 5 — Moisture Transport Model

For the cold-storage digital twin, Module 5 should model moisture as a transient 3-D field:

$$ \boxed{\omega=\omega(x,y,z,t)} $$

where \(\omega\) is the humidity ratio, preferably used instead of \(w\) so that \(w\) can remain the \(z\)-direction velocity component.

The module must account for:

moisture initially present in the room
moisture entering through injection points
moisture entering through leakage/infiltration
different inlet velocities
different inlet temperature
different inlet RH/humidity/dew point
moisture transport by advection
molecular and turbulent diffusion
condensation
evaporation
latent heat coupling
moisture carried out through leakage/exhaust
spatially varying sources
time-dependent sources
pressure-dependent leakage
coupling with temperature and airflow
5.1 Primary Moisture Variable

The recommended primary moisture variable is humidity ratio:

$$ \boxed{\omega=\frac{m_v}{m_{da}}} $$

where

\(m_v\) = mass of water vapor
\(m_{da}\) = mass of dry air

Units:

$$ [\omega]=kg_{water}/kg_{dry\,air} $$

The moisture field is therefore:

$$ \boxed{\omega=\omega(x,y,z,t)} $$

At a computational cell:

$$ \omega_{i,j,k}^{n} $$

represents the humidity ratio at position \((x_i,y_j,z_k)\) and time \(t_n\).

5.2 Relation Between Humidity Ratio and Vapor Pressure

The fundamental psychrometric relationship is:

$$ \boxed{ \omega= 0.621945 \frac{p_v}{P-p_v} } $$

where

\(p_v\) = water-vapor partial pressure
\(P\) = absolute pressure.

Rearranging:

$$ \boxed{ p_v= \frac{\omega P}{0.621945+\omega} } $$

Dry-air partial pressure:

$$ \boxed{ p_{da}=P-p_v } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ p_{da}=\frac{0.621945P}{0.621945+\omega} } $$
5.3 Relative Humidity

Relative humidity at every computational cell:

$$ \boxed{ RH= 100\frac{p_v}{p_{ws}(T)} } $$

where

$$ p_{ws}(T) $$

is saturation vapor pressure at local temperature.

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ RH(x,y,z,t) = 100 \frac{p_v(x,y,z,t)} {p_{ws}[T(x,y,z,t)]} } $$

This is important because RH is not an independent transport variable.

The solver transports \(\omega\), while:

$$ RH=f(T,P,\omega) $$

is calculated afterward.

5.4 Saturation Humidity Ratio

At saturation:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_s= 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws}(T)} {P-p_{ws}(T)} } $$

Therefore, at every location:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_s(x,y,z,t) = 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws}[T(x,y,z,t)]} {P(x,y,z,t)-p_{ws}[T(x,y,z,t)]} } $$

This becomes the key quantity for condensation and evaporation.

5.5 Moisture Conservation Equation

The most important equation in Module 5 is:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf{u}\omega) = \nabla\cdot (\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) + S_\omega + S_{evap} - S_{cond} } $$

where

$$ \mathbf u=(u,v,w) $$

is the air velocity vector.

This equation contains all major moisture-transport mechanisms.

5.6 Expanded 3-D Form

For Cartesian coordinates:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}u\omega)}{\partial x} + \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}v\omega)}{\partial y} + \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}w\omega)}{\partial z} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x} \left( \rho_{da}D_{eff}\frac{\partial\omega}{\partial x} \right) + \frac{\partial}{\partial y} \left( \rho_{da}D_{eff}\frac{\partial\omega}{\partial y} \right) + \frac{\partial}{\partial z} \left( \rho_{da}D_{eff}\frac{\partial\omega}{\partial z} \right) + S_\omega+S_{evap}-S_{cond} } $$

This is the equation that should ultimately be solved numerically.

5.7 Constant-Density Simplification

If \(\rho_{da}\) is assumed approximately constant:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial\omega}{\partial t} + \mathbf u\cdot\nabla\omega = D_{eff}\nabla^2\omega + S_\omega' + S_{evap}' - S_{cond}' } $$

Expanded:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial\omega}{\partial t} + u\frac{\partial\omega}{\partial x} + v\frac{\partial\omega}{\partial y} + w\frac{\partial\omega}{\partial z} = D_{eff} \left( \frac{\partial^2\omega}{\partial x^2} + \frac{\partial^2\omega}{\partial y^2} + \frac{\partial^2\omega}{\partial z^2} \right) + S_\omega' + S_{evap}' - S_{cond}' } $$
5.8 Moisture Diffusion

Molecular diffusion coefficient:

$$ D_m $$

For a more realistic cold-storage model, use an effective diffusivity:

$$ \boxed{ D_{eff}=D_m+D_t } $$

where

\(D_m\) = molecular moisture diffusivity
\(D_t\) = turbulent/eddy diffusivity.

For turbulent transport:

$$ \boxed{ D_t=\frac{\nu_t}{Sc_t} } $$

where

\(\nu_t\) = turbulent kinematic viscosity
\(Sc_t\) = turbulent Schmidt number.

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ D_{eff}=D_m+\frac{\nu_t}{Sc_t} } $$

For a simple laminar prototype:

$$ \boxed{D_{eff}=D_m} $$
5.9 Moisture Diffusion Flux

Fick's law:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf J_m=-\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega } $$

Components:

$$ \boxed{ J_x=-\rho_{da}D_{eff}\frac{\partial\omega}{\partial x} } $$ $$ \boxed{ J_y=-\rho_{da}D_{eff}\frac{\partial\omega}{\partial y} } $$ $$ \boxed{ J_z=-\rho_{da}D_{eff}\frac{\partial\omega}{\partial z} } $$
5.10 Moisture Advection

Moisture transported by moving air is:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf J_{adv} = \rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega } $$

Components:

$$ J_{adv,x}=\rho_{da}u\omega $$ $$ J_{adv,y}=\rho_{da}v\omega $$ $$ J_{adv,z}=\rho_{da}w\omega $$

Total moisture flux:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf J_\omega = \rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega - \rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega } $$
5.11 Moisture Transport Due to Injected Air

Suppose air is injected at location:

$$ \mathbf x_i=(x_i,y_i,z_i) $$

with:

$$ T_i,\quad P_i,\quad RH_i,\quad \omega_i,\quad \dot V_i,\quad \dot m_i,\quad \mathbf u_i $$

The inlet humidity ratio is:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_i= 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,i}} {P_i-p_{v,i}} } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,i}=RH_i\,p_{ws}(T_i) } $$

if RH is expressed as a fraction.

For RH in percentage:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,i} = \frac{RH_i}{100}p_{ws}(T_i) } $$
5.12 Injection Mass Flow Rate

If volumetric flow rate is specified:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_i=\rho_i\dot V_i } $$

If inlet velocity and inlet area are specified:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_i=A_iU_i } $$

therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_i=\rho_iA_iU_i } $$

where:

\(A_i\) = injection area
\(U_i=|\mathbf u_i|\).
5.13 Moisture Mass Flow Entering Through Injection

Dry-air mass flow:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{da,i} = \frac{\dot m_i}{1+\omega_i} } $$

Water-vapor mass flow:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,i} = \dot m_{da,i}\omega_i } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,i} = \frac{\dot m_i\omega_i} {1+\omega_i} } $$

This is the actual water mass entering the cold room.

5.14 Moisture Source Term From Injection

If injection is represented as a volumetric source:

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,i} = \frac{\dot m_{da,i}\omega_i} {V_{source,i}} } $$

Units:

$$ [S_{\omega}] = kg_{water}/(m^3s) $$

For multiple injection points:

$$ \boxed{ S_\omega = \sum_{i=1}^{N} S_{\omega,i} } $$
5.15 Spatially Distributed Injection

For a point source located at:

$$ \mathbf x_i $$

the ideal mathematical representation is:

$$ \boxed{ S_\omega(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_{da,i}\omega_i \delta(\mathbf x-\mathbf x_i) } $$

For numerical implementation, a Gaussian distribution is preferable:

$$ \boxed{ G_i(\mathbf x) = \frac{1} {(2\pi\sigma_i^2)^{3/2}} \exp \left[ -\frac{|\mathbf x-\mathbf x_i|^2} {2\sigma_i^2} \right] } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,i}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_{da,i}\omega_i G_i(\mathbf x) } $$

with time dependence:

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,i}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_{da,i}(t)\omega_i(t)G_i(\mathbf x) } $$
5.16 Directional Injection

If injection has speed \(U_i\) and direction vector:

$$ \hat{\mathbf d}_i=(d_x,d_y,d_z) $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u_i=U_i\hat{\mathbf d}_i } $$

with:

$$ d_x^2+d_y^2+d_z^2=1 $$

Therefore:

$$ u_i=U_id_x $$ $$ v_i=U_id_y $$ $$ w_i=U_id_z $$

The moisture advection generated by this inlet becomes:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf J_{adv,i} = \rho_{da,i} U_i\hat{\mathbf d}_i\omega_i } $$
5.17 Injection Specified by Pressure

If injection is pressure-driven:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta P=P_i-P_{room} } $$

For an orifice:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_i = C_dA_i \sqrt{\frac{2|\Delta P|}{\rho_i}} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_i=\rho_i\dot V_i } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,i} = \frac{\rho_i\dot V_i\omega_i} {1+\omega_i} } $$
5.18 Leakage / Infiltration Moisture

For leakage through an opening:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_L = C_dA_L \sqrt{\frac{2|\Delta P|}{\rho}} } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta P=P_{outside}-P_{inside} } $$

The direction is determined by the sign of \(\Delta P\).

If:

$$ P_{outside}>P_{inside} $$

then outside air enters.

If:

$$ P_{inside}>P_{outside} $$

then air leaves.

5.19 Leakage Moisture From Outside Air

Outside air properties:

$$ T_o,\quad P_o,\quad RH_o $$

First:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,o} = \frac{RH_o}{100}p_{ws}(T_o) } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_o = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,o}} {P_o-p_{v,o}} } $$

Dry-air mass flow:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{da,L} = \frac{\rho_o\dot V_L} {1+\omega_o} } $$

Water-vapor infiltration rate:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,L} = \dot m_{da,L}\omega_o } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,L} = \frac{\rho_o\dot V_L\omega_o} {1+\omega_o} } $$
5.20 Leakage Moisture Source

For an opening/source volume:

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,L} = \frac{\dot m_{v,L}}{V_{source}} } $$

or, more correctly in a finite-volume boundary formulation, apply the moisture mass flux directly at the boundary:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m''_{v,L} = \rho_{da,o}U_n\omega_o } $$

where \(U_n\) is normal velocity through the opening.

5.21 Time-Dependent Leakage

If a door opens at \(t=t_o\) and closes at \(t=t_c\):

$$ A(t)= \begin{cases} A_{door}, &t_o\le t\le t_c\\ 0,&\text{otherwise} \end{cases} $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_L(t) = C_dA(t) \sqrt{\frac{2|\Delta P(t)|}{\rho(t)}} } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,L}(t) = \frac{\rho_o\dot V_L(t)\omega_o} {1+\omega_o} } $$

This allows the digital twin to reproduce transient moisture spikes during door opening.

5.22 Condensation Criterion

At every cell:

$$ \boxed{ p_v>p_{ws}(T) } $$

means supersaturation.

Equivalent condition:

$$ \boxed{ RH>100\% } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ \omega>\omega_s(T,P) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \omega>\omega_s \quad\Rightarrow\quad \text{condensation} } $$
5.23 Condensation Amount

For equilibrium condensation:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{cond} = \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$

The resulting humidity ratio is:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{new} = \min(\omega,\omega_s) } $$

Water condensed per kg dry air:

$$ \boxed{ m_{cond,da} = \omega-\omega_s } $$

Therefore, for dry-air mass \(m_{da}\):

$$ \boxed{ m_{cond} = m_{da} (\omega-\omega_s) } $$

for:

$$ \omega>\omega_s. $$
5.24 Dynamic Condensation Rate

Instead of instantaneous equilibrium, use a condensation-rate coefficient:

$$ \boxed{ S_{cond} = k_{cond} \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$

where \(k_{cond}\) controls condensation timescale.

More physically:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{d\omega}{dt}\bigg|_{cond} = -k_{cond} \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ S_{cond} = \rho_{da}k_{cond} \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$

in water-mass-per-volume-per-time units.

5.25 Surface Condensation

For a cold wall/surface:

$$ T_s<T_{dp} $$

means condensation occurs.

The dew-point condition is:

$$ \boxed{ T_s<T_{dp} } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ p_{ws}(T_{dp})=p_v } $$

A simplified surface condensation flux may be written:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m''_{cond} = h_m(\rho_{v,\infty}-\rho_{v,s}) } $$

where:

\(h_m\) = mass-transfer coefficient
\(\rho_{v,\infty}\) = vapor density near surface
\(\rho_{v,s}\) = saturation vapor density at surface temperature.

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{cond} = A_s\dot m''_{cond} } $$
5.26 Condensation Latent Heat

The latent heat released is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{cond} = \dot m_{cond}L_v } $$

where:

$$ L_v\approx2.5\times10^6\ J/kg $$

with the exact value depending on temperature.

Volumetric latent source:

$$ \boxed{ S_{latent,cond} = S_{cond}L_v } $$

Therefore, condensation couples directly to Module 4:

$$ \boxed{ S_{energy,+}=L_vS_{cond} } $$
5.27 Evaporation Criterion

Evaporation occurs when liquid water is available and the surrounding air is below saturation:

$$ \boxed{ \omega<\omega_s } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ RH<100\% } $$

and liquid water must exist:

$$ \boxed{ m_{liquid}>0 } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ RH<100\% \;\land\; m_{liquid}>0 \quad\Rightarrow\quad \text{evaporation possible} } $$
5.28 Evaporation Rate

A simple relaxation model:

$$ \boxed{ S_{evap} = k_{evap} \max(0,\omega_s-\omega) } $$

with liquid-water availability constraint.

More explicitly:

$$ \boxed{ S_{evap} = \min \left[ k_{evap}\rho_{da}(\omega_s-\omega), \frac{m_{liquid}}{\Delta t} \right] } $$

This prevents the model from evaporating more water than actually exists.

5.29 Evaporation Latent Heat

Evaporation consumes latent heat:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{evap} = \dot m_{evap}L_v } $$

Energy source term:

$$ \boxed{ S_{energy,evap} = -L_vS_{evap} } $$

Thus:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Condensation}\rightarrow +L_vS_{cond} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \text{Evaporation}\rightarrow -L_vS_{evap} } $$
5.30 Net Phase-Change Source

The net moisture source due to phase change is:

$$ \boxed{ S_{phase} = S_{evap}-S_{cond} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) = \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) + S_\omega + S_{evap} - S_{cond} } $$
5.31 Net Latent-Heat Source

The corresponding energy source is:

$$ \boxed{ S_{latent} = L_v(S_{cond}-S_{evap}) } $$

Notice the opposite signs:

Process	Moisture	Energy
Injection	\(+\)	depends on inlet enthalpy
Leakage inward	\(+\)	depends on outside enthalpy
Condensation	\(-\)	\(+L_vS_{cond}\)
Evaporation	\(+\)	\(-L_vS_{evap}\)
Leakage outward	\(-\)	removes energy
5.32 Liquid Water Inventory

For condensation/evaporation tracking, introduce:

$$ \boxed{ M_l(x,y,z,t) } $$

= liquid-water mass in the cell.

Liquid-water balance:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial M_l}{\partial t} = \dot M_{cond} - \dot M_{evap} } $$

or volumetrically:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial\rho_l}{\partial t} = S_{cond}-S_{evap} } $$

where \(\rho_l\) is liquid-water mass per unit volume.

5.33 Condensate Accumulation

For a surface:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{dM_{cond,surface}}{dt} = \dot m_{cond,surface} - \dot m_{drain} - \dot m_{evap,surface} } $$

This can eventually be used to predict:

frost/condensate accumulation
wet surfaces
drainage requirement
evaporative re-entrainment.
5.34 Moisture Transport With All Sources

The final equation can therefore be written as:

$$ \boxed{ \begin{aligned} \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) ={}& \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) \\ &+ \sum_iS_{\omega,in,i} + \sum_jS_{\omega,leak,j} \\ &+ S_{evap} - S_{cond} \end{aligned} } $$

This is the core Module 5 equation.

5.35 Coupling With Temperature

Moisture transport depends strongly on temperature because:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_s=f(T,P) } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ p_{ws}=f(T) } $$

Therefore:

$$ T \rightarrow p_{ws} \rightarrow \omega_s \rightarrow RH \rightarrow condensation/evaporation $$

while phase change feeds energy back:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Condensation} \rightarrow +L_vS_{cond} \rightarrow T\uparrow } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \text{Evaporation} \rightarrow -L_vS_{evap} \rightarrow T\downarrow } $$

This creates a two-way coupling between Modules 4 and 5.

5.36 Coupling With Air Velocity

Airflow transports moisture:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u \rightarrow \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ u,v,w \rightarrow \text{moisture advection} \rightarrow \omega(x,y,z,t) } $$

Conversely, temperature and humidity influence density:

$$ \boxed{ \rho=\rho(T,P,\omega) } $$

which can influence airflow and pressure.

Thus the complete coupling is:

$$ \boxed{ P,T,\omega \rightarrow \rho \rightarrow \mathbf u \rightarrow \omega } $$
5.37 Coupling With Injection Velocity

For each inlet:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_i=A_iU_i } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_i=\rho_iA_iU_i } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,i} = \frac{\rho_iA_iU_i\omega_i} {1+\omega_i} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ U_i,T_i,RH_i,P_i,A_i \rightarrow \omega_i \rightarrow \dot m_{v,i} \rightarrow S_{\omega,i} } $$

This is exactly the mechanism needed for multiple injection locations having different conditions.

5.38 Injection With Different Heat Conditions

If the incoming air has specific heat \(c_{p,i}\):

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{sensible,i} = \dot m_i c_{p,i} (T_i-T_{ref}) } $$

Moisture-related latent energy associated with its humidity:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{latent,i} = \dot m_{da,i}\omega_iL_v } $$

Approximate total air enthalpy:

$$ \boxed{ h_i = c_{p,da}T_i + \omega_i(c_{p,v}T_i+L_v) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{total,i} = \dot m_{da,i}h_i } $$

This is important because an inlet with the same temperature but different humidity does not have the same total energy.

5.39 Moisture Enthalpy Coupling

Moist-air specific enthalpy:

$$ \boxed{ h = 1.006T+ \omega(2501+1.86T) } $$

in:

$$ kJ/kg_{da} $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial h}{\partial\omega} = 2501+1.86T } $$

Approximately:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta h_{latent} \approx L_v\Delta\omega } $$
5.40 Total Moisture-Associated Energy

Water-vapor latent energy in a cell:

$$ \boxed{ E_{latent} = m_{da}\omega L_v } $$

Since:

$$ m_{da}=\rho_{da}V $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ E_{latent} = \rho_{da}V\omega L_v } $$

Latent energy density:

$$ \boxed{ e_{latent} = \rho_{da}\omega L_v } $$
5.41 Numerical Moisture Equation — FDM

For a simple explicit finite-difference implementation:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{i,j,k}^{n+1} = \omega_{i,j,k}^{n} + \Delta t \left[ D_{eff}\nabla^2\omega - \mathbf u\cdot\nabla\omega + S_\omega' + S_{evap}' - S_{cond}' \right]_{i,j,k}^{n} } $$

For equal grid spacing:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla^2\omega = \frac{ \omega_{i+1,j,k} +\omega_{i-1,j,k} +\omega_{i,j+1,k} +\omega_{i,j-1,k} +\omega_{i,j,k+1} +\omega_{i,j,k-1} -6\omega_{i,j,k} } {\Delta x^2} } $$
5.42 FDM Diffusion Stability

For equal grid spacing:

$$ \boxed{ r_m= \frac{D_{eff}\Delta t}{\Delta x^2} } $$

For a 3-D explicit diffusion scheme:

$$ \boxed{ r_m\le\frac16 } $$

More generally, for unequal spacing:

$$ \boxed{ D_{eff}\Delta t \left( \frac1{\Delta x^2} + \frac1{\Delta y^2} + \frac1{\Delta z^2} \right) \le \frac12 } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta t_{diff} \le \frac{1} {2D_{eff} \left( 1/\Delta x^2+ 1/\Delta y^2+ 1/\Delta z^2 \right)} } $$
5.43 Advection Stability — CFL

For velocity:

$$ \mathbf u=(u,v,w) $$

define:

$$ \boxed{ C_x=\frac{|u|\Delta t}{\Delta x} } $$ $$ \boxed{ C_y=\frac{|v|\Delta t}{\Delta y} } $$ $$ \boxed{ C_z=\frac{|w|\Delta t}{\Delta z} } $$

For a first-order explicit upwind scheme, a conservative condition is:

$$ \boxed{ C_x+C_y+C_z\le1 } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta t_{adv} \le \frac{1} { |u|/\Delta x+ |v|/\Delta y+ |w|/\Delta z } } $$
5.44 Phase-Change Stability

For:

$$ S_{cond}=k_{cond}(\omega-\omega_s) $$

the reaction/relaxation timestep should satisfy approximately:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta t_{phase} \lesssim \frac{1}{k_{cond}} } $$

Similarly for evaporation:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta t_{evap} \lesssim \frac{1}{k_{evap}} } $$
5.45 Combined Timestep

The actual timestep should satisfy all restrictions:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta t = C_{safe} \min ( \Delta t_{diff}, \Delta t_{adv}, \Delta t_{phase}, \Delta t_{source} ) } $$

where:

$$ 0<C_{safe}<1 $$
5.46 Finite-Volume Form

For the final numerical model, FVM is preferable.

General moisture equation:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) = \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) + S_\omega } $$

Integrating over a cell:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{d}{dt} (\rho_{da}\omega V) + \sum_f F_{m,f}\omega_f = \sum_f D_f(\omega_N-\omega_P)A_f + S_\omega V } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ F_{m,f}=\rho_{da,f}u_{n,f}A_f } $$
5.47 Moisture Face Flux

Advective moisture flux:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,adv,f} = \rho_{da,f}u_{n,f}A_f\omega_f } $$

Diffusive flux:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,diff,f} = -\rho_{da,f}D_{eff,f} A_f \frac{\omega_N-\omega_P}{d_{PN}} } $$

Total face flux:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,f} = \dot m_{v,adv,f} + \dot m_{v,diff,f} } $$
5.48 Upwind Moisture Transport

For first-order upwind:

$$ \omega_f= \begin{cases} \omega_P,&F_f>0\\ \omega_N,&F_f<0 \end{cases} $$

where:

$$ F_f=\rho_{da,f}u_{n,f}A_f $$

This is a good first implementation because it is robust for the cold-storage prototype.

5.49 Moisture Balance of Entire Cold Room

Total vapor mass:

$$ \boxed{ M_v(t) = \int_\Omega \rho_{da}\omega\,dV } $$

Discrete:

$$ \boxed{ M_v(t) = \sum_{i,j,k} \rho_{da,i,j,k} \omega_{i,j,k} V_{i,j,k} } $$

Global balance:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{dM_v}{dt} = \dot M_{v,in} + \dot M_{v,evap} - \dot M_{v,cond} - \dot M_{v,out} } $$

With multiple sources:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{dM_v}{dt} = \sum_i\dot M_{v,in,i} + \sum_j\dot M_{v,leak,in,j} + \dot M_{v,evap} - \dot M_{v,cond} - \dot M_{v,out} } $$

This equation should be used as one of the verification tests for the digital twin.

5.50 Moisture Conservation Error

Define:

$$ \boxed{ \epsilon_M = M_v(t) - \left[ M_v(0) + M_{v,in} + M_{v,evap} - M_{v,cond} - M_{v,out} \right] } $$

Relative error:

$$ \boxed{ \epsilon_{M,\%} = 100 \frac{|\epsilon_M|} {M_{v,reference}} } $$

This should approach zero as the numerical scheme is refined.

5.51 Local Moisture Residence Time

For a region of volume \(V\):

$$ \boxed{ \tau_m = \frac{M_v} {\dot M_{v,out}} } $$

This provides an estimate of how long moisture remains inside the cold storage.

5.52 Moisture Mixing / Uniformity

Mean humidity ratio:

$$ \boxed{ \bar\omega(t) = \frac{1}{V} \int_\Omega\omega\,dV } $$

Discrete:

$$ \boxed{ \bar\omega = \frac{\sum\omega_{ijk}V_{ijk}} {\sum V_{ijk}} } $$

Moisture standard deviation:

$$ \boxed{ \sigma_\omega = \sqrt{ \frac1V \int_\Omega (\omega-\bar\omega)^2dV } } $$

Coefficient of variation:

$$ \boxed{ CV_\omega = \frac{\sigma_\omega}{\bar\omega} } $$

This is useful for determining whether humidity is spatially uniform.

5.53 Local Condensation Rate Field

The dashboard should calculate:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{cond}(x,y,z,t) } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ S_{cond}(x,y,z,t) } $$

Then visualize:

$$ \boxed{ C(x,y,z,t) = S_{cond} } $$

This gives a 3-D condensation-risk map.

5.54 Local Evaporation Rate Field

Similarly:

$$ \boxed{ E(x,y,z,t) = S_{evap} } $$

so the dashboard can show:

evaporation zones
condensation zones
dry zones
saturated zones.
5.55 Dew-Point Condensation Map

At each cell:

$$ \boxed{ T_{dp}=f(P,\omega) } $$

Condensation risk:

$$ \boxed{ T<T_{dp} } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ T-T_{dp}<0 } $$

Define condensation margin:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta T_{cond} = T-T_{dp} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta T_{cond}<0 \Rightarrow \text{condensation risk} } $$
5.56 Moisture Transport Through Time

Your digital twin should therefore store:

$$ \boxed{ \omega(x,y,z,t) } $$

and derive:

$$ \boxed{ RH(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ p_v(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ T_{dp}(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \omega_s(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ S_{cond}(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ S_{evap}(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ E_{latent}(x,y,z,t) } $$
5.57 Complete Coupled System

Ultimately Modules 2–5 should interact as:

$$ \boxed{ \begin{aligned} &\text{Initial Conditions}\\ &\downarrow\\ &T,P,\omega,\mathbf u\\ &\downarrow\\ &\text{Psychrometric Engine}\\ &\downarrow\\ &p_v,p_{ws},RH,\omega_s,T_{dp},h,\rho\\ &\downarrow\\ &\text{Airflow}\\ &\downarrow\\ &\mathbf u(x,y,z,t)\\ &\downarrow\\ &\text{Moisture Transport}\\ &\downarrow\\ &\omega(x,y,z,t)\\ &\downarrow\\ &\text{Condensation / Evaporation}\\ &\downarrow\\ &S_{cond},S_{evap}\\ &\downarrow\\ &\text{Heat Transfer}\\ &\downarrow\\ &T(x,y,z,t) \end{aligned} } $$

So the important feedback loop is:

$$ \boxed{ T,P,\omega \rightarrow RH,\omega_s,T_{dp} \rightarrow condensation/evaporation \rightarrow latent\ heat \rightarrow T } $$

while:

$$ \boxed{ T,P,\omega \rightarrow \rho \rightarrow \mathbf u \rightarrow moisture\ advection } $$
5.58 Final Governing Equation for Your Digital Twin

For the full Module 5 implementation, I would use this as the master equation:

$$ \boxed{ \begin{aligned} \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) ={}& \nabla\cdot (\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) \\ &+ \sum_i S_{\omega,in,i}(\mathbf x,t) \\ &+ \sum_j S_{\omega,leak,j}(\mathbf x,t) \\ &+ S_{evap}(\mathbf x,t) - S_{cond}(\mathbf x,t) \end{aligned} } $$

with:

$$ \boxed{ D_{eff}=D_m+\frac{\nu_t}{Sc_t} } $$ $$ \boxed{ S_{cond} = \rho_{da}k_{cond} \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$ $$ \boxed{ S_{evap} = \rho_{da}k_{evap} \max(0,\omega_s-\omega) } $$

subject to:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_s= 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws}(T)} {P-p_{ws}(T)} } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ RH= 100\frac{p_v}{p_{ws}(T)} } $$

with:

$$ \boxed{ p_v= \frac{\omega P}{0.621945+\omega} } $$
Module 5 → Module 4 coupling

The most important coupling equations are:

$$ \boxed{ S_{latent} = L_v(S_{cond}-S_{evap}) } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ E_{latent} = \rho_{da}V\omega L_v } $$

Thus:

Moisture equation

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} +\nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) = \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) +S_{in}+S_{leak}+S_{evap}-S_{cond} } $$

Energy equation

$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p\frac{DT}{Dt} = \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) + S_Q + L_vS_{cond} - L_vS_{evap} } $$

These two equations should not be treated independently. They form the core thermo-moisture coupling of your numerical cold-storage twin.

One critical implementation point

Do not independently transport RH, dew point, wet-bulb temperature, latent heat, etc. The numerical solver should primarily solve:

$$ \boxed{\mathbf Q=[T,P,\mathbf u,\omega]} $$

and derive:

$$ \boxed{ RH,\ p_v,\ p_{ws},\ \omega_s,\ T_{dp},\ T_{wb},\ h,\rho,\ S_{cond},S_{evap},E_{latent} } $$

from those primary fields at every:

$$ \boxed{(x,y,z,t)} $$

That architecture will keep the model thermodynamically consistent and makes the different inlet/leakage conditions at different locations and different times straightforward to implement.















Module 6 — Condensation & Evaporation Model

Module 6 should take the temperature, pressure, velocity, humidity ratio, psychrometric properties, and moisture field produced by Modules 3–5 and calculate, at every location and every time:

$$ \boxed{ C=C(x,y,z,t) } $$

for condensation, and

$$ \boxed{ E=E(x,y,z,t) } $$

for evaporation.

The model should handle:

condensation in the air volume
condensation on cold walls/surfaces
evaporation of accumulated condensate
injected air with different \(T,P,RH,\omega,U,\dot V,\dot m\)
leaked/infiltrated air with different \(T,P,RH\)
latent heat release/absorption
sensible heat
total enthalpy/energy
spatially varying condensation
transient condensation
condensate accumulation
drainage
frost/ice formation if temperature is below freezing
coupling with the heat and moisture equations.
6.1 Primary State Variables

At each computational cell:

$$ \boxed{ T=T(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ P=P(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \mathbf u=(u,v,w) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \omega=\omega(x,y,z,t) } $$

where \(\omega\) is humidity ratio.

The condensation module derives:

$$ \boxed{ p_v,\quad p_{ws},\quad RH,\quad \omega_s,\quad T_{dp},\quad S_{cond},\quad S_{evap} } $$

from these variables.

6.2 Water-Vapor Partial Pressure
$$ \boxed{ p_v= \frac{\omega P} {0.621945+\omega} } $$

Dry-air partial pressure:

$$ \boxed{ p_{da}=P-p_v } $$
6.3 Saturation Vapor Pressure

At local temperature:

$$ \boxed{ p_{ws}=p_{ws}(T) } $$

For a practical numerical model, use a validated water/ice saturation-pressure correlation or property library rather than relying permanently on a simple empirical approximation.

6.4 Saturation Humidity Ratio
$$ \boxed{ \omega_s(T,P) = 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws}(T)} {P-p_{ws}(T)} } $$

This is one of the most important equations in Module 6.

6.5 Relative Humidity
$$ \boxed{ RH= 100\frac{p_v}{p_{ws}(T)} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ RH>100\% \iff \omega>\omega_s } $$

approximately indicates supersaturation and therefore condensation potential.

6.6 Dew-Point Temperature

Dew point is defined by:

$$ \boxed{ p_{ws}(T_{dp})=p_v } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ T_{dp}=p_{ws}^{-1}(p_v) } $$

Condensation occurs on a surface when:

$$ \boxed{ T_s<T_{dp} } $$

or equivalently:

$$ \boxed{ p_v>p_{ws}(T_s) } $$
6.7 Condensation Criterion in Air

For volumetric condensation:

$$ \boxed{ \omega>\omega_s(T,P) } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ RH>100\% } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ S_{cond}>0 } $$

Otherwise:

$$ \boxed{ S_{cond}=0 } $$

for the simple equilibrium model.

6.8 Supersaturation Ratio

Define:

$$ \boxed{ S= \frac{p_v}{p_{ws}(T)} } $$

Since:

$$ RH=100S $$

we have:

$$ \boxed{ S=\frac{RH}{100} } $$

Condensation condition:

$$ \boxed{ S>1 } $$
6.9 Supersaturation Excess

Define:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta\omega_{sup} = \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta p_{sup} = \max(0,p_v-p_{ws}) } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta RH = \max(0,RH-100) } $$

These can be used as condensation-risk indicators.

6.10 Equilibrium Condensation

If the model assumes instantaneous thermodynamic equilibrium:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{new} = \min(\omega,\omega_s) } $$

The amount removed from the vapor phase is:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta\omega_{cond} = \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$

For dry-air mass \(m_{da}\):

$$ \boxed{ m_{cond} = m_{da} \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$
6.11 Condensation Rate

For a transient model, use a finite condensation rate rather than instantaneous equilibrium:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{d\omega}{dt}\bigg|_{cond} = -k_{cond} \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$

where:

$$ k_{cond}>0 $$

is a condensation-rate coefficient.

Thus:

$$ \boxed{ S_{cond} = \rho_{da}k_{cond} \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$

Units:

$$ [S_{cond}] = kg_{water}\,m^{-3}s^{-1} $$
6.12 Condensation Mass Rate in a Cell

For cell volume \(V_c\):

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{cond} = S_{cond}V_c } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{cond} = \rho_{da} k_{cond} \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) V_c } $$

Over one timestep:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta m_{cond} = \dot m_{cond}\Delta t } $$
6.13 Total Condensation Rate

For the entire cold room:

$$ \boxed{ \dot M_{cond} = \int_\Omega S_{cond}\,dV } $$

For discrete cells:

$$ \boxed{ \dot M_{cond} = \sum_{i,j,k} S_{cond,i,j,k}V_{i,j,k} } $$
6.14 Cumulative Condensation

Total accumulated condensation:

$$ \boxed{ M_{cond}(t) = \int_0^t \dot M_{cond}(\tau)\,d\tau } $$

Discrete:

$$ \boxed{ M_{cond}^{n} = M_{cond}^{n-1} + \dot M_{cond}^{n}\Delta t } $$
6.15 Condensation on Cold Surfaces

For a wall/surface:

$$ \boxed{ T_s<T_{dp} } $$

means the surface is below the dew point.

Equivalent:

$$ \boxed{ p_v>p_{ws}(T_s) } $$

The vapor concentration difference can be represented using:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta\rho_v = \rho_{v,\infty}-\rho_{v,s} } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_v=\frac{p_v}{R_vT} } $$

and at the surface:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{v,s} = \frac{p_{ws}(T_s)} {R_vT_s} } $$
6.16 Surface Condensation Mass Flux

A simplified mass-transfer relation is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m''_{cond} = h_m (\rho_{v,\infty}-\rho_{v,s}) } $$

where:

\(h_m\) = mass-transfer coefficient
\(\rho_{v,\infty}\) = vapor density adjacent to surface
\(\rho_{v,s}\) = saturation vapor density at surface.

Condensation occurs only if:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{v,\infty}>\rho_{v,s} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m''_{cond} = h_m \max (0,\rho_{v,\infty}-\rho_{v,s}) } $$
6.17 Total Surface Condensation Rate

For surface area \(A_s\):

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{cond,s} = A_s\dot m''_{cond} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{cond,s} = A_sh_m \max (0,\rho_{v,\infty}-\rho_{v,s}) } $$
6.18 Mass-Transfer Coefficient

The mass-transfer coefficient can be related to Sherwood number:

$$ \boxed{ Sh= \frac{h_mL}{D_{eff}} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ h_m= \frac{ShD_{eff}}{L} } $$

For example, for external forced convection, an appropriate correlation may provide:

$$ \boxed{ Sh=f(Re,Sc) } $$
6.19 Reynolds Number

For airflow over a surface:

$$ \boxed{ Re= \frac{\rho UL}{\mu} } $$

where:

\(U\) = local air speed
\(L\) = characteristic length
\(\mu\) = dynamic viscosity.
6.20 Schmidt Number
$$ \boxed{ Sc= \frac{\mu}{\rho D_{eff}} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ Sh=f(Re,Sc) } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ h_m=\frac{ShD_{eff}}{L} } $$

This provides the connection between air velocity and condensation rate.

6.21 Effect of Injection Velocity

For injected air:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_i=A_iU_i } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_i=\rho_iA_iU_i } $$

The incoming vapor mass rate is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,i} = \frac{\rho_iA_iU_i\omega_i} {1+\omega_i} } $$

Therefore, increasing inlet velocity can increase:

moisture transport
mixing
vapor delivery
surface mass-transfer coefficient
condensation rate when the surface is below dew point.
6.22 Injected-Air Psychrometrics
