where:

\(P\) = pressure
\(\boldsymbol\tau\) = viscous stress tensor
\(\rho\mathbf g\) = gravity
\(\mathbf S_u\) = momentum source.
7.15 Expanded X-Momentum Equation
$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho u)}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial(\rho u^2)}{\partial x} + \frac{\partial(\rho uv)}{\partial y} + \frac{\partial(\rho uw)}{\partial z} = -\frac{\partial P}{\partial x} + (\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol\tau)_x + \rho g_x + S_{u,x} } $$
7.16 Expanded Y-Momentum Equation
$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho v)}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial(\rho vu)}{\partial x} + \frac{\partial(\rho v^2)}{\partial y} + \frac{\partial(\rho vw)}{\partial z} = -\frac{\partial P}{\partial y} + (\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol\tau)_y + \rho g_y + S_{u,y} } $$
7.17 Expanded Z-Momentum Equation
$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho w)}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial(\rho wu)}{\partial x} + \frac{\partial(\rho wv)}{\partial y} + \frac{\partial(\rho w^2)}{\partial z} = -\frac{\partial P}{\partial z} + (\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol\tau)_z + \rho g_z + S_{u,z} } $$
7.18 Newtonian Stress Tensor

For a Newtonian fluid:

$$ \boxed{ \boldsymbol\tau = \mu \left[ \nabla\mathbf u+ (\nabla\mathbf u)^T \right] - \frac23\mu (\nabla\cdot\mathbf u)\mathbf I } $$

For incompressible flow:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0 } $$

therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \boldsymbol\tau = \mu \left[ \nabla\mathbf u+ (\nabla\mathbf u)^T \right] } $$
7.19 Simplified Momentum Equation

For constant \(\rho,\mu\) and incompressible flow:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial\mathbf u}{\partial t} + (\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u = -\frac1\rho\nabla P + \nu\nabla^2\mathbf u + \mathbf g + \frac{\mathbf S_u}{\rho} } $$

This is the Navier–Stokes form most useful for a first airflow implementation.

7.20 Convective Acceleration
$$ \boxed{ (\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u } $$

Expanded:

$$ \boxed{ (\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u = u\frac{\partial\mathbf u}{\partial x} + v\frac{\partial\mathbf u}{\partial y} + w\frac{\partial\mathbf u}{\partial z} } $$
7.21 Buoyancy

Temperature differences produce density differences.

The gravitational force is:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf F_g=\rho\mathbf g } $$

For a Boussinesq approximation:

$$ \boxed{ \rho = \rho_0 [1-\beta(T-T_0)] } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ \beta = -\frac1{\rho} \left(\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial T}\right)_P } $$

The buoyancy term becomes approximately:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf F_b = -\rho_0\beta(T-T_0)\mathbf g } $$

This allows warm air to rise and cold air to sink.

7.22 Humidity Effect on Density

Since:

$$ \rho=\rho(T,P,\omega) $$

humidity can also modify buoyancy.

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ T,\omega,P \rightarrow \rho \rightarrow \mathbf F_g \rightarrow \mathbf u } $$

This becomes important when there are strong humidity differences between injected/leaked air and room air.

7.23 Pressure Gradient

Pressure force per unit volume:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf F_P=-\nabla P } $$

Components:

$$ F_{P,x}=-\frac{\partial P}{\partial x} $$ $$ F_{P,y}=-\frac{\partial P}{\partial y} $$ $$ F_{P,z}=-\frac{\partial P}{\partial z} $$

Pressure gradients drive the airflow.

7.24 Pressure–Velocity Relationship

Conceptually:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla P \rightarrow \mathbf u } $$

while continuity imposes:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0 } $$

Therefore the pressure field is the field that adjusts so that the velocity field satisfies mass conservation.

7.25 Pressure Poisson Equation

For incompressible flow:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0 } $$

Take divergence of the momentum equation:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla^2P = -\rho \nabla\cdot [ (\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u ] + \nabla\cdot (\mu\nabla^2\mathbf u) + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf g) + \nabla\cdot\mathbf S_u } $$

For constant \(\rho,\mu\) and no source:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla^2P = -\rho \nabla\cdot [ (\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u ] } $$

This is the fundamental pressure-field equation in a projection-type solver.

7.26 Expanded Pressure Poisson Equation

For constant-density incompressible flow:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial^2P}{\partial x^2} + \frac{\partial^2P}{\partial y^2} + \frac{\partial^2P}{\partial z^2} = -\rho \left[ \left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{\partial v}{\partial y}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial z}\right)^2 + 2\frac{\partial u}{\partial y} \frac{\partial v}{\partial x} + 2\frac{\partial u}{\partial z} \frac{\partial w}{\partial x} + 2\frac{\partial v}{\partial z} \frac{\partial w}{\partial y} \right] } $$

This can be used for a simplified pressure solver.

7.27 Pressure Correction

For a pressure-correction algorithm:

$$ \boxed{ P^{n+1}=P^n+P' } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u^{n+1} = \mathbf u^* - \frac{\Delta t}{\rho}\nabla P' } $$

where:

\(\mathbf u^*\) = provisional velocity
\(P'\) = pressure correction.

The corrected velocity must satisfy:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla\cdot\mathbf u^{n+1}=0 } $$
7.28 Projection Method

First calculate provisional velocity:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\mathbf u^*-\mathbf u^n}{\Delta t} = -(\mathbf u^n\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u^n + \nu\nabla^2\mathbf u^n + \mathbf g + \frac{\mathbf S_u}{\rho} } $$

Then solve:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla^2P^{n+1} = \frac{\rho}{\Delta t} \nabla\cdot\mathbf u^* } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u^{n+1} = \mathbf u^* - \frac{\Delta t}{\rho} \nabla P^{n+1} } $$

This is one practical way to implement Module 7.

7.29 Pressure Boundary Conditions

At a specified-pressure inlet:

$$ \boxed{ P=P_{in} } $$

At an outlet:

$$ \boxed{ P=P_{out} } $$

At a closed wall:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial P}{\partial n} = 0 } $$

for a simple Neumann pressure condition.

7.30 Velocity Boundary Conditions

At a stationary no-slip wall:

$$ \boxed{ u=v=w=0 } $$

At an inlet:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u=\mathbf u_{in} } $$

At a specified mass-flow inlet:

$$ \boxed{ \int_A\rho\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf n\,dA = \dot m_{in} } $$

At an outlet, a suitable convective/outflow condition can be used.

7.31 Injection Momentum Source

For an injection point with mass flow:

$$ \dot m_i $$

and velocity:

$$ \mathbf u_i $$

the momentum flow rate is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot{\mathbf M}_i = \dot m_i\mathbf u_i } $$

For a source volume:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,i} = \frac{\dot m_i\mathbf u_i} {V_{source,i}} } $$

Thus:

$$ \boxed{ S_{u,x,i} = \frac{\dot m_i u_i} {V_{source,i}} } $$ $$ \boxed{ S_{u,y,i} = \frac{\dot m_i v_i} {V_{source,i}} } $$ $$ \boxed{ S_{u,z,i} = \frac{\dot m_i w_i} {V_{source,i}} } $$
7.32 Multiple Injection Points

For \(N\) injection points:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_u = \sum_{i=1}^{N} \mathbf S_{u,i} } $$

Each source may have different:

$$ \boxed{ (x_i,y_i,z_i, U_i,T_i,P_i,RH_i,\omega_i,A_i,\dot V_i,\dot m_i,\hat{\mathbf d}_i) } $$
7.33 Spatial Injection Distribution

For a Gaussian source:

$$ \boxed{ G_i(\mathbf x) = \frac{1} {(2\pi\sigma_i^2)^{3/2}} \exp \left[ -\frac{|\mathbf x-\mathbf x_i|^2} {2\sigma_i^2} \right] } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,i} = \dot m_i\mathbf u_iG_i(\mathbf x) } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ S_{m,i} = \dot m_iG_i(\mathbf x) } $$
7.34 Leakage Flow

For leakage opening \(j\):

$$ \boxed{ \Delta P_j = P_{outside,j}-P_{inside,j} } $$

Flow rate:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_{L,j} = C_dA_{L,j} \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P_j|} {\rho_{L,j}} } } $$

Mass flow:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{L,j} = \rho_{L,j}\dot V_{L,j} } $$
7.35 Leakage Velocity
$$ \boxed{ U_{L,j} = \frac{\dot V_{L,j}}{A_{L,j}} } $$

Direction:

$$ \boxed{ \hat{\mathbf d}_{L,j} = \begin{cases} \text{outside}\rightarrow\text{inside}, & P_{outside}>P_{inside} \\[4pt] \text{inside}\rightarrow\text{outside}, & P_{inside}>P_{outside} \end{cases} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u_{L,j} = U_{L,j}\hat{\mathbf d}_{L,j} } $$
7.36 Leakage Momentum Source
$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,L,j} = \frac{ \dot m_{L,j}\mathbf u_{L,j} } {V_{source,j}} } $$

For all leakage points:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,L} = \sum_j\mathbf S_{u,L,j} } $$
7.37 Time-Dependent Injection

If injection varies with time:

$$ \boxed{ U_i=U_i(t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ T_i=T_i(t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ RH_i=RH_i(t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ P_i=P_i(t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot V_i=\dot V_i(t) } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_i(t) = \rho_i(t)A_iU_i(t) } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,i} = \mathbf S_{u,i}(x,y,z,t) } $$
7.38 Time-Dependent Leakage

For a door:

$$ A_{door}(t)= \begin{cases} A_{door},&t_{open}\le t<t_{close}\\ 0,&otherwise \end{cases} $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_{door}(t) = C_dA_{door}(t) \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P(t)|}{\rho(t)} } } $$

This produces a transient airflow event.

7.39 Moisture Transport Coupling

The airflow equation provides:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u(x,y,z,t) } $$

to Module 5.

Moisture transport:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) = \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) + S_{in}+S_{leak}+S_{evap}-S_{cond} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u \rightarrow \omega } $$
7.40 Temperature Transport Coupling

Airflow also transports heat:

$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p \left( \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} + \mathbf u\cdot\nabla T \right) = \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) + S_Q + L_v(S_{cond}-S_{evap}) } $$

Thus:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u \rightarrow T } $$
7.41 Condensation Coupling

Local saturation humidity:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_s=\omega_s(T,P) } $$

Condensation:

$$ \boxed{ S_{cond} = \rho_{da}k_{cond} \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ T,P,\omega \rightarrow S_{cond} } $$

Latent heat:

$$ \boxed{ S_{latent,cond}=L_vS_{cond} } $$

which feeds back to temperature.

7.42 Evaporation Coupling
$$ \boxed{ S_{evap} = \rho_{da}k_{evap} \max(0,\omega_s-\omega) } $$

provided liquid water exists.

Then:

$$ \boxed{ S_{latent,evap} = -L_vS_{evap} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ S_{evap} \rightarrow \omega\uparrow } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ S_{evap} \rightarrow T\downarrow } $$
7.43 Inlet Humidity

For injected air:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,i} = \frac{RH_i}{100} p_{ws}(T_i) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \omega_i = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,i}} {P_i-p_{v,i}} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ T_i,P_i,RH_i \rightarrow \omega_i } $$
7.44 Inlet Latent Heat
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{latent,i} = \dot m_{da,i}\omega_iL_v } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{da,i} = \frac{\dot m_i}{1+\omega_i} } $$
7.45 Inlet Sensible Heat
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{sensible,i} = \dot m_i c_{p,i} (T_i-T_{ref}) } $$
7.46 Inlet Total Enthalpy
$$ \boxed{ h_i = c_{p,da}T_i + \omega_i (L_v+c_{p,v}T_i) } $$

Approximate engineering form:

$$ \boxed{ h_i = 1.006T_i + \omega_i(2501+1.86T_i) } $$

in \(kJ/kg_{da}\).

7.47 Total Inlet Energy Rate
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{total,i} = \dot m_{da,i}h_i } $$

This is the total energy carried by the incoming moist air.

7.48 Airflow Heat Flux

Convective heat transport:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf q_{conv} = \rho c_p\mathbf uT } $$

More rigorously for moist air, an enthalpy formulation can be used:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf J_h = \rho_{da}\mathbf u h } $$

This is preferable when the moisture and latent-energy effects are strongly coupled.

7.49 Moisture Flux
$$ \boxed{ \mathbf J_\omega = \rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega - \rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega } $$
7.50 Total Energy Flux

Using dry-air-based moist-air enthalpy:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf J_h = \rho_{da}\mathbf u h } $$

with:

$$ \boxed{ h=h(T,\omega) } $$

This gives the energy carried through each face of the cold-storage domain.

7.51 Reynolds Number

For every region:

$$ \boxed{ Re= \frac{\rho UL}{\mu} } $$

This determines whether airflow is approximately laminar or turbulent.

7.52 Prandtl Number
$$ \boxed{ Pr= \frac{\mu c_p}{k} } $$
7.53 Schmidt Number
$$ \boxed{ Sc= \frac{\mu}{\rho D_{eff}} } $$
7.54 Peclet Number

Thermal:

$$ \boxed{ Pe= \frac{UL}{\alpha} } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ \alpha=\frac{k}{\rho c_p} } $$

Moisture:

$$ \boxed{ Pe_m= \frac{UL}{D_{eff}} } $$

These indicate whether transport is dominated by advection or diffusion.

7.55 Airflow CFL Condition

For an explicit scheme:

$$ \boxed{ C_x= \frac{|u|\Delta t}{\Delta x} } $$ $$ \boxed{ C_y= \frac{|v|\Delta t}{\Delta y} } $$ $$ \boxed{ C_z= \frac{|w|\Delta t}{\Delta z} } $$

A conservative first-order condition is:

$$ \boxed{ C_x+C_y+C_z\le1 } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta t_{adv} \le \frac1{ |u|/\Delta x+ |v|/\Delta y+ |w|/\Delta z } } $$
7.56 Pressure Numerical Equation

For a 3-D finite-difference grid:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{ P_{i+1,j,k} -2P_{i,j,k} +P_{i-1,j,k} }{\Delta x^2} + \frac{ P_{i,j+1,k} -2P_{i,j,k} +P_{i,j-1,k} }{\Delta y^2} + \frac{ P_{i,j,k+1} -2P_{i,j,k} +P_{i,j,k-1} }{\Delta z^2} = S_P } $$

where \(S_P\) comes from the pressure Poisson equation.

7.57 Velocity Numerical Update

For example, the x-velocity:

$$ \boxed{ u^{n+1} = u^n + \Delta t \left[ -u\frac{\partial u}{\partial x} -v\frac{\partial u}{\partial y} -w\frac{\partial u}{\partial z} -\frac1\rho\frac{\partial P}{\partial x} + \nu\nabla^2u + g_x + \frac{S_{u,x}}{\rho} \right]^n } $$

Similarly:

$$ \boxed{ v^{n+1} = v^n + \Delta t \left[ -(\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)v -\frac1\rho\frac{\partial P}{\partial y} +\nu\nabla^2v +g_y +\frac{S_{u,y}}{\rho} \right]^n } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ w^{n+1} = w^n + \Delta t \left[ -(\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)w -\frac1\rho\frac{\partial P}{\partial z} +\nu\nabla^2w +g_z +\frac{S_{u,z}}{\rho} \right]^n } $$
7.58 Airflow Mass Balance

For the entire cold room:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{dM_{air}}{dt} = \dot M_{in} + \dot M_{leak,in} - \dot M_{out} - \dot M_{leak,out} } $$

For a steady closed room:

$$ \boxed{ \dot M_{in}=\dot M_{out} } $$
7.59 Global Continuity Error

Define:

$$ \boxed{ E_{mass} = M_{air}(t) - \left[ M_{air}(0) + M_{in} - M_{out} \right] } $$

A conservative numerical solver should produce:

$$ \boxed{ E_{mass}\rightarrow0 } $$

with grid/time-step refinement.

7.60 Pressure Drop Through a Duct

For an inlet duct:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta P = \left( f\frac{L}{D} + \sum K \right) \frac{\rho U^2}{2} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ U = \sqrt{ \frac{2\Delta P} {\rho \left( fL/D+\sum K \right)} } } $$

This can be used if your cold-storage inlet is connected to a duct or fan.

7.61 Darcy Friction Factor

For laminar flow:

$$ \boxed{ f=\frac{64}{Re} } $$

For turbulent flow, an appropriate correlation such as Colebrook can be used:

$$ \boxed{ \frac1{\sqrt f} = -2\log_{10} \left( \frac{\epsilon/D}{3.7} + \frac{2.51}{Re\sqrt f} \right) } $$
7.62 Local Air Residence Time

For a room volume \(V\) and volumetric flow rate \(\dot V\):

$$ \boxed{ \tau_{air} = \frac{V}{\dot V} } $$

Air changes per hour:

$$ \boxed{ ACH = \frac{\dot V}{V}\times3600 } $$
7.63 Air Changes With Multiple Inlets
$$ \boxed{ ACH = \frac{\sum_i\dot V_i}{V_{room}} \times3600 } $$

For leakage:

$$ \boxed{ ACH_{leak} = \frac{\sum_j\dot V_{leak,j}} {V_{room}} \times3600 } $$
7.64 Complete Coupling of Injected Air

An inlet \(i\) is defined by:

$$ \boxed{ I_i= [ x_i,y_i,z_i, U_i, \hat{\mathbf d}_i, T_i, P_i, RH_i, \omega_i, A_i, \dot V_i, \dot m_i, c_{p,i}, h_i ] } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ (T_i,P_i,RH_i) \rightarrow\omega_i } $$ $$ \boxed{ (A_i,U_i,\rho_i) \rightarrow\dot V_i,\dot m_i } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_i,\mathbf u_i \rightarrow \mathbf S_u } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_i\omega_i \rightarrow S_\omega } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_i h_i \rightarrow S_E } $$
7.65 Complete Coupling of Leakage

For leakage \(j\):

$$ \boxed{ L_j= [ x_j,y_j,z_j, A_j, P_{out,j}, P_{in,j}, T_{out,j}, RH_{out,j} ] } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta P_j \rightarrow \dot V_{L,j} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot V_{L,j} \rightarrow \dot m_{L,j} } $$ $$ \boxed{ T_{out,j},P_{out,j},RH_{out,j} \rightarrow \omega_{out,j} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{L,j}\omega_{out,j} \rightarrow S_\omega } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{L,j}\mathbf u_{L,j} \rightarrow S_u } $$
7.66 Complete Transient Coupling

At every timestep:

$$ \boxed{ t^n\rightarrow t^{n+1} } $$

the computational sequence should conceptually be:

Step 1 — Update thermodynamic properties
$$ \boxed{ T^n,P^n,\omega^n \rightarrow \rho^n,\mu^n,c_p^n,k^n } $$
Step 2 — Apply injection/leakage
$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{in}, \dot m_{leak}, \mathbf S_u, S_\omega } $$
Step 3 — Solve momentum
$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u^* } $$
Step 4 — Solve pressure
$$ \boxed{ P^{n+1} } $$
Step 5 — Correct velocity
$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u^{n+1} } $$
Step 6 — Solve heat
$$ \boxed{ T^{n+1} } $$
Step 7 — Solve moisture
$$ \boxed{ \omega^{n+1} } $$
Step 8 — Calculate condensation/evaporation
$$ \boxed{ S_{cond}^{n+1}, S_{evap}^{n+1} } $$
Step 9 — Apply latent heat
$$ \boxed{ S_{latent} = L_v(S_{cond}-S_{evap}) } $$
Step 10 — Recalculate psychrometrics
$$ \boxed{ RH,T_{dp},\omega_s,h,\rho } $$
7.67 Master Airflow Equation

For the final numerical model:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho\mathbf u)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u\otimes\mathbf u) = -\nabla P + \nabla\cdot\boldsymbol\tau + \rho\mathbf g + \sum_i\mathbf S_{u,in,i} + \sum_j\mathbf S_{u,leak,j} } $$

subject to:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u) = \sum_iS_{m,in,i} + \sum_jS_{m,leak,j} } $$

and, for the incompressible approximation:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0 } $$

with pressure determined from the pressure-velocity coupling.

7.68 Master Pressure Equation

For an incompressible projection solver:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla^2P^{n+1} = \frac{\rho}{\Delta t} \nabla\cdot\mathbf u^* } $$

followed by:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u^{n+1} = \mathbf u^* - \frac{\Delta t}{\rho} \nabla P^{n+1} } $$

such that:

$$ \boxed{ \nabla\cdot\mathbf u^{n+1}=0 } $$
7.69 Master Coupled System for Your Cold Storage

The complete numerical twin now has the following principal equations:

Continuity
$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u) = S_m } $$
Momentum
$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho\mathbf u)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u\otimes\mathbf u) = -\nabla P + \nabla\cdot\boldsymbol\tau + \rho\mathbf g + \mathbf S_u } $$
Energy
$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p \frac{DT}{Dt} = \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) + S_Q + L_v(S_{cond}-S_{evap}) } $$
Moisture
$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) = \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) + S_{in} + S_{leak} + S_{evap} - S_{cond} } $$
Psychrometrics
$$ \boxed{ p_v= \frac{\omega P}{0.621945+\omega} } $$ $$ \boxed{ RH= 100\frac{p_v}{p_{ws}(T)} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \omega_s= 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws}(T)} {P-p_{ws}(T)} } $$
Condensation
$$ \boxed{ S_{cond} = \rho_{da}k_{cond} \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$
Evaporation
$$ \boxed{ S_{evap} = \rho_{da}k_{evap} \max(0,\omega_s-\omega) } $$
7.70 Overall Digital-Twin Coupling

The complete physics can now be represented as:

                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │ Injection Points │
                    │ U,T,P,RH,ω,Q,ṁ   │
                    └────────┬─────────┘
                             │
                             ▼
                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │ Leakage / Door   │
                    │ ΔP,A,T,RH,U       │
                    └────────┬─────────┘
                             │
                             ▼
                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │   CONTINUITY     │
                    │  ∂ρ/∂t + ∇·ρu   │
                    └────────┬─────────┘
                             │
                             ▼
                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │    MOMENTUM      │
                    │ Navier–Stokes    │
                    └────────┬─────────┘
                             │
                             ▼
                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │ PRESSURE FIELD   │
                    │   ∇²P equation   │
                    └────────┬─────────┘
                             │
                             ▼
                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │ VELOCITY FIELD   │
                    │ u(x,y,z,t)       │
                    └──────┬─────┬─────┘
                           │     │
                    ┌──────▼─┐ ┌─▼────────┐
                    │  HEAT  │ │ MOISTURE │
                    │ Module4│ │ Module 5 │
                    └────┬───┘ └────┬─────┘
                         │           │
                         └─────┬─────┘
                               ▼
                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │ CONDENSATION /   │
                    │ EVAPORATION      │
                    │    Module 6      │
                    └────────┬─────────┘
                             │
                       Latent heat
                             │
                             ▼
                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │ Temperature T    │
                    │ & density ρ      │
                    └────────┬─────────┘
                             │
                             └──────► AIRFLOW

The resulting fields are:

$$ \boxed{ T(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ P(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ u(x,y,z,t),v(x,y,z,t),w(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \omega(x,y,z,t) } $$

and derived fields:

$$ \boxed{ RH(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ T_{dp}(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_{cond}(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_{evap}(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ Q_{latent}(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ Q_{sensible}(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ Q_{total}(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \rho(x,y,z,t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ U(x,y,z,t) } $$

This makes Module 7 the airflow/pressure backbone of the digital twin: Modules 4–6 use its velocity field for advection, while temperature and humidity feed back into density, buoyancy, pressure and therefore airflow.




















Module 8 — Air Injection Model

Module 8 defines how air enters the cold-storage domain, where it enters, how much enters, its velocity, direction, temperature, pressure, humidity, sensible/latent/total energy, and how all of these quantities vary with time.

The injection field should ultimately be represented as:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{in}=\mathbf S_{in}(x,y,z,t) } $$

and the inlet state as:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf I_i(t)= [x_i,y_i,z_i,A_i,U_i,\hat{\mathbf d}_i,T_i,P_i,\omega_i,RH_i, \dot V_i,\dot m_i,c_{p,i},h_i,\dot Q_i] } $$

for injection point \(i\).

8.1 Injection Location

For injection point \(i\):

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf x_i=(x_i,y_i,z_i) } $$

At time \(t\):

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf x_i(t) } $$

if the injection location itself changes.

For a stationary inlet:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf x_i(t)=\mathbf x_i } $$
8.2 Injection Area
$$ \boxed{ A_i } $$

For a circular inlet:

$$ \boxed{ A_i=\frac{\pi D_i^2}{4} } $$

For a rectangular inlet:

$$ \boxed{ A_i=W_iH_i } $$
8.3 Injection Velocity

The inlet speed is:

$$ \boxed{ U_i=|\mathbf u_i| } $$

with:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u_i=U_i\hat{\mathbf d}_i } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ |\hat{\mathbf d}_i|=1 } $$

Therefore:

$$ u_i=U_id_{x,i} $$ $$ v_i=U_id_{y,i} $$ $$ w_i=U_id_{z,i} $$
8.4 Volumetric Flow Rate

If velocity is known:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_i=A_iU_i } $$

If the flow rate is known:

$$ \boxed{ U_i=\frac{\dot V_i}{A_i} } $$

For time-varying injection:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_i=\dot V_i(t) } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ U_i(t)=\frac{\dot V_i(t)}{A_i(t)} } $$
8.5 Injection Mass Flow Rate
$$ \boxed{ \dot m_i=\rho_i\dot V_i } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_i=\rho_iA_iU_i } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_i=\rho(T_i,P_i,\omega_i) } $$
8.6 Dry-Air Mass Flow Rate

Since:

$$ \omega_i=\frac{m_{v,i}}{m_{da,i}} $$

we have:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{da,i} = \frac{\dot m_i}{1+\omega_i} } $$
8.7 Water-Vapor Mass Flow Rate
$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,i} = \dot m_{da,i}\omega_i } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,i} = \frac{\dot m_i\omega_i} {1+\omega_i} } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,i} = \frac{\rho_iA_iU_i\omega_i} {1+\omega_i} } $$

This is the moisture actually injected into the cold room.

8.8 Injection Temperature

The inlet temperature can be:

$$ \boxed{ T_i=T_i(t) } $$

and may be different for every inlet:

$$ \boxed{ T_1\neq T_2\neq T_3 } $$

The temperature field after injection becomes:

$$ \boxed{ T=T(x,y,z,t) } $$
8.9 Injection Pressure
$$ \boxed{ P_i=P_i(t) } $$

Pressure difference relative to the room:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta P_i=P_i-P_{room} } $$

This pressure difference can determine the flow rate when injection is pressure-driven.

8.10 Pressure-Driven Injection

For an orifice:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_i = C_{d,i}A_i \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P_i|} {\rho_i} } } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_i = \rho_iC_{d,i}A_i \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P_i|} {\rho_i} } } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ U_i= C_{d,i} \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P_i|} {\rho_i} } } $$
8.11 Injection Humidity From RH

If inlet RH is specified:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,i} = \frac{RH_i}{100}p_{ws}(T_i) } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_i = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,i}} {P_i-p_{v,i}} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ RH_i,T_i,P_i \rightarrow \omega_i } $$
8.12 Injection Humidity From Dew Point

If dew-point temperature \(T_{dp,i}\) is specified:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,i}=p_{ws}(T_{dp,i}) } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_i = 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws}(T_{dp,i})} {P_i-p_{ws}(T_{dp,i})} } $$
8.13 Injection Humidity From Absolute Humidity

If water-vapor density is specified:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_{v,i} = \frac{p_{v,i}}{R_vT_{K,i}} } $$

therefore:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,i} = \rho_{v,i}R_vT_{K,i} } $$

and then:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_i = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,i}} {P_i-p_{v,i}} } $$
8.14 Injection Relative Humidity After Conversion

Once \(\omega_i\) is known:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,i} = \frac{\omega_iP_i} {0.621945+\omega_i} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ RH_i = 100 \frac{p_{v,i}} {p_{ws}(T_i)} } $$
8.15 Injection Enthalpy

Moist-air specific enthalpy on a dry-air basis:

$$ \boxed{ h_i = 1.006T_i+ \omega_i(2501+1.86T_i) } $$

in:

$$ kJ/kg_{da} $$

More generally:

$$ \boxed{ h_i = c_{p,da}T_i+ \omega_i (L_v+c_{p,v}T_i) } $$
8.16 Injection Specific Heat

Moist-air specific heat on a dry-air basis:

$$ \boxed{ c_{p,h,i} = c_{p,da} + \omega_i c_{p,v} } $$

On a total moist-air mass basis:

$$ \boxed{ c_{p,ma,i} = \frac{ c_{p,da}+\omega_i c_{p,v} } {1+\omega_i} } $$
8.17 Injection Sensible Heat Rate

Relative to reference temperature \(T_{ref}\):

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{s,i} = \dot m_i c_{p,ma,i} (T_i-T_{ref}) } $$

or on a dry-air basis:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{s,i} = \dot m_{da,i} c_{p,h,i} (T_i-T_{ref}) } $$
8.18 Injection Latent Energy

The latent component is:

$$ \boxed{ h_{latent,i} = \omega_iL_v } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{latent,i} = \dot m_{da,i}\omega_iL_v } $$
8.19 Injection Total Energy
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{total,i} = \dot m_{da,i}h_i } $$

Approximately:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{total,i} = \dot Q_{s,i} + \dot Q_{latent,i} } $$

The enthalpy formulation is preferable when the air stream contains significant moisture.

8.20 Injection Energy Source

For a source volume \(V_{src,i}\):

$$ \boxed{ S_{E,i} = \frac{\dot m_{da,i}h_i} {V_{src,i}} } $$

If using a temperature equation plus explicit latent source, use the corresponding sensible component consistently and avoid double-counting latent energy.

8.21 Injection Moisture Source
$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,i} = \frac{\dot m_{v,i}} {V_{src,i}} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,i} = \frac{ \dot m_{da,i}\omega_i } {V_{src,i}} } $$
8.22 Injection Mass Source
$$ \boxed{ S_{m,i} = \frac{\dot m_i} {V_{src,i}} } $$
8.23 Injection Momentum Source

Momentum flow:

$$ \boxed{ \dot{\mathbf M}_i = \dot m_i\mathbf u_i } $$

Source term:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,i} = \frac{\dot m_i\mathbf u_i} {V_{src,i}} } $$
8.24 Multiple Injection Points

For \(N\) injection points:

$$ \boxed{ S_m=\sum_{i=1}^{N}S_{m,i} } $$ $$ \boxed{ S_\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{N}S_{\omega,i} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_u = \sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf S_{u,i} } $$ $$ \boxed{ S_E=\sum_{i=1}^{N}S_{E,i} } $$
8.25 Spatial Injection Source

For an ideal point source:

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,i}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_{v,i}(t) \delta(\mathbf x-\mathbf x_i) } $$

For numerical implementation, a Gaussian source can be used:

$$ \boxed{ G_i(\mathbf x) = \frac{1} {(2\pi\sigma_i^2)^{3/2}} e^{-\frac{|\mathbf x-\mathbf x_i|^2}{2\sigma_i^2}} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,i}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_{v,i}(t)G_i(\mathbf x) } $$

Similarly:

$$ \boxed{ S_{m,i}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_i(t)G_i(\mathbf x) } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,i}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_i(t)\mathbf u_i(t)G_i(\mathbf x) } $$
8.26 Time-Dependent Injection

Every inlet parameter can vary with time:

$$ \boxed{ U_i=U_i(t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ T_i=T_i(t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ P_i=P_i(t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ RH_i=RH_i(t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ A_i=A_i(t) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot V_i=\dot V_i(t) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_i(t) = \rho_i(t)A_i(t)U_i(t) } $$
8.27 Injection Condensation Immediately at the Inlet

Incoming air can itself become supersaturated after mixing/cooling.

At the inlet:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{i}>\omega_s(T_i,P_i) } $$

implies potential condensation.

After mixing:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{mix}>\omega_s(T_{mix},P_{mix}) } $$

implies condensation in the local mixing region.

8.28 Injection Mixing

For two air streams:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{da,mix} = \dot m_{da,1} + \dot m_{da,2} } $$

Humidity ratio:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{mix} = \frac{ \dot m_{da,1}\omega_1+ \dot m_{da,2}\omega_2 } { \dot m_{da,1}+\dot m_{da,2} } } $$

Enthalpy:

$$ \boxed{ h_{mix} = \frac{ \dot m_{da,1}h_1+ \dot m_{da,2}h_2 } { \dot m_{da,1}+\dot m_{da,2} } } $$

Then solve:

$$ \boxed{ h_{mix}=h(T_{mix},\omega_{mix}) } $$

to obtain the mixed temperature.

8.29 Injection-Induced Moisture Transport

The injected vapor is subsequently transported according to:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) = \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) + S_{\omega,in} + S_{evap} - S_{cond} } $$
Module 9 — Air Leakage / Infiltration Model

Module 9 describes air entering or leaving the cold storage through:

wall leakage
cracks
door gaps
opened doors
seals
vents
pressure-driven infiltration
intentional exhaust
accidental openings.

The fundamental quantities are:

$$ \boxed{ A_L,\Delta P_L,C_d,\rho_L,\dot V_L,\dot m_L, T_L,P_L,RH_L,\omega_L } $$

at location:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf x_L=(x_L,y_L,z_L) } $$

and time:

$$ \boxed{ t } $$
9.1 Leakage Pressure Difference
$$ \boxed{ \Delta P_L = P_{outside}-P_{inside} } $$

If:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta P_L>0 } $$

outside air enters.

If:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta P_L<0 } $$

air leaves the room.

9.2 Leakage Opening Area
$$ \boxed{ A_L=A_L(t) } $$

For a door:

$$ \boxed{ A_L(t)=A_{door} } $$

while open and:

$$ \boxed{ A_L(t)=0 } $$

while closed, in a simple model.

9.3 Leakage Volumetric Flow Rate

For an orifice-type opening:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_L = C_dA_L \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P_L|} {\rho_L} } } $$
9.4 Leakage Velocity
$$ \boxed{ U_L = \frac{\dot V_L}{A_L} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ U_L = C_d \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P_L|} {\rho_L} } } $$
9.5 Leakage Mass Flow Rate
$$ \boxed{ \dot m_L = \rho_L\dot V_L } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_L = C_dA_L \sqrt{ 2\rho_L|\Delta P_L| } } $$
9.6 Leakage Direction

Define the outward normal:

$$ \hat{\mathbf n}_L $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u_L = U_L\hat{\mathbf d}_L } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ \hat{\mathbf d}_L= \begin{cases} -\hat{\mathbf n}_L,&P_{outside}>P_{inside}\\ +\hat{\mathbf n}_L,&P_{inside}>P_{outside} \end{cases} } $$

depending on the chosen normal convention.

9.7 Leakage Air Temperature

Outside/leaked air may have:

$$ \boxed{ T_L=T_L(x_L,t) } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ T_L\neq T_{room} } $$

This temperature difference produces sensible heat transfer.

9.8 Leakage Air Pressure
$$ \boxed{ P_L=P_L(x_L,t) } $$

Usually the absolute pressure is used for psychrometric calculations.

Pressure difference is:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta P_L=P_L-P_{room} } $$
9.9 Leakage Air Relative Humidity
$$ \boxed{ RH_L=RH_L(x_L,t) } $$

Vapor pressure:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,L} = \frac{RH_L}{100} p_{ws}(T_L) } $$
9.10 Leakage Humidity Ratio
$$ \boxed{ \omega_L = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,L}} {P_L-p_{v,L}} } $$
9.11 Leakage Dry-Air Flow Rate
$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{da,L} = \frac{\dot m_L} {1+\omega_L} } $$
9.12 Leakage Water-Vapor Flow Rate
$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,L} = \dot m_{da,L}\omega_L } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,L} = \frac{ \dot m_L\omega_L } {1+\omega_L} } $$
9.13 Leakage Moisture Source

If outside air enters:

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,L} = \frac{\dot m_{v,L}} {V_{src,L}} } $$

or, for a boundary face, directly impose the vapor mass flux:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m''_{v,L} = \rho_{da,L}U_{n,L}\omega_L } $$
9.14 Leakage Momentum
$$ \boxed{ \dot{\mathbf M}_L = \dot m_L\mathbf u_L } $$

Volumetric momentum source:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,L} = \frac{ \dot m_L\mathbf u_L } {V_{src,L}} } $$
9.15 Leakage Sensible Heat

The sensible heat brought into the room is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{s,L} = \dot m_Lc_{p,ma,L} (T_L-T_{room,ref}) } $$

If comparing directly with local room air:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{s,L} = \dot m_Lc_{p,ma,L} (T_L-T_{local}) } $$
9.16 Leakage Latent Energy
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{latent,L} = \dot m_{da,L}\omega_LL_v } $$
9.17 Leakage Total Enthalpy
$$ \boxed{ h_L = 1.006T_L+ \omega_L(2501+1.86T_L) } $$

Total energy rate:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{total,L} = \dot m_{da,L}h_L } $$
9.18 Leakage Heat Transfer Relative to Room

The net enthalpy transport can be written:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{L} = \dot m_{da,L} (h_L-h_{room}) } $$

This is preferable when the purpose is to determine the net energy effect of infiltration.

9.19 Leakage Moisture Transport

The leaked air becomes a source in the moisture equation:

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,total} = S_{\omega,in} + S_{\omega,leak} + S_{evap} - S_{cond} } $$
9.20 Door Opening Model

For a door that opens at \(t_o\) and closes at \(t_c\):

$$ \boxed{ A_L(t)= \begin{cases} A_{door},& t_o\le t<t_c\\ 0,&\text{otherwise} \end{cases} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_L(t) = C_dA_L(t) \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P_L(t)|} {\rho_L(t)} } } $$
9.21 Door Opening With Variable Opening Area

For partial opening:

$$ \boxed{ A_L(t)=A_{door}f_{open}(t) } $$

where:

$$ 0\le f_{open}(t)\le1 $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_L(t) = C_dA_{door}f_{open}(t) \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P_L(t)|} {\rho_L(t)} } } $$
9.22 Multiple Leakage Locations

For \(N_L\) leakage points:

$$ \boxed{ \dot M_L = \sum_{j=1}^{N_L}\dot m_{L,j} } $$

Moisture:

$$ \boxed{ \dot M_{v,L} = \sum_{j=1}^{N_L} \dot m_{v,L,j} } $$

Momentum:

$$ \boxed{ \dot{\mathbf M}_L = \sum_{j=1}^{N_L} \dot m_{L,j}\mathbf u_{L,j} } $$

Energy:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_L = \sum_{j=1}^{N_L} \dot m_{da,L,j} (h_{L,j}-h_{room,j}) } $$
9.23 Spatial Leakage Source

For leakage point \(j\):

$$ \boxed{ S_{m,L,j}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_{L,j}(t) \delta(\mathbf x-\mathbf x_{L,j}) } $$

For a Gaussian representation:

$$ \boxed{ S_{m,L,j}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_{L,j}(t)G_j(\mathbf x) } $$

Moisture:

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,L,j}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_{v,L,j}(t)G_j(\mathbf x) } $$

Momentum:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,L,j} = \dot m_{L,j}\mathbf u_{L,j}G_j(\mathbf x) } $$
9.24 Leakage-Induced Condensation

The most important phenomenon in cold storage is often:

$$ \boxed{ T_{outside}>T_{room} } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ RH_{outside}\gg RH_{room} } $$

The incoming vapor can therefore raise:

$$ \omega $$

locally.

Condensation occurs if:

$$ \boxed{ \omega>\omega_s(T,P) } $$

or at a surface:

$$ \boxed{ T_s<T_{dp} } $$
9.25 Leakage Condensation Rate

Once leaked moisture enters the room:

$$ \boxed{ S_{cond} = \rho_{da}k_{cond} \max(0,\omega-\omega_s) } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{cond} = S_{cond}V_c } $$
9.26 Leakage-Induced Evaporation

If leaked air mixes with an already wet region:

$$ \boxed{ \omega<\omega_s } $$

and liquid water exists:

$$ \boxed{ M_l>0 } $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ S_{evap} = \rho_{da}k_{evap} \max(0,\omega_s-\omega) } $$
9.27 Latent Heat From Leakage-Induced Condensation
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{cond} = \dot m_{cond}L_v } $$

Volumetric source:

$$ \boxed{ S_{latent,cond} = L_vS_{cond} } $$
9.28 Latent Heat From Evaporation
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{evap} = \dot m_{evap}L_v } $$

Energy sink:

$$ \boxed{ S_{latent,evap} = -L_vS_{evap} } $$
9.29 Net Phase-Change Heat
$$ \boxed{ S_{latent} = L_v(S_{cond}-S_{evap}) } $$
9.30 Leakage and Pressure Feedback

Leakage depends on:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta P=P_{outside}-P_{inside} } $$

but pressure itself changes because of air entering/leaving:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{in}-\dot m_{out} \rightarrow P(t) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ P(t) \rightarrow \dot V_L(t) \rightarrow \dot m_L(t) \rightarrow P(t+\Delta t) } $$

This is a transient feedback loop.

9.31 Leakage and Temperature Feedback

Leakage changes temperature:

$$ \boxed{ T_{outside} \rightarrow \dot Q_L \rightarrow T_{room} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ T_{room} \rightarrow \rho \rightarrow \Delta P \rightarrow \dot V_L } $$
9.32 Leakage and Humidity Feedback

Similarly:

$$ \boxed{ RH_{outside},T_{outside} \rightarrow \omega_{outside} } $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{outside} \rightarrow \omega_{room} } $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ T_{room},P_{room},\omega_{room} \rightarrow RH_{room},T_{dp} } $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ RH,T_{dp} \rightarrow condensation } $$
9.33 Complete Injection + Leakage Mass Balance

For the entire room:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{dM_{air}}{dt} = \sum_i\dot m_{in,i} + \sum_j\dot m_{leak,in,j} - \dot M_{out} - \sum_j\dot m_{leak,out,j} } $$
9.34 Complete Water-Vapor Balance
$$ \boxed{ \frac{dM_v}{dt} = \sum_i\dot m_{v,in,i} + \sum_j\dot m_{v,leak,in,j} + \dot M_{evap} - \dot M_{cond} - \dot M_{v,out} } $$
9.35 Complete Liquid-Water Balance
$$ \boxed{ \frac{dM_l}{dt} = \dot M_{cond} - \dot M_{evap} - \dot M_{drain} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{d}{dt}(M_v+M_l) = \dot M_{v,in} + \dot M_{v,leak,in} - \dot M_{v,out} - \dot M_{drain} } $$
9.36 Complete Energy Balance

The room-level energy balance can be expressed consistently using enthalpy:

$$ \boxed{ \frac{dE}{dt} = \dot Q_{wall} + \sum_i\dot Q_{in,i} + \sum_j\dot Q_{leak,j} - \dot Q_{out} + \dot Q_{internal} } $$

If using an explicit temperature + vapor formulation, the phase-change contribution is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{phase} = L_v (\dot M_{cond}-\dot M_{evap}) } $$

and must be included exactly once.

9.37 Master Injection Equation Set

For every injection \(i\):

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_i=A_iU_i } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_i=\rho_iA_iU_i } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_{da,i} = \frac{\rho_iA_iU_i} {1+\omega_i} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,i} = \frac{\rho_iA_iU_i\omega_i} {1+\omega_i} } $$ $$ \boxed{ p_{v,i} = \frac{RH_i}{100}p_{ws}(T_i) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \omega_i = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,i}} {P_i-p_{v,i}} } $$ $$ \boxed{ h_i = 1.006T_i+ \omega_i(2501+1.86T_i) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{total,i} = \dot m_{da,i}h_i } $$ $$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,i} = \frac{\dot m_i\mathbf u_i} {V_{src,i}} } $$ $$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,i} = \frac{\dot m_{v,i}} {V_{src,i}} } $$
9.38 Master Leakage Equation Set

For every leakage point \(j\):

$$ \boxed{ \Delta P_j=P_{out,j}-P_{in,j} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot V_{L,j} = C_{d,j}A_{L,j} \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P_j|} {\rho_{L,j}} } } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_{L,j} = \rho_{L,j}\dot V_{L,j} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \omega_{L,j} = 0.621945 \frac{ (RH_{L,j}/100)p_{ws}(T_{L,j}) }{ P_{L,j} - (RH_{L,j}/100)p_{ws}(T_{L,j}) } } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,L,j} = \frac{ \dot m_{L,j}\omega_{L,j} } {1+\omega_{L,j}} } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{L,j} = \dot m_{da,L,j} (h_{L,j}-h_{room,j}) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,L,j} = \frac{ \dot m_{L,j}\mathbf u_{L,j} } {V_{src,L,j}} } $$ $$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,L,j} = \frac{ \dot m_{v,L,j} } {V_{src,L,j}} } $$
9.39 Final Coupling of Modules 7, 8 and 9

The airflow module provides:

$$ \boxed{ P(x,y,z,t),\quad \mathbf u(x,y,z,t) } $$

Module 8 provides:

$$ \boxed{ S_{in}(x,y,z,t) } $$

Module 9 provides:

$$ \boxed{ S_{leak}(x,y,z,t) } $$

These enter the governing equations:

Continuity
$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u) = S_{m,in}+S_{m,leak} } $$
Momentum
$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho\mathbf u)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u\otimes\mathbf u) = -\nabla P + \nabla\cdot\boldsymbol\tau + \rho\mathbf g + \mathbf S_{u,in} + \mathbf S_{u,leak} } $$
Heat
$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p \frac{DT}{Dt} = \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) + S_Q + L_v(S_{cond}-S_{evap}) } $$
Moisture
$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) = \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) + S_{\omega,in} + S_{\omega,leak} + S_{evap} - S_{cond} } $$
9.40 Complete Digital-Twin State

At every computational location:

$$ \boxed{ (x,y,z) } $$

and every time:

$$ \boxed{ t } $$

the state becomes:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf Q(x,y,z,t) = [ T,P,u,v,w,\omega, \rho,RH,T_{dp},h, S_{cond},S_{evap} ] } $$

while every injection is:

$$ \boxed{ I_i(x_i,y_i,z_i,t) = [ A_i,U_i,\mathbf d_i,T_i,P_i,RH_i,\omega_i, \dot V_i,\dot m_i,c_{p,i},h_i, \dot Q_{s,i},\dot Q_{l,i},\dot Q_{total,i} ] } $$

and every leakage is:

$$ \boxed{ L_j(x_j,y_j,z_j,t) = [ A_j,\Delta P_j,U_j,T_j,P_j,RH_j,\omega_j, \dot V_j,\dot m_j, \dot Q_{s,j},\dot Q_{l,j},\dot Q_{total,j} ] } $$
9.41 Overall Physics Flow
                 MODULE 8
              AIR INJECTION
                    │
       ┌────────────┼─────────────┐
       │            │             │
       ▼            ▼             ▼
   Velocity       T, P, RH       Flow rate
       │            │             │
       │            ▼             │
       │          ω, h, ρ         │
       │            │             │
       └────────────┼─────────────┘
                    ▼
             Mass + Momentum
                    │
                    ▼
              MODULE 7
          AIRFLOW + PRESSURE
                    │
                    ▼
           u(x,y,z,t), P(x,y,z,t)
                    │
        ┌───────────┴───────────┐
        ▼                       ▼
   MODULE 5                 MODULE 4
 MOISTURE TRANSPORT       HEAT TRANSFER
        │                       │
        └───────────┬───────────┘
                    ▼
              MODULE 6
      CONDENSATION / EVAPORATION
                    │
          ┌─────────┴─────────┐
          ▼                   ▼
     Condensation         Evaporation
          │                   │
       + Lv·m               - Lv·m
          │                   │
          └─────────┬─────────┘
                    ▼
               Temperature
                    │
                    ▼
                 Density
                    │
                    ▼
              AIRFLOW FIELD


                 MODULE 9
          LEAKAGE / INFILTRATION
                    │
              ΔP, A, Cd, ρ
                    │
                    ▼
              Leakage flow
                    │
       ┌────────────┼────────────┐
       ▼            ▼            ▼
   Momentum      Moisture      Energy
       │            │            │
       └────────────┼────────────┘
                    ▼
          Coupled 3-D solution
The key distinction between Modules 8 and 9

Module 8 — Air Injection is generally a controlled/intentional source:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_{in},U_{in},T_{in},P_{in},RH_{in},A_{in} } $$

while Module 9 — Leakage is generally a pressure-driven uncontrolled flow:

$$ \boxed{ \Delta P,A_{leak},C_d,\rho \rightarrow \dot V_{leak} } $$

Both ultimately produce:

$$ \boxed{ S_m,\quad \mathbf S_u,\quad S_\omega,\quad S_E } $$

which are coupled into the continuity, momentum, moisture and energy equations.

One implementation recommendation: represent deliberate air injection primarily as a boundary condition/mass-flow inlet rather than a volumetric point source whenever the physical inlet has a real opening. Use Gaussian/volumetric sources only when you intentionally want a sub-grid point/plume representation. This will make the numerical model more conservative and physically interpretable.


















Modules 10–12: Door Opening / Leakage Event, Wall Heat Transfer & Internal Heat Sources

For the cold-storage numerical digital twin, these three modules should be treated as transient source/boundary modules. Their outputs ultimately enter the coupled conservation equations for mass, momentum, moisture, and energy.

I will use:

\(x,y,z\): spatial coordinates [m]
\(t\): time [s]
\(T\): temperature [°C] unless \(T_K\) is explicitly used [K]
\(P\): absolute pressure [Pa]
\(\mathbf u=(u,v,w)\): velocity [m/s]
\(\omega\): humidity ratio [kg\(_v\)/kg\(_{da}\)]
\(RH\): relative humidity [%]
\(V_c\): computational-cell volume [m³]
\(\dot V\): volumetric flow rate [m³/s]
\(\dot m\): mass flow rate [kg/s]
\(h\): specific enthalpy [J/kg or kJ/kg\(_{da}\)]
\(c_p\): specific heat [J/(kg·K)]
\(L_v\): latent heat of vaporization [J/kg]
\(k\): thermal conductivity [W/(m·K)]
10. Door Opening / Leakage Event

A door-opening event is fundamentally a transient infiltration boundary condition.

When the door opens, outside air enters or inside air exits depending on the pressure difference, while simultaneously transporting:

sensible heat
moisture
latent energy
mass
momentum

Therefore, door opening should not simply be represented as a fixed temperature boundary.

10.1 Door Geometry

Define the door by:

$$ \mathbf x_d=(x_d,y_d,z_d) $$

Door dimensions:

$$ A_d=W_dH_d $$

where:

\(W_d\): door width [m]
\(H_d\): door height [m]
\(A_d\): door opening area [m²]

If only part of the door is open:

$$ A_{open}(t)=f_{open}(t)A_d $$

where:

$$ 0\le f_{open}(t)\le1 $$

For a fully closed door:

$$ f_{open}(t)=0 $$

For a fully open door:

$$ f_{open}(t)=1 $$
10.2 Door Opening Function

Define the door state:

$$ D(t)= \begin{cases} 0,&\text{closed}\\ 1,&\text{open} \end{cases} $$

Then:

$$ A_{open}(t)=D(t)A_d $$

For an opening event from \(t_o\) to \(t_c\):

$$ D(t)= \begin{cases} 1,&t_o\le t<t_c\\ 0,&\text{otherwise} \end{cases} $$

Hence:

$$ A_{open}(t)= \begin{cases} A_d,&t_o\le t<t_c\\ 0,&\text{otherwise} \end{cases} $$
10.3 Pressure Difference Across Door

The driving pressure difference is:

$$ \Delta P_d(t)=P_{out}(t)-P_{in}(t) $$

where:

\(P_{out}\): outside pressure
\(P_{in}\): cold-room pressure

The sign determines flow direction.

Outside → inside
$$ P_{out}>P_{in} $$
Inside → outside
$$ P_{in}>P_{out} $$
10.4 Door Airflow

For an orifice-type opening:

$$ \boxed{ \dot V_d(t) = C_dA_{open}(t) \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P_d(t)|}{\rho_d(t)} } } $$

where:

\(C_d\): discharge coefficient
\(\rho_d\): density of entering/leaving air

Mass flow:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_d(t)=\rho_d(t)\dot V_d(t) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_d(t) = C_dA_{open}(t) \sqrt{2\rho_d(t)|\Delta P_d(t)|} } $$
10.5 Door Air Velocity

Average velocity through the door:

$$ \boxed{ U_d(t)=\frac{\dot V_d(t)}{A_{open}(t)} } $$

or directly:

$$ \boxed{ U_d(t) = C_d \sqrt{ \frac{2|\Delta P_d(t)|}{\rho_d(t)} } } $$

Velocity vector:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf u_d(t)=U_d(t)\hat{\mathbf n}_d } $$

where \(\hat{\mathbf n}_d\) is the door normal vector.

10.6 Outside-Air Psychrometrics

At the door, outside conditions are:

$$ T_{out}(t),\quad P_{out}(t),\quad RH_{out}(t) $$

Saturation pressure:

$$ p_{ws,out}=p_{ws}(T_{out}) $$

Water-vapor partial pressure:

$$ \boxed{ p_{v,out} = \frac{RH_{out}}{100}p_{ws,out} } $$

Humidity ratio:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_{out} = 0.621945 \frac{p_{v,out}} {P_{out}-p_{v,out}} } $$

Dry-air mass flow:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{da,d} = \frac{\dot m_d}{1+\omega_{out}} } $$

Water-vapor mass flow:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,d} = \dot m_{da,d}\omega_{out} } $$
10.7 Door Sensible Heat Load

Using moist-air specific heat:

$$ c_{p,ma} = \frac{c_{p,da}+\omega c_{p,v}} {1+\omega} $$

The sensible heat carried by infiltration is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{s,d} = \dot m_d c_{p,ma,out} (T_{out}-T_{in}) } $$

For the cold room, this becomes a heat gain when:

$$ T_{out}>T_{in} $$
10.8 Door Enthalpy

Moist-air enthalpy:

$$ \boxed{ h = 1.006T+\omega(2501+1.86T) } $$

in kJ/kg dry air.

Thus:

$$ h_{out} = 1.006T_{out} + \omega_{out}(2501+1.86T_{out}) $$

and room air:

$$ h_{in} = 1.006T_{in} + \omega_{in}(2501+1.86T_{in}) $$

The net infiltration energy rate is:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{door} = \dot m_{da,d} (h_{out}-h_{in}) } $$

This is generally the preferred energy formulation because it automatically includes sensible + latent effects.

10.9 Door Latent Heat

Water vapor entering the room carries latent energy:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{latent,d} = \dot m_{v,d}L_v } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{latent,d} = \dot m_{da,d}\omega_{out}L_v } $$

However, if the energy equation uses the enthalpy difference:

$$ \dot Q_{door} = \dot m_{da,d}(h_{out}-h_{in}) $$

do not add \(\dot Q_{latent,d}\) again, otherwise latent energy is double-counted.

10.10 Door Moisture Source

Volumetric moisture source:

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,d} = \frac{\dot m_{v,d}} {V_{source,d}} } $$

If represented spatially using a source distribution \(G_d\):

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,d}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_{v,d}(t)G_d(\mathbf x) } $$

where:

$$ \int_\Omega G_d(\mathbf x)dV=1 $$
10.11 Door Mass Source
$$ \boxed{ S_{m,d}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_d(t)G_d(\mathbf x) } $$

For a finite-volume source cell:

$$ \boxed{ S_{m,d} = \frac{\dot m_d}{V_c} } $$
10.12 Door Momentum Source

Momentum entering:

$$ \boxed{ \dot{\mathbf M}_d = \dot m_d\mathbf u_d } $$

Volumetric momentum source:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,d} = \frac{\dot m_d\mathbf u_d}{V_{source,d}} } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_{u,d}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot m_d(t)\mathbf u_d(t)G_d(\mathbf x) } $$
10.13 Door Energy Source

Using dry-air enthalpy:

$$ \boxed{ S_{E,d} = \frac{ \dot m_{da,d}(h_{out}-h_{in}) } {V_{source,d}} } $$

If absolute incoming energy is required:

$$ \boxed{ S_{E,in,d} = \frac{\dot m_{da,d}h_{out}} {V_{source,d}} } $$
10.14 Door Opening Duration

If the door is open for:

$$ \Delta t_d=t_c-t_o $$

the infiltrated volume is:

$$ \boxed{ V_{inf} = \int_{t_o}^{t_c}\dot V_d(t)\,dt } $$

Infiltrated mass:

$$ \boxed{ M_{inf} = \int_{t_o}^{t_c}\dot m_d(t)\,dt } $$

Infiltrated vapor:

$$ \boxed{ M_{v,inf} = \int_{t_o}^{t_c}\dot m_{v,d}(t)\,dt } $$

Total infiltration energy:

$$ \boxed{ E_{inf} = \int_{t_o}^{t_c} \dot Q_{door}(t)\,dt } $$
10.15 Door Condensation

After humid outside air enters:

$$ \omega>\omega_s(T,P) $$

may occur locally.

Saturation humidity ratio:

$$ \boxed{ \omega_s = 0.621945 \frac{p_{ws}(T)} {P-p_{ws}(T)} } $$

Condensation occurs when:

$$ \boxed{ \omega>\omega_s } $$

or:

$$ \boxed{ RH>100\% } $$

Condensed mass:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{cond} = \rho_{da}k_{cond} \max(0,\omega-\omega_s)V_c } $$

Latent heat released:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{cond} = \dot m_{cond}L_v } $$

Cumulative condensation:

$$ \boxed{ M_{cond}(t) = \int_0^t\dot m_{cond}(\tau)d\tau } $$
10.16 Door Evaporation

If liquid water exists and air is undersaturated:

$$ \omega<\omega_s $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{evap} = \rho_{da}k_{evap} \max(0,\omega_s-\omega)V_c } $$

Latent energy consumed:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{evap} = \dot m_{evap}L_v } $$

Net phase-change energy:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{phase} = L_v(\dot m_{cond}-\dot m_{evap}) } $$
10.17 Complete Door Model

At every timestep:

$$ \boxed{ D(t) \rightarrow A_{open}(t) \rightarrow \Delta P(t) \rightarrow \dot V_d(t) \rightarrow \dot m_d(t) } $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ (T_{out},P_{out},RH_{out}) \rightarrow \omega_{out},h_{out},\rho_{out} } $$

then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_d \rightarrow S_m,\mathbf S_u,S_\omega,S_E } $$

then the coupled solver updates:

$$ \boxed{ T(x,y,z,t),P(x,y,z,t),\mathbf u(x,y,z,t),\omega(x,y,z,t) } $$

and condensation/evaporation is calculated from the updated local psychrometric state.

11. Wall Heat Transfer

The wall module determines heat transfer between:

outside environment → wall → cold-room air

This is primarily an energy boundary condition.

11.1 Wall Geometry

For each wall surface:

$$ A_w=L_wH_w $$

where:

\(L_w\): wall length
\(H_w\): wall height

Wall surface location:

$$ \mathbf x_w=(x_w,y_w,z_w) $$

Wall thickness:

$$ L_w^{th} $$
11.2 Convective Heat Transfer

Outside convection:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{out} = h_oA_w(T_{out}-T_{s,o}) } $$

Inside convection:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{in} = h_iA_w(T_{s,i}-T_{in}) } $$

where:

\(h_o\): outside convective coefficient
\(h_i\): inside convective coefficient
\(T_{s,o}\): outside wall-surface temperature
\(T_{s,i}\): inside wall-surface temperature
11.3 Wall Conduction

Fourier's law:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{cond} = -k_wA_w \frac{dT}{dx} } $$

For a plane wall:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{cond} = \frac{k_wA_w}{L_w^{th}} (T_{s,o}-T_{s,i}) } $$
11.4 Thermal Resistance of Wall

Outside convection resistance:

$$ \boxed{ R_o=\frac{1}{h_oA_w} } $$

Wall conduction resistance:

$$ \boxed{ R_{wall} = \frac{L_w^{th}} {k_wA_w} } $$

Inside convection resistance:

$$ \boxed{ R_i=\frac{1}{h_iA_w} } $$

Total resistance:

$$ \boxed{ R_{total} = \frac{1}{h_oA_w} + \frac{L_w^{th}}{k_wA_w} + \frac{1}{h_iA_w} } $$
11.5 Overall Heat-Transfer Coefficient
$$ \boxed{ U = \frac{1}{R_{total}A_w} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ U= \frac{1} { 1/h_o+ L_w^{th}/k_w+ 1/h_i } } $$
11.6 Wall Heat Transfer Rate
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{wall} = UA_w(T_{out}-T_{in}) } $$

For a cold room:

$$ T_{out}>T_{in} $$

means heat enters the room:

$$ \dot Q_{wall}>0 $$
11.7 Multilayer Wall

For multiple layers:

$$ \boxed{ R_{total} = \frac1{h_oA} + \sum_{j=1}^{N} \frac{L_j}{k_jA} + \frac1{h_iA} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ U= \frac{1}{ 1/h_o+ \sum_jL_j/k_j+ 1/h_i } } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{wall}=UA(T_{out}-T_{in}) } $$
11.8 Thermal Contact Resistance

If two wall materials have contact resistance:

$$ \boxed{ R_c=\frac{1}{h_cA} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ R_{total} = \frac1{h_oA} + \sum_j\frac{L_j}{k_jA} + \sum R_c + \frac1{h_iA} } $$
11.9 Wall Heat Flux

Heat flux:

$$ \boxed{ q''_{wall} = \frac{\dot Q_{wall}}{A_w} } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ q''_{wall} = U(T_{out}-T_{in}) } $$
11.10 Transient Wall Conduction

If wall temperature itself is modeled:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_wc_{p,w} \frac{\partial T_w}{\partial t} = \nabla\cdot(k_w\nabla T_w) } $$

For 3-D:

$$ \boxed{ \rho_wc_{p,w}\frac{\partial T_w}{\partial t} = k_w \left( \frac{\partial^2T_w}{\partial x^2} + \frac{\partial^2T_w}{\partial y^2} + \frac{\partial^2T_w}{\partial z^2} \right) } $$

Thermal diffusivity:

$$ \boxed{ \alpha_w= \frac{k_w}{\rho_wc_{p,w}} } $$
11.11 Wall Boundary Conditions

Outside:

$$ \boxed{ -k_w\frac{\partial T_w}{\partial n} = h_o(T_{out}-T_{s,o}) } $$

Inside:

$$ \boxed{ -k_w\frac{\partial T_w}{\partial n} = h_i(T_{s,i}-T_{in}) } $$

For a coupled numerical model, the wall therefore interacts directly with the room temperature field.

11.12 Solar Heat Gain

If solar radiation is considered:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{solar} = \alpha_sI_sA_w } $$

where:

\(\alpha_s\): solar absorptivity
\(I_s\): solar irradiance [W/m²]

Effective outside temperature can also be represented using a sol-air temperature:

$$ \boxed{ T_{sol-air} = T_{out} + \frac{\alpha_sI_s}{h_o} } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{wall} = UA(T_{sol-air}-T_{in}) } $$
11.13 Radiation Heat Transfer

Outside long-wave radiation:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{rad,o} = \epsilon\sigma A (T_{sur,K}^4-T_{s,o,K}^4) } $$

Inside:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{rad,i} = \epsilon\sigma A (T_{s,i,K}^4-T_{room,K}^4) } $$

where:

$$ \sigma=5.670374419\times10^{-8} \;W/(m^2K^4) $$

If radiation is included in the model, the total wall heat transfer can be represented as:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{wall} = \dot Q_{conv} + \dot Q_{rad} } $$
11.14 Floor and Ceiling

The same formulation applies to floor and ceiling:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_f=U_fA_f(T_{ground}-T_{room}) } $$ $$ \boxed{ \dot Q_c=U_cA_c(T_{outside}-T_{room}) } $$

Total envelope heat load:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{envelope} = \sum_j\dot Q_{wall,j} + \dot Q_{floor} + \dot Q_{ceiling} } $$
11.15 Spatial Wall Heat Source

For a volumetric numerical solver:

$$ \boxed{ S_{Q,wall} = \frac{\dot Q_{wall}}{V_{source}} } $$

If distributed spatially:

$$ \boxed{ S_{Q,wall}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot Q_{wall}(t)G_{wall}(\mathbf x) } $$
12. Internal Heat Sources

Internal heat sources represent heat generated inside the cold-storage room.

Examples:

stored products
workers
lighting
motors
fans
compressors located inside
forklifts
evaporator equipment
electrical equipment
product respiration
other machinery
12.1 Generic Internal Heat Source

For source \(i\):

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_i(t) } $$

Total:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{internal}(t) = \sum_{i=1}^{N}\dot Q_i(t) } $$
12.2 Volumetric Heat Source

For a source occupying volume \(V_i\):

$$ \boxed{ S_{Q,i} = \frac{\dot Q_i}{V_i} } $$

Units:

$$ [S_Q]=W/m^3 $$

The energy equation receives:

$$ \boxed{ +S_Q } $$
12.3 Spatial Heat Distribution

For a point-like source:

$$ \boxed{ S_{Q,i}(\mathbf x,t) = \dot Q_i(t)G_i(\mathbf x) } $$

with:

$$ \boxed{ G_i(\mathbf x) = \frac{1} {(2\pi\sigma_i^2)^{3/2}} \exp \left[ -\frac{|\mathbf x-\mathbf x_i|^2} {2\sigma_i^2} \right] } $$

and:

$$ \int_\Omega G_i\,dV\approx1 $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ \int_\Omega S_{Q,i}dV = \dot Q_i } $$

This is particularly useful for your 3-D digital twin.

12.4 Heat Source at a Specific Location

For:

$$ \mathbf x_i=(x_i,y_i,z_i) $$

the distance from the source is:

$$ \boxed{ r_i= \sqrt{ (x-x_i)^2+ (y-y_i)^2+ (z-z_i)^2 } } $$

Then:

$$ \boxed{ S_{Q,i}(x,y,z,t) = \frac{\dot Q_i(t)} {(2\pi\sigma_i^2)^{3/2}} e^{-r_i^2/(2\sigma_i^2)} } $$
12.5 Sensible Heat from People

For \(N_p\) occupants:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{people} = N_pq_{person} } $$

where \(q_{person}\) is the heat generation per person.

Separate sensible and latent heat:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{people,total} = \dot Q_{people,s} + \dot Q_{people,l} } $$

Moisture generation:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,people} = N_p\dot m_{v,person} } $$

Thus people can simultaneously provide:

$$ S_Q $$

and:

$$ S_\omega $$
12.6 Lighting Heat

If electrical lighting power is \(P_L\):

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{lighting} = f_LP_L } $$

where \(f_L\) is the fraction converted into room heat.

Often:

$$ f_L\approx1 $$

for a simplified model.

Volumetric source:

$$ \boxed{ S_{Q,L} = \frac{\dot Q_{lighting}}{V_{light}} } $$
12.7 Motor / Fan Heat

For motor power:

$$ P_m $$

the heat transferred into the room can be represented as:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{motor} = \eta_hP_m } $$

where \(\eta_h\) is the fraction appearing as room heat.

If essentially all motor losses become heat:

$$ \dot Q_{motor}\approx P_{loss} $$
12.8 Product Heat Load

For incoming product:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{product} = \dot m_{product}c_{p,product} (T_{product,in}-T_{product,room}) } $$

If product temperature changes transiently:

$$ \boxed{ m_pc_p \frac{dT_p}{dt} = -\dot Q_{product} } $$

For cooling:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{product} = \dot m_pc_p(T_{product}-T_{room}) } $$
12.9 Product Latent Heat

If product contains moisture:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{latent,product} = \dot m_{water}L_v } $$

Total product load:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{product,total} = \dot Q_{product,sensible} + \dot Q_{product,latent} } $$
12.10 Respiration Heat

For fresh produce:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_{resp} = m_{product}q_{resp}(T,t) } $$

where \(q_{resp}\) is respiration heat generation per unit product mass.

Volumetric source:

$$ \boxed{ S_{Q,resp} = \frac{\dot Q_{resp}}{V_{product}} } $$

Respiration may also generate moisture:

$$ \boxed{ \dot m_{v,resp} = m_{product}r_{resp}(T,t) } $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{ S_{\omega,resp} = \frac{\dot m_{v,resp}} {V_{source}} } $$
12.11 Internal Heat Source With Time Dependence

Any source can vary with time:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_i=\dot Q_i(x_i,y_i,z_i,t) } $$

For an operating schedule:

$$ \dot Q_i(t)= \begin{cases} Q_{i,on},&t_{on}\le t<t_{off}\\ 0,&\text{otherwise} \end{cases} $$

For periodic operation:

$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_i(t) = Q_{avg} + Q_{amp}\sin(\omega_ft+\phi) } $$
12.12 Internal Heat Source + Moisture Source

A source can simultaneously produce heat and moisture.

Define:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf S_i= [S_{m,i},S_{u,i},S_{\omega,i},S_{Q,i}] } $$

For example, a worker:

$$ S_{Q,worker} = \frac{\dot Q_{worker}} {V_{source}} $$

and:

$$ S_{\omega,worker} = \frac{\dot m_{v,worker}} {V_{source}} $$
12.13 Internal Heat Source and Airflow

If the source heats air, buoyancy changes:

$$ T\uparrow $$

therefore density changes:

$$ \rho=\rho(T,P,\omega) $$

which changes:

$$ \nabla P $$

and therefore:

$$ \mathbf u $$

So:

$$ \boxed{ S_Q \rightarrow T \rightarrow \rho \rightarrow P,\mathbf u \rightarrow \text{heat/moisture transport} } $$

This feedback is important for the digital twin.

13. Combined Formulation

These three modules ultimately feed the coupled solver.

Door
$$ \boxed{ D(t) \rightarrow \Delta P \rightarrow \dot V \rightarrow \dot m \rightarrow S_m,S_u,S_\omega,S_E } $$
Wall
$$ \boxed{ T_{out},T_{room},A,U \rightarrow \dot Q_{wall} \rightarrow S_{Q,wall} } $$
Internal sources
$$ \boxed{ \dot Q_i,\dot m_{v,i} \rightarrow S_{Q,i},S_{\omega,i} } $$

Therefore the governing equations become:

Mass
$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u) = S_m } $$
Momentum
$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho\mathbf u)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u\otimes\mathbf u) = -\nabla P + \nabla\cdot\boldsymbol\tau + \rho\mathbf g + \mathbf S_u } $$
Moisture
$$ \boxed{ \frac{\partial(\rho_{da}\omega)}{\partial t} + \nabla\cdot(\rho_{da}\mathbf u\omega) = \nabla\cdot (\rho_{da}D_{eff}\nabla\omega) + S_\omega + S_{evap} - S_{cond} } $$
Energy
$$ \boxed{ \rho c_p \frac{DT}{Dt} = \nabla\cdot(k\nabla T) + S_{Q,door} + S_{Q,wall} + S_{Q,internal} + L_v(S_{cond}-S_{evap}) } $$

where:

$$ \boxed{ S_{Q,internal} = \sum_iS_{Q,i} } $$

and:

$$ \boxed{ S_{Q,wall} = \frac{ \sum_jU_jA_j(T_{out,j}-T_{room,j}) } {V_{source}} } $$
14. Complete Time-Dependent State

At every spatial location and timestep, the digital twin should ultimately maintain:

$$ \boxed{ \mathbf Q(x,y,z,t) = [ T,P,u,v,w,\omega ] } $$

and derive:

$$ \boxed{ [ RH,p_v,p_{ws},\omega_s,T_{dp},T_{wb}, h,v,\rho,c_p, Q_s,Q_l,Q_{total}, \dot m_{cond},\dot m_{evap} ] } $$

while the event/source modules provide:

$$ \boxed{ [ \dot V_{door}, \dot m_{door}, \dot Q_{door}, \dot m_{v,door}, \dot Q_{wall}, \dot Q_{internal}, S_m,S_u,S_\omega,S_Q ] } $$

This gives the required spatially and temporally varying numerical digital twin:

$$ \boxed{ \phi=\phi(x,y,z,t) } $$

for temperature, pressure, velocity, humidity, heat, condensation and other derived psychrometric quantities.

Important implementation point: for your eventual solver, I would treat door opening as a transient boundary/source event, wall heat transfer as a boundary flux, and internal loads as volumetric source terms. That distinction will make the FVM formulation much cleaner and conserve mass/energy correctly.
